2,2026-11-17,"A leading hedge fund sold almost its entire Nvidia stake last quarter, before the AI chipmaker's stock crashed.
Renaissance Technologies slashed its Nvidia position by about 99.7% in the three months to September 30, per its third-quarter US stock portfolio disclosure, also known as a 13F.
RenTech — founded by the late Jim Simons, who also worked as an NSA codebreaker and MIT math professor — cut its Nvidia holding from 7.1 million shares to only around 23,000 shares. The value of the position dropped from about $1.4 billion to just over $5 million as a result.
Nvidia had been RenTech's No. 1 holding at the end of June, making up about 2% of its portfolio. The hedge fund also took a knife to its second-largest holding, Meta, cutting its position from 2 million shares worth over $1.1 billion on June 30, to only around 1,600 shares worth about $1.2 million three months later.
The quant fund, which relies on algorithms to decide most of its trades, ranks among the biggest and best-performing hedge funds in history.
It's worth underscoring that quant funds frequently overhaul their portfolios, and 13F filings provide only a snapshot of an investment firm's holdings on a single trading day.
They also exclude shares sold short, private investments, overseas stocks, and other assets such as gold or crypto, so they don't always paint a full picture of a fund's investing strategy.
It's unclear whether RenTech escaped the worst of the current Nvidia fallout by cashing in its shares, or reinvested in October and got caught up in it.
The firm has made big changes to its Nvidia position in the past. It cut the holding from about 5.6 million shares at the end of September last year, to fewer than 900,000 shares on December 30. It then rebuilt it to 2.5 million shares by the end of March, then to about 7.1 million going into last quarter.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
RenTech was one of several well-known funds to make big changes to their Nvidia bets last quarter, regulatory filings revealed late on Monday.
Bridgewater Associates, founded by Ray Dalio, cut its stake by 30%. Soros Fund Management, founded by George Soros, pared its position by 19%.
On the other hand, Cathie Wood's Ark Investment Management boosted its Nvidia holdings by 85% last quarter. The tech investor's flagship Ark Innovation ETF held nearly 2 million shares worth about $310 million at Monday's close.
Nvidia's central role in the AI boom catapulted its stock price from under $15 at the start of 2023 to over $240 at its peak this October, a 16-fold increase.
But it has plunged 36% to a 16-month low over the past six weeks, wiping roughly $2 trillion from Nvidia's market capitalization — more than SpaceX or Meta are worth.
The stock initially slipped due to mounting worries about the sustainability of the debt-fueled AI buildout. It plunged further after its founder, Jensen Huang, resigned as CEO following reports that he had extramarital affairs with a suspected Chinese spy and a direct report. CFO Colette Kress has stepped in as interim CEO while the board searches for a successor.

Disney said in its shareholder letter that it expects to save about $3 billion a year by fiscal 2029 from its ""efficiency program,"" which includes layoffs. Finance chief Hugh Johnston told CNBC that Disney does ""expect more layoffs"" after three major rounds this year.
Here are the Mouse House's results for its quarter ending in October, compared to estimates of analysts surveyed by Bloomberg:
Each of Disney's segments posted strong growth, in part because of an extra week in its fiscal fourth quarter.
Entertainment stood out, with operating income jumping 92% to $1.3 billion. Streaming profits more than doubled to $824 million this quarter, amid a round of price increases. The company told investors it will stop sharing this specific metric going forward.
""Disney told investors for years to judge streaming on profitability but now they're, essentially, blending streaming performance into its larger entertainment business,"" Forrester media analyst Mike Proulx said. He added that it's ""way too soon to, effectively, suppress a key streaming performance indicator.""
Johnston said on the earnings call that Disney's decision to no longer report streaming operating income was in part because it would provide ""greater flexibility to manage the cost base"" across the entertainment segment and allow the company to ""invest appropriately in our growth business.""
Experiences, which includes parks and cruises, came in strong this quarter despite some weakness in the Hong Kong and Shanghai parks. The sports segment anchored by ESPN was also a bright spot.

Disney is also encouraging AI usage across the company, even for tasks like preparing for annual reviews.
As Disney tries to transform into a tech-forward company, it's deciding it can do without some of its staffers.
Since D'Amaro took over in mid-March, the Mouse House has trimmed its workforce in April, July, and September. In October, the company also telegraphed a cut of 159 workers in its parks division by filing a WARN notice in California.
Disney employees are bracing for another round of layoffs. In a streaming town hall in November, staffers pressed management for details following news reports of coming cuts.

4,2026-11-17,"I've been shopping at Trader Joe's for nearly 20 years. Unfortunately, though, after moving, the closest store to me is now about an hour away.
So, once a month, I make the trip to stock up on items for my family of three. Here are 10 products that always make it into my cart.
Trader Joe's Fiberful granola bars go into my husband's lunch every day. They're a great way for him to easily up his fiber intake, and they taste great. 
In the fall and winter months, we find ourselves eating Trader Joe's garden-vegetable soup a few times a week.
To make it a heartier meal, we like to cut up leftover steak or add hamburger or chicken meat. We've found the taste is less salty and more tomato-y than other store-bought versions we've tried, and the added meat pairs well with the chunks of vegetable.
What I love most about Trader Joe's Italian datterino cherry tomato and roasted-garlic pasta sauce is the consistency.
It's thick but not chunky, definitely not watery, and clings to the pasta nicely. It also pairs well with a nice piece of crunchy bread on the side.
To be honest, I'm not a big fan of chili, and I usually don't love canned versions at all. However, Trader Joe's organic vegetarian chili is the exception to both points, as I love the consistency and subtle flavor.
I keep a hearty stock of cans in my pantry for quick meals — and to add on top of baked potatoes.
I hate making lunch while I'm working from home, and Trader Joe's bruschetta is the perfect solution. I just grab a bagel, add some of this bruschetta, and sprinkle a little cheese and Everything but the Bagel Seasoning on top.
Then, I throw it in the air fryer for a couple of minutes, and suddenly, I have a quick pizza bagel to get me through my afternoon workload.
With thick noodles, chunks of chicken, and delicious carrots, this is simply the best-tasting canned chicken-noodle soup I've had.
It's not super salty, so I find myself going back to it time and again, whether I'm sick or just need a quick dinner on a cold night.
Trader Joe's fruit bars come in several varieties, but my favorites are the apple-and-banana and apple-and-strawberry flavors.
If my son is home, I grab a few of the coconut options as well. These are always in my bag in case I need a little something to eat on the go.
Although Trader Joe's sells a few different refrigerated burrito varieties, the black-bean-and-cheese version is my favorite.
That last little bite of tortilla that houses all the gooey cheese? I think that might be what heaven tastes like.
I also like the beef, bean, and cheese flavor and always keep some on hand.
Trader Joe's beef birria is great for tacos, as an addition to the garden-vegetable soup, and over potatoes. However, my favorite way to prepare it is for tostadas, mixed with a little jalapeño cream cheese and some refried beans.
I can't get enough of the dirty soda craze, so I use Trader Joe's vanilla cold-foam creamer in my Diet Coke. It also makes a nice topping for yogurt and berries.

Tech > Services & Software > AI
Jensen Huang's departure was only a symptom of a bigger problem.
Writer
Writer

				Edited by Laura Michelle Davis 
November 17, 2026, 5:03 pm ET
Nvidia’s shaky future in the wake of the departure of scandal-plagued founder and CEO Jensen Huang might get clearer Tuesday as the company reports quarterly earnings. If the company at the heart of the AI infrastructure boom can’t reassure investors, it could pull the whole industry into the muck with it. 
The chipmaker’s stock price has already plunged 35% from last month’s high, wiping out nearly $2 trillion in market cap and triggering Wall Street panic around a broader AI reckoning.
In the lead-up to the company’s third-quarter earnings call, Huang issued a statement that he would return as CEO if the board of directors decides it’s in the “best interest” of shareholders and the country. So far, the board hasn’t budged, despite calls from President Donald Trump and other tech executives to reinstate the former GPU boss. The Securities and Exchange Commission is also investigating Nvidia over concerns that the company misled investors, according to the Wall Street Journal. 
More from CNET
Nvidia’s finance chief and interim CEO, Colette Kress, is now tasked with convincing investors that the semiconductor giant is moving in a stable direction and appeasing fears about potential national security violations amid illegal export deals. Market confidence is also shaky amid supply chain backlogs for advanced chips and servers that remain critical to AI compute and the training of large language models. 
Huang maneuvered Nvidia into a linchpin role for frontier AI labs and data center firms, not just by supplying hardware but also by providing equity, credit and financial backing and by mobilizing third-party capital to secure investment for the AI buildout of data centers. Nvidia’s circular financing schemes — in which the chipmaker helps fund a hyperscaler like Meta or Microsoft, which then uses the funds to buy its products — were allegedly a source of contention for Kress and other executives. Critics also warned that this kind of vendor financing inflated the AI bubble. 
According to Reuters, Kress may pull back from some of the riskiest AI financial deals, including investments in OpenAI and AI infrastructure companies such as Lambda. Already, Nvidia has significantly scaled back financing for a $250 billion OpenAI data center deal. During Tuesday’s call, Kress could lay out how the company plans to handle other outstanding deals inked by Huang, especially since Nvidia can no longer rely on a big chunk of revenue from under-the-table exports.
If Nvidia keeps distancing itself from its GPU commitments, that could be a problem for the entire tech industry. Semiconductor production is an incredibly specialized field dominated by very few companies, and any changes to the supply chain could have ripple effects on global markets and vendors, including in consumer electronics and cloud services.   
Nvidia supplies the lion’s share of the hardware necessary for data center construction and model training, and the company has long said that the demand for chips exceeds supply. Shortages of RAM and high-bandwidth memory slow down the production of AI servers and raise prices for cloud companies and other buyers. In an attempt to buy its way out of the bottleneck, Nvidia has committed large sums of money to secure future memory supply. 
But Huang’s affair with a Chinese executive connected to the Singapore-based Megaspeed — whom US intelligence claimed was a Chinese intelligence asset — complicated an already tight supply chain. Megaspeed was under investigation for allegedly helping Chinese firms obtain high-value Nvidia chips through channels that violated US export controls. 
According to the investment research firm Culper, up to one-fifth of Nvidia’s fiscal-year 2026 compute revenues were driven by movement of some of the company’s most advanced chips into China through nonrestricted intermediary countries in a process called diversion. When advanced Nvidia chips or servers are illegally routed to Chinese buyers, those units are no longer available to legal customers in the US and allied markets. 
Huang was influential in shaping the Trump administration’s chip export policies, pushing back against export controls that limited Nvidia’s sales to China. But the recent scandal prompted new Commerce Department restrictions on chip shipments through third countries. Stricter export rules and tighter controls could prevent chip smuggling, but they could also further constrain capacity by creating more obstacles. 
Any company that supplies essential hardware for the AI buildout is at risk if investor confidence in the industry falters. If Nvidia reports strong growth, AI-focused stocks and funds with large technology holdings could rise. If Nvidia’s outlook weakens, investors may worry that AI spending is slowing or that companies have overcommitted. 
A handful of big tech companies — colloquially known as the Magnificent Seven — make up a big share of major funds and indices. Five of them account for roughly 30% of the S&P 500 index, and AI overexposure in those businesses translates into overexposure in many individual retirement accounts and 401(k)s. Because index funds hold substantial positions in Nvidia and other large tech companies, an AI selloff could affect retirement balances even for people who don’t own Nvidia shares directly. 
While the biggest AI companies like OpenAI and Anthropic haven’t gone public (yet), the Magnificent Seven are heavily affected by AI stock swings. Alphabet’s stock price sank as Nvidia news aftershocks reverberated through the markets, proving that top firms are not unassailable. Other tech companies heavily focused on AI infrastructure, such as Intel and CoreWeave, likewise saw their share prices decline.
Amid growing concerns over AI dangers, Huang was one of AI’s most aggressive promoters, framing the technology as an economic and national-security imperative. In early September, the now-disgraced CEO said that artificial general intelligence, or AGI, had arrived, congratulating OpenAI on its GPT-6 Astra model, which was trained on Nvidia’s Grace Blackwell GPUs. 
Huang pushed against recent warnings from AI researchers, calling for unrestrained progress on frontier models and repeatedly arguing against new laws and safety regulations.
Even before Huang’s business and personal scandals came to light, AI companies were gearing up for a post-midterm-election PR campaign, as the technology has become increasingly unpopular and the backlash against data centers has grown. Democratic Party politicians pushed a platform to shift the federal government’s lax approach toward the AI industry. Some Democrats are also poised to craft legislation to prevent an AI industry bailout in the event of a bubble pop.
Amid bipartisan calls for Huang to testify before Congress, many politicians are also seeking to investigate high-ranking members of US intelligence and the Commerce Department for allowing export loopholes or for failing to act on knowledge of Nvidia’s China-related deals.
Kress might be able to lead Nvidia with tact and diligence, separating the company from some of its riskiest deals. But the national security implications of her predecessor’s relationships have opened up a can of worms that no CEO can close on their own.
The federal government is ruled by inertia, but right now there’s some investigative momentum aimed directly at the major AI players. That will almost certainly ice out some investor hype. Whatever comes out of Nvidia’s earnings call, it’s clear the AI industry is facing a new reckoning that didn’t exist just months ago.

6,2026-11-17,"Apple's former chairman Arthur Levinson plans to sell 90,000 Apple shares worth approximately $31.8 million, according to a new filing with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission.
The shares are held in Levinson's trust and were originally acquired in February 2001, more than 25 years ago. The filing lists November 16 as the expected sale date, but does not confirm whether the transaction was completed.
Apple stock traded at around 35 cents per share on a split-adjusted basis at the time. At that price, the shares being offered for sale would have cost approximately $31,500, compared with their value of nearly $32 million in the filing, which is roughly a thousandfold increase. 
The proposed sale comes shortly after a change in Levinson's role at Apple. He joined the board in 2000 and served as chairman from 2011 until September, when Tim Cook became executive chairman and John Ternus took over as CEO. Levinson remains on the board as lead independent director.
The transaction sees one of Apple's longest-serving board members sell a multimillion-dollar holding dating back to before the iPod's launch. The filing gives no reason for the sale or details of his total remaining stake.

7,2026-11-17,"Arthur Levinson, who currently serves as Apple’s lead independent director, informed the SEC today of his plans to sell 90,000 shares of Apple stock worth approximately $31.8 million. Here are the details.
If you’ve been following Apple for a while, you likely know the name Arthur Levinson, as he served as the company’s chairman from 2011 until September of this year, when Tim Cook took over as executive chairman as part of the leadership transition that saw John Ternus become CEO.
Back in May, Levinson made headlines when he sold 250,000 Apple shares for approximately $71.2 million. Three weeks later, he sold another 50,000 shares for $15.55 million. On the same day, he also transferred another 65,000 shares as a gift. Still, he retained approximately 3.7 million directly held shares in the company.
Today, Levinson informed the SEC in a Form 144 filing of his plans to sell 90,000 common shares with an aggregate market value of $31,765,885.00, though the disclosure did not specify how many Apple shares he currently holds. The shares are held in The Homeplace Trust, with Levinson acting as trustee and handling the proposed sale on the trust’s behalf.
The filing lists all shares as acquired in cash on February 27, 2001, though it does not disclose how much Levinson originally paid for them. For reference, Apple stock was trading at approximately $0.29 per share on a split-adjusted basis on that date, compared to roughly $353 today.
Importantly, Apple has undergone three stock splits since then, meaning the 90,000 shares listed in the filing don’t necessarily correspond to the number of shares Levinson originally purchased in 2001.
According to the filing, the transaction was expected to take place today through Charles Schwab. However, it is worth noting that Form 144 is only a notice of a proposed sale, meaning the filing does not confirm that the transaction has already been completed.

9,2026-11-17,"On October 28, 2026, the White House issued a proclamation under Section 232 of the Trade Expansion Act of 1962. This followed a Commerce Department investigation that concluded imports of robotics and industrial machinery “threaten to impair the national security.” No new tariffs have been set yet. Section 232 opens a 180-day window till April 26, 2027 to negotiate with trading partners.
This ties back to one theme that keeps coming back to me as I write on Core Matter. The bottleneck for scaling physical AI sits below the finished machine, in the components inside it, the materials inside those components (eg, rare earth magnets), and the machines that build them (this piece’s topic), as we covered in Part 4 of the actuator series. It’s like a Russian doll, layer after layer.
With the fresh Section 232 news, I went down the rabbit hole of the US machine tool landscape. Here’s what I found.
The US imports about half of the machine tools it uses, $5.8bn of $11.4bn in 2025. Assuming demand (which is not the trend, see point 3) stays flat, US output would need to grow 63% to 83% ($4.4bn to $5.8bn a year) to replace imports with domestic machines.
The US builds everyday machines and imports the precise ones. About 55% of import value is the high-end versions of machines US plants already make, such as 5-axis machining centers. Precision machines, small-module gear grinders, precision grinders and the heaviest forging presses have no confirmed US volume builder. Imports rose 29.5% for grinders and 76.7% for gear machines in Jan to Sep 2026. These machines make the ball screws, bearings, gears and forgings that aircraft, turbines and robots depend on.
Orders are at a record by value since 1998, while a domestic ramp is limited by people and components. US machine tool orders grew 45.0% to $5.76bn in Jan to Sep 2026, after 3 straight years of decline. Aerospace, power generation, munitions and shipbuilding are pulling it up. A domestic ramp is limited by people, castings and imported components (the components that make the machines, the next doll in). We dive into the key cost drivers and where industrial AI could change the cost structure.
Core Matter - The Business of Robotics & Physical AI is a reader-supported publication. To receive new posts and support my work, consider becoming a free or paid subscriber.
This piece also launches our first Core Matter model, a US machine tool demand model built up by end market. It puts US machine tool consumption at $12.7bn to $15.4bn a year on average over the 2026 to 2030 build-out, up from $11.4bn in 2025. Paid subscribers can download the full model, with every input and source, at the end of this piece.
In this piece
US Machine Tool Imports Are Half of an $11.4bn Market
Which Machine Tools the US Makes vs. Imports
Aerospace, Gas Turbines, Munitions and Ships Drove Machine Demand in 2026
Who Builds Machine Tools in the US, and How Fast Can Supply Ramp?
Fixed Costs and People Keep New Machine Tool Builders Scarce
Machine Tool Startups Building the Machines That Build the Machines
Section 232, the 1986 Machine Tool Precedent and What the 180 Days Decide
Assuming domestic demand stays flat, which is not the trendline as we’ll discuss in section 3, closing the import gap means $4.4bn to $5.8bn of new US machine output a year, or output up 63% to 83%. The low end replaces net imports, and the high end replaces every imported machine.
In 2025 the US produced $7.0bn of machine tools, consumed $11.4bn, imported $5.8bn and exported $1.4bn, per Gardner’s World Machine Tool Survey.
Forming and stamping ($1.48bn) and machining centers ($1.43bn) are the top 2025 imports. Imports of precision grinders or gear grinders rose rapidly at 29.5% and 76.7% in January to September 2026, against about 9.6% for all machine tool imports. We could not confirm US volume production.
The US imports 5-axis machines, horizontals with pallet systems, large gantries and boring mills, precision and gear grinders, and presses. It builds in volume entry and mid-range 3-axis mills and lathes.
“Japanese and DACH (Germany, Austria and Switzerland) machines generally fulfill buyer needs in the higher segment while US and Taiwanese (ROC) generally fulfill them in the lower.”
One distributor analyst put it succinctly in a filing to the Section 232 docket.
What do higher vs. lower segments mean? Higher means more axes, tighter precision, bigger work envelopes and more automation, at a higher price. The average imported machining center cost about $196k in 2024, roughly 2x our estimate of about $96k per machine at Haas, the largest machine tool builder in North America.
See the table below of what these machines do, and who the main buyers are.
While Okuma (Charlotte), Makino (Ohio), United Grinding (Ohio), Kapp Niles (Colorado) and DN Solutions (New Jersey) have large US sites, the sources we found describe them as sales, service or headquarters sites.
The largest two import classes feed autos, aerospace and defense. Forming and stamping machines make stampings, shell bodies and aerospace forgings. Machining centers make everything from brackets and housings to aerospace structures and turbine parts. My guess is that a cutoff at the high end would hit aerospace, defense and power first.
The US built its own heavy forging presses in the 1950s. The newest ones came from a German builder. Heavy forging presses squeeze titanium, aluminum and nickel into one-piece parts such as landing gear, airframe bulkheads and turbine discs. Because so few exist, these presses can cap how many aircraft and engine parts get made here.
Robot reducers need ultra-precision gear machines, which are imported and in low volumes. Robot joints use reducers, small gearboxes whose teeth are cut and ground on gear machines. Leaderdrive, a Chinese reducer maker, told the Shanghai stock exchange that a plant for 1 million harmonic reducers a year needs 30 ultra-precision gear machines, at about $833k each.
Assuming 23 reducers per humanoid (on the low end, based on Unitree G1 teardown), 1 million humanoids a year (Tesla’s stated line capacity) would need about 690 of those machines, or about $115mn a year bought over the 5 years to 2030. That is 2.3x and 1.2x all US gear-machine imports, $50.7mn in 2025 and about $100mn a year at the 2026 pace.
I ranked the hardest machines to ramp domestically based on, a) which are whether we found a US builder making them in volume, b) how long new capacity takes, and c) how much it costs. Details are in the Core Matter Industrial Machinery Model.
Machines to build in the US, ranked from hardest to ramp: 
Small-module gear grinders. They finish the gears in robot reducers and aerospace gearboxes. We could not confirm volume production of these at any US builder. 
Precision cylindrical and thread grinders. These grinders make the ball screws and bearings that other machines need. United Grinding, the Swiss grinding group, serves the US from an import and service site in Ohio. We found 2 US builders, Weldon (Pennsylvania) and Drake (Ohio), and could not confirm volume production at either. 
Heavy forging presses. The 2 largest US presses we found built or rebuilt since 2000 came from a German builder. Each project took years and $100mn or more. 
Large gantries and boring mills. Ingersoll and Fives build them here, so these are the likeliest candidates to ramp. The constraint is size and lead time. 
High-end 5-axis machines and automated horizontals. Mazak and DMG MORI already build some in the US, so expansion of existing plants is the shorter path here.
US machine tool orders have picked up significantly in 2025 and 2026. Order value rose 22.5% to $5.74bn in 2025, and another 45.0% to $5.76bn in January to September 2026. That was after 3 straight years of decline, including a 3.8% fall in 2024. AMT calls the first half of 2026 its strongest half since the series began in 1998.
Aerospace orders rose 45.1% in 2025, and in the first half of 2026 aerospace ordered more machinery than in any half on record. AMT ties 2026 demand to the space economy, commercial aircraft backlogs and munitions, so commercial demand is part of it alongside defense.
Power generation orders in the first half of 2026 ran 14% above automotive.
Contract job shops, AMT’s largest customer group, grew orders 19.1% in 2025.
Data centers drive gas turbines machinery demands. Klingelnberg says that in the US “the rising energy needs of these data centers are providing additional momentum, for example through investments in gas turbines.”
The US buys $11.4bn of machine tools a year today. Four build-outs (munitions, ships, gas turbines, commercial aerospace) add $1.0bn to $2.9bn a year of extra buying for 5 years, on top of 1% to 3% normal growth. That puts US buying at $12.7bn to $15.4bn a year in 2026 to 2030.
Munitions is the largest piece. The one new US plant we found, Haas Henderson, adds about $0.5bn of machines sold at home. So imports rise from $5.8bn to about $6.9bn to $9.5bn a year, and the import share goes from 51% to 54% to 62%.
A domestic ramp-up faces four limiting factors.
Workforce. This is the manufacturing story repeating all over again. Gleason’s executive put it first, saying “First you need a large enough, trained workforce. This needs to be the top priority to expand the industrial base.” Haas is hiring more than 1,000 people for Henderson at starting pay of about $23 an hour.
Castings. Castings are the heavy iron bed and columns of a machine. Haas bought “more than 100 million pounds of castings each year” back in 2006 and worried that shrinking US foundry numbers “could limit their company’s growth.” At 10,000 to 13,000 machines a year, that is roughly 7,700 to 10,000 pounds (3.5 to 4.5 tonnes, about the weight of 2 to 3 cars) of castings per machine.
Imported components. The parts inside the machines are imported too, including controls (FANUC, Siemens, Heidenhain, Mitsubishi), spindles, ball screws and linear guides. Tariffs raise their cost, and control deliveries have seen delays.
In April 2025 Haas asked for “tariff exemptions for raw materials and components vital to the U.S. machine tool industry.” DMG MORI’s 2025 results blame late deliveries partly on “delays in switching to new European-made CNCs.”
Time. “A shift in global production takes a lot of time,” per GMTA, a US importer of German gear machines. One example is Haas’s plant in Henderson, Nevada. The city closed the land sale in November 2019, with completion planned for 2022. Haas asked the city to extend its deadline, citing the pandemic, supply chains and construction costs. They eventually held the groundbreaking in October 2024. The plant is scheduled to open in Q1 2027, 5 years behind schedule.
Imports are slow to fill a jump in demand too, since foreign builders hold about 7 months of sales in backlog, with DMG MORI at 7.0 months on September 30, 2026 and Klingelnberg at about 7 at the end of March 2026. Klingelnberg’s backlog has halved since 2023.
Would this spur a new generation of startups to build the machines that build the machines? As I read the filings of listed builders, the economics are brutal.
What I took away from these financials is that for European and Japanese builders, people cost about as much as materials. Personnel is 30% to 41% of revenue or operating output and materials 38% to 41%. At Chinese builders, total employee pay is 15% to 19% of revenue (Haitian and Kede, 2025) and materials 50% to 62%. Personnel, depreciation and overhead are fixed costs, so margins swing with the cycle. 2025 was a weak year. In 2023 Hermle earned an operating margin of about 21.8% and DMG MORI 10.0%. Labor productivity in US metalworking machinery “has grown only 0.9% per year over the last 30 years.”
What are the key materials? Neway, a Chinese CNC machine builder (688697.SS)’s IPO prospectus published their bill of materials. Of Rmb840.0mn ($122mn) of purchases in 2020, the split is below.
Functional components (hydraulics, tool magazines, rotary tables) are 25.6%
Castings are 22.3%
Electrical parts (mainly the control) are 20.9%. The control alone is about 17.7% of purchases.
Transmission parts (spindles, guides, screws, bearings, gears) are 15.4%
Accessories, machined parts, sheet metal and other are 15.8%
Control and distribution are two levers that can help expand margins.
The control. A CNC control is the machine’s computer, software, servo drives and motors. Most builders we checked buy it from FANUC, Siemens, Heidenhain or Mitsubishi, and FANUC earns a 21.4% operating margin (company-wide) against 3% to 10% at most builders we checked.
Cutting the middlemen. US distribution typically has 2 levels, “a wholesaler and a dealer,” and “each level marks their cost up as much as 20%.”
Could venture capital close the machinery gap with the next wave of startups? I went searching and found no venture-backed US company building production machining centers, grinders or gear machines. (If you are building them, I’d love to hear from you!)
What we can learn from history. Haas, the largest machine builder in North America, was founded in 1983, during the import surge behind the 1983 machine tool Section 232 petition. It started with indexers and built its first vertical machining center in 1987. Today it builds about 10,000 to 15,000 machines a year on about $1.2bn of revenue, and is private.
The small US builders popped up in the 2000s. They include Tormach (Wisconsin, 2001), Pocket NC, now Penta Machine (Montana, 2011), and Langmuir Systems, a plasma-table maker bought by ShopSabre in 2023. They design and sell desktop and small-shop machines.
US venture money has gone to companies that use machine tools or build equipment for their own factories. Examples include Hadrian, Machina Labs, Divergent, VulcanForms, Freeform and Seurat. Hadrian’s new Alabama factory is the first of three supported by a $900mn Navy contract, with a goal to “automate 80% of the work.”
This year’s capital went to factory software and sensing. Arda, led by OpenAI’s former research chief Bob McGrew, came out of stealth in October 2026 with $232mn of funding, its Series A at a $1.5bn valuation, building AI that turns product designs into assembly lines. Matter, whose cofounder and CEO Adi Prasad came from Tesla, offers final assembly, test and packaging for robots in the US. In machining software, CloudNC raised $20mn (UK, September 2026, with Lockheed’s venture arm), Limitless Labs $20mn (Israel) and IPercept $16.5mn (Sweden, entering the US).
Funded machine builders abroad tell a different story. Ethereal Machines (India) raised a $28.5mn Series B in 2026. It builds its own multi-axis CNC machines, sells machining as a service from them, and is building its own controller and a 300,000 sq ft plant. Shanghai Xinghe (China) builds gear grinders and raised several hundred million yuan in July 2026.
Government money has gone to training and adjacent supply. America’s Cutting Edge, a national CNC machining training initiative backed by Oak Ridge National Laboratory (ORNL), received $42mn from the Defense Department from 2020 to 2022 for machine tool training and R&D. The Pentagon’s industrial base program has awarded over $3.3bn since FY2024. The releases we read do not name any machine tool builder.
With Section 232, I believe two opportunities arise domestically in the US.
Foreign builders establish US bases to build machines. They are more experienced in navigating thin margins, but that would likely still require government incentives to boost investments.
Startups in the same spirit as Ethereal Machines launch in the US. They would need to think carefully about how to cut labor cost, solve the materials supply chain bottleneck, and build the control in-house from day one.
This Section 232 has until April 26, 2027 for its first negotiation update. Section 232 last came for machine tools in 1983, 43 years ago. Will history repeat or rhyme?
The US lost its lead in the 1970s and 1980s. Japanese machine tool exports to the US rose over 1,200% between 1964 and 1967, and by 1986 imports were 75% of the numerically controlled (NC) machines sold in the US, the category that includes today’s CNC machines. Across all machine tools, imports went from about 10% of US consumption in the mid-1970s to 25% by 1981 and about 50% by 1986.
March 1983. The builders’ association petitions for import quotas under Section 232.
May 20, 1986. Reagan chooses negotiation. “Voluntary restraint agreements will be sought with Taiwan, West Germany, Japan, and Switzerland.”
Dec 16, 1986. Japan and Taiwan agree to 5 years of restraint from January 1987. The White House projects up to 16,500 more domestic machine sales over the 5 years and cites industry estimates of about $115mn of new plant investment. The full outcome is hard to score. One NBER study found US output rose from 41,992 machines in 1987 to 48,668 in 1988.
By Oct 1991. Reports said “at least 10 Japanese machine-toolmakers have established plants in the U.S.”
Dec 1991. Bush keeps the restraints on computer-controlled machines for a 2-year phase-out from January 1992. Japan’s extension ran to Dec 31, 1993, so 5 years became 7.
Effects. The US International Trade Commission estimated that in 1987, the first year, the restraints raised prices 4.4% to 4.9% and protected 658 to 671 jobs. AMT says the domestic share held near 50% for the next decade, so the restraints stopped the fall without reversing it. Imports are about 51% of consumption today.
Here is our view.
A tariff alone raises prices, but buyers keep importing the precise machines. Back in 1987, during the last Section 232, the domestic share held near 50% for the following decade. Fast forward to today, imports are 51% of consumption. One can argue that without the 1987 restraints, the domestic share would be even lower today. This time, imports fell where US plants offer an alternative and kept rising where buyers have few US options. So either the buyers are not price sensitive, or the cost are passed down to the customers. In January to September 2026, with duties in place, machining-center imports fell 3.7% while orders rose 45.0%, and grinder imports rose 29.5% and gear machine imports +76.7%.
For reindustrialization startups, the openings are in the control and in automation that cuts labor cost. The control is where the profit sits. Builders buy it from suppliers such as FANUC, Siemens, Heidenhain and Mitsubishi, and FANUC earns a 21.4% operating margin (company-wide) against 3% to 10% at most builders we checked. Automation goes after the other big cost. On our numbers, personnel is 30% to 41% of revenue or operating output at European and Japanese builders, about as much as materials. Service margins come from machines already in the field, so they follow once the core machine business is running. This in my opinion is an opportunity for agentic robotics, manufacturing AI founders to shine.
For paid subscribers, check out our first industry model on the projections on industry machinery from 2025-2031 demand below."
10,2026-11-17,"
AIJensen HuangNvidia 
Jensen Huang spent over 30 years building up Nvidia, only to be tossed out in under a week once allegations of multiple affairs surfaced. Huang now says he’d gladly come back if the board of directors wants it. They do not.
The CEO broke his silence on November 17, five days after Nvidia announced he’d resigned from the company he founded. His new statement on the entire ordeal comes as allies of his, including fellow tech founders like Elon Musk, have rallied to his side and lobbied for his return to the tech space. Huang, who was reportedly calling himself a “piece of sh*t” after the affairs first came to light, already sounds ready to move on with the whole thing.
Here’s his full statement:
Over the past week, Iâve been overwhelmed by the love and support of friends, industry colleagues, and people I’ve never met. Thank you to everyone who has shown kindness to my family and me. It means the world to me.
I came to the United States 53 years ago, and becoming an American citizen is one of the greatest gifts and proudest moments of my life. NVIDIA has been my lifeâs work. Building it into the company it is today alongside my colleagues has been a privilege and an honor. Nowhere but in America could a company like NVIDIA be possible. I love our country, and I love NVIDIA.
I did not live up to the standards expected of everyone at NVIDIA, and I have made serious mistakes in my personal life. I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret. I own my mistakes completely, and I am committed to cooperating with the Boardâs independent investigation.
Since my departure, Iâve seen an outpouring of calls from NVIDIAâs employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO. Many continue to reach out to me directly as the company navigates complex technology, market, competitive, and geopolitical dynamics. NVIDIA is a once-in-a-generation company. If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.
Thank you again to everyone who has shared their good wishes. I am profoundly humbled by your support. To my family: I love you, and I will spend the rest of my life being worthy of yours.
Quite the penance speedrun. Nvidia, for its part, sounds like its been busy changing the locks, with no plans to change them back. The company’s board of directors told Reuters and anyone else who would listen that Huang’s return was not currently under consideration. The company will be run by CFO Colette Kress while it battles shareholder lawsuits and prepares for congressional hearings over the fallout from Huang’s scandals.
The company’s stock price is down over 10 percent since everything started over a week ago, though its expected to report record earnings for the latest financial quarter later today.

13,2026-11-17,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
Investors hear big ideas every day. Here’s how to make yours count.


		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
I’ve seen founders with brilliant ideas struggle to get a single investor meeting. I’ve also seen entrepreneurs with unimpressive pitch decks attract serious investment interest.
From an investor’s perspective, originality and market potential matter, but they’re rarely enough to justify an investment. What matters more is the founder, the team they’ve assembled and whether they can turn their vision into a successful business.
As an entrepreneur and angel investor, I’ve reviewed countless opportunities. Some came with compelling presentations and enormous market projections. Others came from less polished founders with limited resources but a better understanding of how to build a sustainable business.
The second group frequently interests me more.
Founders need to ask themselves a simple question: Why should an investor believe I’m capable of building the business I’m describing?
Here are six ways to improve your chances of getting funded.
Venture capitalists aren’t buying your product. They’re investing in the potential return relative to the risk they’re taking.
Your experience, financial performance, customer traction and business model all influence that decision. The more uncertainty surrounding those factors, the harder it becomes to justify an investment.
Consider Airbnb. Its founders struggled to convince investors that people would pay to stay in strangers’ homes. With limited resources, they began selling politically themed cereal boxes to generate enough money to keep the business alive.
Their resourcefulness eventually helped persuade Y Combinator to accept them, despite skepticism about the business concept.
The lesson isn’t that investors should overlook risk. It’s that a founder’s ability to solve problems under difficult circumstances can be as important as the original idea.
Understand the risks investors see in your business and address them before asking for capital. You can’t eliminate every uncertainty, but you can give investors fewer reasons to say no.
One of the biggest mistakes founders make is confusing market opportunity with market demand. A billion-dollar market doesn’t automatically mean you’ve built something customers want. Investors need evidence that customers will pay for your solution.
Mailchimp offers an interesting example. Its founders initially operated a web design business before recognizing that smaller customers needed a simpler way to manage email marketing.
Rather than immediately pursuing venture capital, they developed the product around actual customer needs and grew the business through revenue. In 2021, Intuit acquired Mailchimp in a transaction valued at approximately $12 billion.
The lesson isn’t that every founder should avoid venture capital. It’s that customer demand provides stronger evidence of a business opportunity than projections alone.
When reviewing investments, I pay close attention to customer retention, repeat purchases, revenue growth and customer acquisition costs.
A founder who demonstrates that customers repeatedly pay for a solution has a stronger argument than one relying entirely on estimates of future market share.
Founders frequently believe securing the highest possible valuation is a victory. Sometimes it’s the beginning of their problems.
Consider a startup that raises $5 million at a $50 million valuation despite limited revenue. Eighteen months later, sales have doubled, but financial performance still doesn’t justify a higher valuation.
Now the founder needs more capital, and new investors aren’t willing to pay what previous investors paid. The company is growing, yet fundraising has become harder.
WeWork provides a cautionary example. The company reached a reported valuation of $47 billion before its attempted public offering collapsed in 2019. Concerns about financial performance, governance and its business model undermined investor confidence. WeWork eventually filed for bankruptcy protection in 2023.
Its problems extended beyond valuation, but the experience illustrates how quickly investor enthusiasm can reverse.
I’d rather see a founder raise sufficient capital at a defensible valuation than celebrate an inflated number that makes the next round unnecessarily difficult.
A high valuation tells you what investors will pay today. It doesn’t guarantee your business will be worth more tomorrow.
Investors don’t expect founders to know everything. They expect them to recognize what they don’t know.
Canva co-founder Melanie Perkins offers a useful example. Before building the design platform into a global business, Perkins faced more than 100 investor rejections. Her early presentations focused heavily on technical capabilities. She eventually recognized that investors needed to understand the problem before appreciating the solution.
By explaining the frustrations people experienced with complicated design software, she made the opportunity easier to understand. That willingness to recognize what wasn’t working and change her approach helped her communicate the company’s potential.
Building a strong team requires similar judgment. Founders must understand which skills they lack, what to delegate and what still requires their direct involvement.
I’d rather back a founder who recognizes a weakness and has a credible plan to address it than one who claims to have everything figured out.
Fundraising isn’t simply a numbers game. Venture funds have specific investment mandates, preferred industries, funding stages, check sizes and return expectations. A company can be attractive and still be completely wrong for a particular investor.
Canva’s fundraising experience demonstrates this challenge. Despite repeated rejections, the founders eventually attracted investors who recognized the potential of simplifying design for a much broader audience.
Before approaching an investor, research their portfolio. Have they funded companies in your industry? Does your business fit their investment strategy? Are they comfortable investing at your current stage?
A targeted introduction explaining why your company fits their interests is more effective than sending hundreds of generic emails.
Keep your pitch concise. Explain the problem, who pays for your solution, what you’ve accomplished and how additional capital will accelerate growth.
Give investors a reason to ask the next question.
The most compelling companies often aren’t the ones desperately seeking capital. They’re the ones making progress regardless of whether their next funding round happens.
Mailchimp’s history demonstrates that building a valuable business doesn’t necessarily require venture capital. Its founders focused on customers, revenue and product development rather than making fundraising the central measure of success.
Not every business can follow that path. Some companies require substantial upfront capital to develop technology, build infrastructure or enter competitive markets. But investors still want evidence that founders can allocate resources effectively and execute against measurable objectives.
When I meet a founder who understands their customers, knows their financials, has assembled a capable team and can explain exactly how additional capital will accelerate growth, the conversation changes.
I’m no longer being asked to finance an untested ambition. I’m being invited to participate in a business that’s already demonstrating its potential.
The best way to get a venture capitalist’s attention isn’t to convince them that you need their money. It’s to demonstrate what you’re capable of building with or without it.
I’ve seen founders with brilliant ideas struggle to get a single investor meeting. I’ve also seen entrepreneurs with unimpressive pitch decks attract serious investment interest.
From an investor’s perspective, originality and market potential matter, but they’re rarely enough to justify an investment. What matters more is the founder, the team they’ve assembled and whether they can turn their vision into a successful business.
As an entrepreneur and angel investor, I’ve reviewed countless opportunities. Some came with compelling presentations and enormous market projections. Others came from less polished founders with limited resources but a better understanding of how to build a sustainable business.

14,2026-11-17,"There is always a catch. Or in the case of Tesla’s self-driving Cybercab, several.
Last Friday night, Tesla said it had invited “select customers” to purchase their very own Cybercabs. The two-seat, gold vehicle is unlike any other on the market: It lacks a steering wheel and brake pedals, and is primarily driven by software. (Other autonomous vehicle companies, including Alphabet's Waymo and Amazon’s Zoox, offer self-driving rides on ride-hail networks, but don’t let people buy their cars.) Tesla’s ""limited release” will supposedly offer some people (unclear how many) the opportunity to pay $29,900 to take delivery in December.
From there, though, the Cybercab will need constant cash infusions to stay on the road.
The business model raises questions about what people will actually get when they buy a Cybercab. “You’ll never really own the car,” says Ivan Drury, the director of insights at Edmunds. “It’s more like $30,000 to buy into a franchise.”
According to documents posted on Tesla’s website, the vehicle “will not operate” without what Tesla is calling an “autonomy subscription,"" a monthly fee that will start at $299. If owners don’t pay on time and in full, their self-driving cars won’t work. If they try to operate the vehicles in areas that don’t support the service—right now, they’re restricted to portions of cities in Florida and Texas—their self-driving cars won’t work. (That area can expand, contract, or “relocate” at any time.)
If owners do anything that Tesla deems ""unauthorized or inappropriate,"" the company says it can suspend or cancel the subscription—and their self-driving cars won’t work. If the vehicle loses connectivity, the self-driving cars won’t work. (“Tesla is not responsible for carrier, spectrum, environmental, or other external outages,” its “autonomy agreement” says.)
Tesla also reserves the right to make an owner’s car not work at any time: It says it can, for any safety or security purpose, suspend or terminate access to Cybercab tech, or restrict operations by location, road type, weather, or “condition.” This is particularly salient because the Cybercab is also under investigation by US safety regulators, who are trying to understand why Tesla believes the vehicle meets safety standards without so many of the parts and systems required.
Tesla didn’t respond to a request for comment.
For years, automakers have seen increasingly software-dominated vehicles as an opportunity to get drivers hooked on the same special sauce that makes Netflix, Amazon, and Spotify so potent: monthly subscription services. Because these vehicles can receive over-the-air software updates, manufacturers can offer drivers new services as they make them. The upside is high, tech-style margins that keep rolling in, versus the razor-thin ones seen in car-making. A 2024 report suggested that connected-car features could one day be a $200 billion industry.
In some ways, the subscription model is working. Today, some automakers charge extra for remote start, security, internet connectivity, and advanced driver assistance features. General Motors said it made $2.7 billion in subscription-related revenue last year from fees drivers paid for its OnStar and Super Cruise services.
But some customers have also found the business model yucky, or even offensive. Why charge extra to “unlock” a feature that’s already built into a car? If an automaker can add a new feature at any time, can’t they also take it away? And if they can take it away, what does ""ownership"" mean? In 2022, BMW told drivers in select countries it would activate factory-installed heated seats for a monthly or one-time fee; it eventually backed down after public outcry, pledging the company would no longer charge subscription fees for pre-installed hardware. (BMW is still pursuing a subscription model for some features.) “Consumers hate being nickel-and-dimed for subscriptions,” says Drury.
Tesla was an early mover in the subscription feature space and has offered connectivity and driver-assistance features for monthly or one-time fees for a decade. As the company has shifted away from making cars and toward robotics and autonomous vehicles, executives have made clear that charging for services is the future. (Its Robotaxi service currently picks up fare-paying passengers in parts of eight US cities.) So Tesla buyers may be particularly amenable to paying monthly to make their self-driving cars go.
In exchange for monthly fees, buyers will also get access to other services, too, including remote support, roadside assistance, tire, air filter, and windshield wiper replacements, and tire and camera inspections. (The agreement says that list might change, too.) Cybercabs will be able to “travel autonomously, unoccupied to a Tesla service center,” the company says. The fee doesn't cover insurance.
Cybercab buyers will have other restrictions. The vehicle is for personal use only. Even though CEO Elon Musk has repeatedly said owners will be able to put their vehicles on Tesla’s wider Robotaxi network to make money during downtime, and even has an online interest form for those who want to own and operate their own Cybercab fleets, the current ownership agreement prohibits that. Buyers are also prohibited from selling or transferring their vehicles without Tesla’s written consent. The company “advises against” children under 13 riding in the Cybercab, and prohibits children from riding without an adult present.
In a post on X, Musk showed no apparent compunction about the Cybercab business model, but did say the monthly price for autonomy “is too high.” He wrote that the price should be “more like ~$115/month.”

15,2026-11-17,"Apple could see iPhone shipments rise 7% next year, giving it a 26% share of global smartphone shipments, according to a new report. Here are the details.
As detailed by AppleInsider, a new Morgan Stanley research report says Apple is poised to ship approximately 270 million iPhones in 2027, a 7% bump from 2026.
The forecast comes amid a change of pace for Apple, which launched only the iPhone 18 Pro models and iPhone Duo this fall, with the rest of its iPhone lineup reportedly set to arrive early next year.
Morgan Stanley notes that this could push some purchases from late 2026 into 2027, contributing to the projected shipment growth without necessarily reflecting an increase in underlying demand.
The report also comes as Apple and the rest of the smartphone industry continue to grapple with rising memory and storage prices, though previous reports have suggested that Apple is better positioned than many of its competitors to absorb those costs.
Apple raised iPhone prices in September, after keeping them stable for much of the year while competitors had already introduced price hikes. In some cases, they raised prices more than once within a matter of months.
Back to the report, it notes that global smartphone shipments are expected to drop 5% next year, compared with a previous forecast of 3.6% growth. That said, it’s still an improvement over the estimated 13.8% decline in 2026.
When it comes to Apple and the iPhone specifically, Morgan Stanley notes that consumer interest in upgrading has remained largely unchanged year over year, with 37% of iPhone owners saying they are extremely likely to upgrade within the next 12 months. That’s compared with 41% of smartphone owners overall, a figure that dropped by 2 percentage points year over year.
As for the market’s reaction to the iPhone Duo, Morgan Stanley found that 22% of non-iPhone users surveyed expressed interest in switching to Apple’s foldable device, with that figure reaching 34% in China.
Importantly, Morgan Stanley’s survey was conducted after the iPhone Duo was announced, but before it launched, so these figures reflect consumer interest rather than actual purchasing behavior.
Apple Intelligence was another factor examined in the survey, with Morgan Stanley concluding that AI features are becoming less important in driving iPhone upgrades, while factors such as battery life and upgrade eligibility are gaining importance.
Finally, Morgan Stanley found that Apple’s customer retention rate increased three percentage points to a record 93%, while its net switching rate improved to 12%.
Huawei was the only other major smartphone brand to show a positive and improving net switching rate, while Samsung and Google remained relatively neutral, and Xiaomi, Motorola, Honor, Oppo, and Vivo recorded negative rates.
To read AppleInsider’s full report of Morgan Stanley’s research findings, follow this link.

17,2026-11-17,"London's main shopping thoroughfare, Oxford Street, was quiet on a grey, drizzly morning. Outside the luxury department store, Selfridges, a queue had formed for its much-hyped GTA 6 pop-up.
The shoppers, mostly men, stood bundled in raincoats, hoods and scarves, waiting to see what merchandise was left after the collection sold out online. A delivery truck nearby filled the air with the smell of fish.
Well-dressed Selfridges customers glanced at the line as they passed with the particular dry curiosity Londoners reserve for an inexplicable crowd.
The answer was Rockstar Games’ next Grand Theft Auto release — which drops on Thursday — and the chance to buy a piece of the anticipation surrounding it.
Outside, I spoke with shoppers who had joined the queue for very different reasons.
""What I want to know is, who's this for?"" said JD, a retail worker who spoke to me quietly; he didn't want anyone to know that he had come to conduct what he called ""competitor analysis.""
""If you're willing to spend £2,000 (about $2,650) on a jacket, surely you're not going to be queuing up with the rest of us,"" he said.
JD said he was primarily there to study the visual merchandising, the promotional car,  and the type of customer the pop-up was attracting.
The car, a Ferrari Testarossa with a custom blue-and-pink paint job, is the pop-up's centerpiece and is on sale for $1.3 million. The car is also the model for one of GTA 6's in-game supercars, the Grotti Cheetah.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
""It's a cultural moment,"" JD said of the game's release.
But he was skeptical of the prices. ""From what I've seen, the prices are unreal for some of this merch,"" he said.
The rain began again as we waited. I pulled my scarf over my head as a makeshift hood. Someone barged past and stepped on my foot.
Behind us, a 10-year-old named Jonny began to play with the barriers, humming songs I recognized from TikTok. He was visiting London with his father, Jon, 45, from the Netherlands. The pair had spotted the pop-up the previous day and returned early the next morning, hoping to get inside.
Jonny had preordered GTA 6 months earlier and hoped to buy a hat. ""He's nervous!"" Jon told me.
Further along the queue was David, 33, a Paris-based visitor to London. He said he had seen reports online of much longer waits and had decided to stop by only if the line was manageable.
""I thought I might as well just come and check,"" he said. ""I wasn't going to wait ages.""
When I asked if he was a fan, David shrugged. He had played “most of” the earlier games, he said, much like other people his age.
David said he might buy a relatively inexpensive item, such as a T-shirt or cap, but had not come to resell merchandise.
After I mentioned the booming resale market, though, he looked it up on his phone and showed JD and me an eBay listing: a keyring sold at the popup for $10.50 was being offered for $80.
The three of us looked at the screen.
""Wow,"" JD said.
Inside, the stock shortage was plain to see. A tall man paced the shop floor clutching a roll of cash. He asked several assistants about the items he wanted, but each told him there had been no more deliveries.
Eventually, he found an employee folding a football jersey — apparently the only one available on the floor. It was a return. The man unfurled it in the middle of the shop, only to discover that it was an extra-small women’s crop top. He scowled and put it back down.
Among the items on display were a mirrored dish featuring an alligator wearing sunglasses and the words “Vice City Leonida,” a small silver spoon, and a razor-blade keyring. 
It was a more adult side of the pop-up than excited children outside might have expected.
Elsewhere, JD got hold of what appeared to be the last baseball cap. Jon and Jonny, who had also hoped to buy one, approached the till empty-handed and began chatting to the smiling worker. Then, with something close to sleight of hand, a ""burger-shot"" visor appeared out of nowhere for them.
They bought it immediately. Jon placed it on Jonny’s head, and the boy grinned.
Earlier, as we waited outside, a group of children on a school trip, who looked about 7 or 8 years old, passed by the queue in high-visibility jackets. Several boys whooped and pointed at the pop-up. One literally jumped with excitement, his eyes wide.
They seemed almost too young to know what GTA was. But that may be the point of the pop-up: GTA 6 is no longer just a game release for longtime players. It has become a retail event, a social-media moment, and, for a growing number of shoppers, an item to collect before someone else flips it online.

18,2026-11-17,"Author of the AI Insider newsletter
Jensen Huang's dramatic departure from Nvidia may be the best thing to happen to Anthropic just as the AI startup prepares to go public this week.
Under Huang, Nvidia was investing heavily in cheaper, more open AI models that compete with Anthropic's offerings. The chip giant was also backing startups developing these alternative models and helping make them widely available.
That threatened Anthropic's business model, which involves spending heavily to develop closed AI models and charges customers to use them, often at higher prices than open alternatives.
""People really need to understand that the 'Cold War' going on here is that Nvidia was on a titanic collision course with the labs,"" the CEO of a leading AI startup told Business Insider. This person asked not to be identified while discussing sensitive topics.
The executive, who supports the open-source AI community and is sympathetic to Huang, described an ambitious Nvidia strategy to develop its own AI models, invest in rival startups, and build an ecosystem that could challenge not just Anthropic, but OpenAI too.
This strategy was just beginning to come together. But with Huang gone, the threat has diminished considerably.
The timing is extraordinary. Anthropic is preparing for a stock market debut this week that could value it at $1.4 trillion.
Investors are being asked to make an enormous bet on the future profitability of its AI services. Huang was helping build a world in which companies wouldn't necessarily have to pay Anthropic for that intelligence.
The strategy centered on ""open-weight"" AI models. Unlike Anthropic's Fable and Opus offerings and OpenAI's top GPT models, these alternative systems can be downloaded, modified, and operated independently.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
That makes them attractive to businesses that want greater control over their AI systems without paying a leading AI lab every time someone uses the technology.
Nvidia was pursuing this aggressively.
It developed its own family of open models, called Nemotron. It also backed startups including Reflection AI, Thinking Machines Lab, and Poolside, while moving to acquire Hugging Face, a major platform for sharing open-weight AI models.
Replit CEO Amjad Masad called Jensen Huang's ouster ""an attack on open models"" at a Silicon Valley conference on Monday.
""Jensen is the premier champion of open models,"" Masad said. ""If it wasn't for Jensen, there wouldn't be a lot of funding going into US open model companies.""
On November 3, I watched Huang evangelize this strategy at a Fireworks AI conference in San Francisco.
He acknowledged the value of closed AI models from companies such as Anthropic and OpenAI, but argued that businesses shouldn't have to rely on them. Instead, they could take open-weight models and adapt them to their specific needs, creating AI systems that could be even more powerful.
""By taking an open frontier model, adapting it and providing it with your domain knowledge, your access, your specialized tools, and the proper evaluation, you should be able to create essentially a super intelligence,"" Huang told Fireworks CEO Lin Qiao on stage.
""And I think those times are here,"" he added.
This strategy made business sense for Nvidia. More open models running on its chips meant more potential customers.
It was becoming increasingly important as Anthropic and OpenAI sought alternatives to Nvidia's expensive hardware.
In fact, Anthropic has developed a system that could be Nvidia's worst nightmare, as Business Insider reported in October.
Anthropic disclosed in its IPO filing that it has spent years developing software that allows it to shift AI work between different types of chips from Nvidia, Google, Amazon, and AMD. That gives Anthropic more flexibility to choose the cheapest or most effective chips for a particular task, reducing its dependence on Nvidia and potentially weakening Nvidia's ability to charge premium prices.
The shift is already significant. A November 14 analysis by Michael Cembalest of JPMorgan Asset Management estimated that Nvidia accounted for just 39% of future computing commitments by leading AI labs, down from more than 80% of AI chip revenue in recent years.
There was another key area of AI where Nvidia and Anthropic clashed.
Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei has advocated for very strict limits on advanced US chip sales to China, arguing these restrictions are essential to maintaining America's technological lead.
Huang fought against those restrictions, warning that shutting China out would encourage Chinese companies to develop competing technology.
Now he's gone, Anthropic's worldview is prevailing. On Monday, the US government rolled out new restrictions that close a loophole that allowed Chinese firms to use Nvidia GPUs by renting them from data centers outside China.
It's a win for China hawks within the administration and a huge victory for Amodei, arguably the biggest China hawk of them all.
There are caveats. For instance, Nvidia's interim CEO could continue Huang's strategies. Still, the immediate strategic implications are striking.
Anthropic is about to ask investors to bet on its future dominance of AI. Just days before that moment, one of the industry's most powerful figures pushing an alternative vision has been forced out.
For Anthropic, that's enormous.

19,2026-11-17,"
NintendoPhysical GamesRemakeThe Legend of Zelda: Ocarina Of Time 
Physical games are dying, unless your game is called The Legend of Zelda. The Switch 2 remake of Ocarina of Time just sold 1 million physical copies in its first week in the U.S., the most of any release since Tears of the Kingdom released in 2023.
“After just one week in market, The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time is 2026’s best-selling physical video game in the US,” Circana senior director and video game industry advisor Mat Piscatella posted on Bluesky on November 16. “It is the 1st title to surpass 1M physical units in a US launch week since 2023’s The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom.”
Nintendo previously announced that Ocarina of Time sold 5.2 million copies globally across physical and digital in its first four days, which blew the original game’s debut on Nintendo 64 out of the water. It’s a wild number when you consider it’s a Switch 2 exclusive. The newest Nintendo console has only been out for roughly 18 months and only has an install base of around 26 million units, making the attach rate for the game nearly one in five.
There are, of course, two ways to take the Ocarina of Time remake’s physical game sales milestone. The first is to see it as a sign of how strong Switch 2 owners’ attachment to cartridge-based gaming remains even as digital swallows most of the market for game sales. The other is to note that no other game has managed to sell 1 million physical copies in the U.S. within its first week in over three years.
Physical game sales in the U.S. hit a 30-year low in 2025. Back in July, amid the frenzy over Sony’s announcement of the end of PlayStation discs in 2028, Piscatella pointed out that only seven PS5 games released in the first half of 2026 had sold more than 100,000 physical copies at the time. Total spending on physical games actually bounced upward this past August, however, which appears to have been thanks to Switch 2 game key cards.
Physical games might be dying, but on Switch 2 they’re not going without a fight.

20,2026-11-17,"When Silicon Valley entrepreneur Chad Steelberg decided he wanted to get involved in the war in Ukraine, he had no idea where to start. So the then CEO of enterprise AI company Veritone did what he knew best: built an artificial intelligence model to help him figure it out. Today, he has proprietary munition and missile designs, several government partnerships, and a defense-as-a-service business valued at more than $1 billion.
Steelberg, alongside fellow Google alumnus and former Apple executive Andy Baynes, says they are trying to break the Russia-Ukraine war “stalemate,” Silicon Valley–style. They believe their experience building and iterating technology quickly can help bring Western weapon manufacturing in line with the rapid innovation cycles seen on the battlefield and beat the “attritional cat-and-mouse technology game” that the war, as Baynes describes it, has become.
Steelberg, originally from Newport Beach, California, started itching to “make a difference” on the battlefield after he took in a couple hundred Ukrainian refugees into his French chateau in early 2022. He says he was regularly “heartbroken” by the outpouring of grief whenever bad news arrived about a husband, father, or son back home. “I believe I could really change the outcome of this and every war,” Steelberg tells WIRED. ""I'm not saying this is purely altruistic,” he adds. “Of course we're going to be making money doing it.""
This week, their firm, Tiberius Aerospace, said it had secured $85 million in Series A financing, led by European technology investment firm Lakestar and Rochefort Asset Management, a national-security-focused investment firm licensed under the US Defense Department. Ukrainian venture capital firm UA1 is also an investor.
Tiberius began as Steelberg’s private research lab, incorporated in 2021, which employed people across France, the UK, and US to experiment on left-field ideas across a range of technologies, from drones and electric vertical takeoff aircraft to quantum computing. After the invasion, he pivoted the lab’s AI and aerospace engineers into writing code and building spreadsheets to objectively analyze the war’s many factors and calculate what could move the needle. The AI model they created quickly ruled out building better defensive technology because that would just harden the stalemate.
With his aim narrowed to building an offensive weapon, Steelberg developed several metrics to numerically analyze the lethality of different systems, including cost-effectiveness, and used the AI engine to simulate weapons under different conditions and identify what would disrupt the market. Don’t build another drone, it suggested, build longer-range artillery munitions. The resulting system, called Sceptre, can be fired from a 155-millimeter howitzer—the most common artillery weapon donated to Ukraine. As well as traveling farther than currently available rounds, they claim they can reach supersonic speeds and altitudes beyond jamming range.
Giving Ukraine’s guns extra range is important, Sam Cranny-Evans, an associate fellow at UK defense think tank the Royal United Services Institute, tells WIRED, because it would allow Ukrainian gunners to operate beyond the reach of Russian drone attacks. 155-mm shells “can cause massive damage to a group of vehicles or infantry,” he says. “Any technology that can help Ukraine extend the reach of its howitzers and return them to combat would be valuable.”
With a prototype he’d designed with the help of AI, Steelberg also started to expand the Tiberius team, including bringing on board Baynes as chief strategy officer in 2023. Baynes had first traveled to Ukraine around a decade ago to investigate how local startup Ring, now owned by Amazon, had outdone Nest, which he’d been working on and eventually sold to Google. He fell in love with Kyiv’s ambitious software engineers and booming tech scene–responsible for globally successful tech tools like Grammarly and Snapchat’s face filters. In 2016, he bought a local life-science tech company with the aim of accelerating next-generation medicines through automating clinical trials and improving protein modeling. “What drew me into Kyiv was the hunger and ambition in the engineering community,” he tells WIRED. “It was difficult to not be enormously attracted to that and to see if it's something that could be put into really critical-mission problem sets like cancer drugs or speeding up clinical trials for life-science companies.”
After the Russian invasion, Baynes set himself up in Budapest for around six months to help with the logistics of evacuating women and children and moving equipment into the country. He asked his global network for help finding medical equipment, vehicles, and accommodation for refugees—bringing him back in touch with Steelberg. Baynes believes the West’s response to the invasion has been “inadequate” and that Ukraine needs better offensive capabilities to push Russia’s army back.
In April, Tiberius announced it had successfully fired one of its Sceptre projectiles from a NATO-standard 155-mm howitzer for the first time. It is now working with the US Army to mature the system, and it has announced a $500 million technology collaboration contract with UK partners to manufacture, license, and distribute the munition. It is also developing a missile, which it expects to start generating revenue next year.
Tiberius has turned the AI system it initially built into a product called GRAIL (Generative Real-Time Artificial Intelligence for Lethality). Steelberg says he is “most excited about” this. He hopes the platform can enable continuous upgrades to the weapon designs Tiberius licenses by decentralizing manufacturing. Steelberg says that, as well as automating supply chain management and compliance, the software simulates weapons against different targets in a range of environments and weather conditions, such as an Arctic peninsula. In addition to open-sourced data, the team is trying to get hold of much sought-after active combat data from around the world, including Ukraine. The idea is that this all enables users to test, iterate, and design weapons for specific uses much quicker.
“In the world of AI and design and development getting faster and faster, what is it that the West or the group of allied nations have an undeniable advantage on?” Baynes asks. “If you can weaponize all of the spare capacity and precision manufacturing across the UK, France, Germany, the Nordic countries, the US,” he says, “that's something that our adversaries today can never ever match.” Tiberius aims to achieve this by copying “the Apple model,” continues Baynes, who reported to Steve Jobs for four years. He says outsourcing manufacturing to many third-party subcontractors can accelerate development and deployment and keeps expensive production costs off Tiberius’ balance sheet during peacetime.
“We're taking the approach of Silicon Valley,” says Steelberg. “When you run into a big, hard, hairy problem, you not only cross that, but you turn around, you build a bridge—it’s a toll bridge, obviously—so the entire industry can cross that hurdle faster, better, stronger. They might pay a small fee, but it's better than dying in the ditch and not making it across it.”
