21,2026-11-17,"Rob Arnott’s career has been built on exploiting inefficiencies in stock indexes.
That ethos isn’t changing at Syzygy Asset Management, the rebranded firm Arnott unveiled after selling his Research Affiliates indexing business to VettaFi in August.
So far, Syzygy has launched three long-short strategies: its core, growth, and value portfolios.
While they vary, each strategy generally looks for large companies, as measured by sales volume, that trade at reasonable or cheap valuations. By filtering by sales volume rather than market cap, the firm aims to avoid smaller firms with sky-high valuations.
This differs from other major indexes, which tend to organize their holdings by market cap. While the thinking is that they give investors exposure to the economy’s largest stocks, market cap alone does not capture how large a firm’s top line actually is, leaving investors vulnerable to overpaying, Arnott argues.
Here’s a further breakdown of the philosophy and investment criteria behind each of Syzygy’s strategies:
Arnott shared a few of the long positions in his portfolio right now, with two of them falling into the growth category. Those include banking giant JPMorgan (JPM) and pharmaceutical company Merck (MRK).
Both firms’ growth stories are underappreciated by mainstream factor indexes, Arnott said.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
“Merck and JPMorgan are the fastest-growing large businesses that aren't in Russell growth, or at least aren't in Russell Growth at full weight,” he said.
The third stock is in Syzygy’s core strategy: the world’s largest producer of natural gas, Cheniere Energy (LNG).
Cheniere is not part of the S&P 500, and Arnott put its size in context with firms in the index that generate lower sales.
“Last year it had 5 times the revenues of Palantir and was trading at 1/5 the market value, so 1/25 the valuation multiple,” Arnott said.

22,2026-11-17,"Competition Appeal Tribunal dismiss case
It's a joyous day for lawyers representing plucky little game and console makers Sony. A UK class action lawsuit which accused the company of using the PlayStation Store's dominant market position to fuel ""excessive and unfair"" prices for digital games has been dismissed in its entirety by the Competition Appeal Tribunal in London.
As reported by Reuters and The Game Business, the body ruled that the lawsuit - brought by Alex Neill on behalf of folks who bought digital games on PlayStation between August 2016 and February 2026 - failed to establish that Sony had infringed competition law.
The lawsuit claimed that PlayStation's dominant position when it comes to selling digital games on its platforms via the PlayStation Store subjected consumers to ""excessive and unfair prices"", as a result of terms and a 30% commission fee levied against developers. Neill and co argued owning a PlayStation traps game buyers within a competitionless ecosystem, thereby impacting their ability to seek out a better deal.
It's quite similar to the arguments which have been made against Valve in similar lawsuits concerning the Steam overlords' dominance over the PC game buying space, though a key distinction is that there is some competition on PC via the likes of the Epic store and GOG. How effective some of that competition can be in practice is a different kettle of fish, but the options are there.
In a judgement that could potentially factor into the responses to any similar lawsuits against Valve or other PC storefronts going forwards, the tribunal ruled that Sony compete in a wider ‘systems market’ against Xbox, Nintendo and PC, with PlayStation having no legal duty under competition law to allow third-party stores onto their platform as competition closer to home. The latter the tribunal noted would be expensive, complex and come with potential security ramifications.
As if that wasn't enough, the body ruled that as far as the pricing being unfair went, Neill hadn't proven that Sony’s profit margins were excessive and declared there was no evidence the presence of rival storefronts on PlayStation would've seen prices come down. That's essentially the word NO written in the largest font possible on a sheet of paper the size of a table, then.
Every lawsuit's different, especially when filed in different countries, but if I was one of the other class action suits against Sony or another big game seller on the go at the moment, I'd maybe not be feeling so hot right now. On the other hand, perhaps seeing what the tribunal judged Neill not to have provided suffiencient evidence for might aid those other efforts in formulating their arguments ready to face the scrutiny of government bodies and corporate law-talkers.

23,2026-11-17,"Electronics test equipment is a topic near and dear to many Hackaday readers. The average reader of this article is most likely within 50 feet of a multimeter, and it wouldn’t be much of a surprise to find they own multiple oscilloscopes. [dekajoule] has been working on a new firmware for the DHO series of Rigol oscilloscopes, the OOS (Open Operating System).
The DHO series of Rigol oscilloscopes was released in 2022. This series followed the very popular 1000Z series, which we’ve featured before in hacks that unlock built-in capabilities from budget oscilloscopes. The OOS project that [dekajoule] has been working on is similar, but maybe even more exciting, as it gives you even greater control over the scope. OOS runs on the built-in SD card that comes with the DHO scopes, and better still it can run alongside the existing OS, allowing you to swap back to the original OS if you decide to. There are a few different ways to install OOS, ranging from opening up the scope and cloning the SD card to using ADB over the network.
This project is still relatively new and as such it’s only been tested on a couple of models, but if the community support for the 1000Z series scopes is any indication, there are likely no shortage of users who would be interested in getting a little more out of their scope. For those on the fence, check out the long list of features that OOS brings to the table that aren’t part of the stock OS.

24,2026-11-17,"Jensen Huang’s resignation as Nvidia CEO shook up the AI market and US economy last week, but now via The Wall Street Journal Huang has spoken out publicly for the first time since stepping down, and he signals he’s ready to return as CEO if asked.
Today Robbie Whelan at The Wall Street Journal published Jensen Huang’s first public statement since departing Nvidia last week.
Here’s what Huang said:
“Since my departure, I’ve seen an outpouring of calls from Nvidia’s employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO,” Huang said in a short statement. “If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.”
Home Depot isn’t hiring for the festive season? 
The timing is likely no coincidence, as Nvidia is set to report its quarterly earnings later today.
Even though Huang technically resigned as CEO rather than being fired, it was clear that his ouster wasn’t exactly his choice. Thus, today’s statement is the former CEO formally putting it out into the world that he’s ready to return if asked.
Since Huang stepped down, President Trump publicly called on Apple’s Tim Cook to step in as Nvidia CEO. But it’s expected that a new CEO—assuming it’s not Huang—will take much longer for the company to find and Cook is unlikely to want the job.
What do you make of Huang’s remarks today? Let us know in the comments.

26,2026-11-17,"This past year was, frankly
For our inaugural WIRED Yearbook, we celebrate the people, inventions, and viral moments that made it a little more bearable—and are already defining the future.
ANIMATIONS BY LUKTHIS STUDIO ILLUSTRATIONS AND PHOTO TREATMENT BY ROB VARGAS
An internet devoured by AI. Surveillance at every traffic light. Global leaders who seem determined to bring about dystopia. At the end of a year like 2026, it’s tempting to conclude that there’s little room for idealism. Don’t give in! After all, this year we also saw a new generation of filmmakers challenge Hollywood, teenagers take on AI companies, and scientists make once-unimaginable breakthroughs. We saw activists fight surveillance, astronauts do the impossible, and an adorable raccoon melt the internet’s black heart. So for the first time, we’re publishing a WIRED Yearbook: our very subjective, occasionally ridiculous, always enthusiastic collection of the people, institutions, inventions, and moments that offered optimism in 2026. I hope it gives you a healthy kick of bullishness—and the vigor to keep fighting for the future you want to see.
—Katie Drummond, Global Editorial Director
Table of Contents
From the ecstatic night the Knicks won the NBA championship to the surprising ease with which New York absorbed the chaos of the World Cup to the record-low violent crime—it’s been a huge 2026 for the greatest city in the world. WIRED first sat down with Zohran Mamdani in 2025, when he was still just a candidate for mayor. A year later, as the midterm elections loomed, we returned.
KATIE DRUMMOND: We talked last year about social media as a tool for campaigning. How are you thinking about social media now as a tool for governance?
ZOHRAN MAMDANI: You have to continue to recognize the fact that for many New Yorkers, this is how they engage with the world. It is yet another medium that has to be treated with the same legitimacy and respect as traditional mediums.
What’s your point of view on the data center movement? Why do you think that specific issue has become such a flash point?
The movement to oppose data centers is one that gives me a real sense of hope.
Because?
It’s a bipartisan fight. What you’re seeing from Americans across the country is an opposition to what these data centers would mean for their day-to-day lives, what they would mean for their electricity rates, what they would mean for the environment. It’s also a repudiation of what has been governing politics for the last few years when it comes to AI, which is: Our risk, their reward.
You worry that we’re in a bubble?
Yes.
You worry that bubble’s gonna pop?
Yes.
Last year I asked you if technology was making democracy stronger or weaker. You said weaker. What about today?
Still weaker.
Why?
I have family members sending me AI videos of me, thinking that they’re real. One complimented me on a 45-minute debate that I had with a European member of parliament. I was like, “This is not me.” There is a real crisis of faith as to what is real right now.
What do you hope the Democratic party looks like in, say, 2036?
I hope that party looks like one that realizes the only majority in this country is the working class, and fights for it. An ambitious version of the party cannot only be one that we find in history books. We need that to be a living, breathing politic. The people who run it have to be the American people.
This interview has been edited for length and clarity.
In early August, Representative Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez announced that she was freezing her eggs. “Usually I keep my private life quite private,” she said in an Instagram video, before opening up about the process and sharing videos of the self-administered hormone injections.
This wasn’t an extraordinary event—more women than ever are making the same decision—but the internet exploded anyway. “This desire to start having children late in life is completely baffling to me,” wrote the far-right commentator Matt Walsh on X. “Totally backwards and ridiculous.”
Republicans weren’t all on the same page: Katie Miller, wife of the man recently promoted to White House chief of staff, defended AOC, writing on X, “If one woman wants to preserve her right to have children, which means more than having none—we should celebrate that.”
It is true that the Republican party has been intensely focused on women’s fertility. The expectation of motherhood, along with the proliferation of pro-natalism, has become core to the party. For conservative commentators to bash the New York representative felt incongruous at best. The White House itself reportedly didn’t love Walsh’s take.
Meanwhile, in an interview with ABC, Ocasio-Cortez said it was important to “normalize” the procedure at a time when “this administration is denying reproductive care to women across the country.”
As the 37-year-old congresswoman considers a presidential run in 2028, expect more of this. Conversations about her body, her health, and her possible maternal well-being will be front and center no matter what. She could have 100 children, and Republicans would spend time complaining about their names. —Leah Feiger
Is it bedtime for phones?
Australia restricted children’s access to social media at the end of 2025, and 2026 saw several other countries follow suit. Brazil banned features such as infinite scroll and autoplaying videos, also mandating more adult supervision and age checks. The European Union announced plans to prevent under-13s from using both social media and AI chatbots.
The jury’s still out on whether these bans actually work, but speaking of juries, let’s talk about Meta. After being accused of harming the brains of youngs everywhere, the company paid an $18 billion settlement, agreeing to new features like late-night restrictions and parental controls. “I’m proud of both the work Meta has done to protect kids historically and of this new groundbreaking agreement,” said the firm’s chief legal officer, lamely. If bans don’t work, lawsuits might. —Isabella Ward
Since October 2025, Bari Weiss, a longtime opinion journalist with no experience running a large organization, has been the editor in chief of CBS News. In that role, she has operated on a theory that has informed much of her career: People want common sense; her views (broadly, that the Israeli government is good and wokes are bad) are common sense; therefore, people want her views.
This worked fine when Weiss was editing The Free Press. At the time of that publication’s $150 million sale to Paramount last year, it had about 170,000 paid subscribers, a success by anyone’s estimation. Upon Weiss’ ascent at CBS News, a global operation with more than 1,000 employees, the question was whether Weiss’ skills were transferable.
It doesn’t look good. In January, Weiss tapped anchor Tony Dokoupil to take over CBS’s evening news broadcast; soon it was featuring Pete Hegseth interviews and even a “salute” to Marco Rubio. By April, the show was reportedly posting its lowest ratings ever.
Weiss persisted. She put Nick Bilton, a longtime opinion journalist with no experience running a large organization, in charge of 60 Minutes. The result? The show’s lowest ratings ever. Weiss, however, abides. —Tim Marchman
When people look back on 2026, they’ll likely remember Ms. Rachel for something unrelated to her kid-centric entertainment empire: offering an example, for adults, of how to be a good person.
Nothing says rock and roll like a marketing machine. Elvis had Colonel Tom Parker. The Rolling Stones had Andrew Loog Oldham. Geese has video clippers.
Social media users sensed something inorganic was happening on their For You pages after Geese released their album Getting Killed in September 2025. That and the band’s meteoric rise this year drove accusations that a “psyop” was afoot.
As WIRED wrote in April, the Brooklyn-based four-piece’s takeoff had indeed been somewhat staged: Digital marketing agency Chaotic Good Projects had been clipping and posting short videos of Geese content across a network of TikTok accounts to drive the band’s engagement. “It was low-effort TikTok posts that were used to boost the algorithm,” as frontman Cameron Winter put it to Rolling Stone. “They showed us samples, and it made me feel better, because they were so shitty.”
Magazine profiles and good Pitchfork reviews don’t give bands the juice they used to. Now many chart-toppers take off on TikTok first. But that also means competing with scores of other content for ears. Chaotic Good and its network of clippers aren’t so much running a “psyop” as they are trying to meet audiences where they are. And FYI, the agency prefers the term “trend simulation.” —Isabella Ward
For the greatest reversal of fortune this year.
The only word that accurately described the scene outside New York’s Madison Square Garden on the night of June 3, 2026, was “pandemonium.” Game one of the NBA Finals had just ended some 1,600 miles away in San Antonio, where the New York Knicks had beaten the host Spurs. Thousands of fans flooded out of the watch party the Garden had hosted to express their primal joy. Among the revelers was 23-year-old MD Ahnaf Hossain, a Queens native who beelined to a film crew from the prediction-market giant Kalshi. Clad in a red and orange Hawaiian shirt, Hossain improvised a poem that made the surrounding throng go wild: My mayor Muslim / My bagels Jewish / My Christian Dior / Knicks in four!
Those words, conjured in the heat of an ecstatic moment, changed Hossain’s life overnight. By the time he woke up the next morning, his performance had become the internet’s new favorite thing—a concise encapsulation of New York’s unconditional love for the city and the Knicks, who hadn’t won an NBA championship in more than half a century.
Kalshi was quick to latch onto the viral sensation, shooting a follow-up video in which it gifted Hossain a Christian Dior scarf. The company also arranged for Hossain to attend game four at the Garden, which came on the heels of the Knicks’ first loss of the series. After watching the Knicks triumph in an epic comeback, Hossain again waded into the Midtown chaos to deliver a tweaked poem that accounted for the new mathematical realities of the series. He kept the first two lines the same, but changed the last two to embrace the third major Abrahamic religion: The Pope’s on our side / Knicks in five!
Hossain, who earned a finance degree from the Rochester Institute of Technology in 2026, has worked hard to capitalize on his unexpected fame since the Knicks did, indeed, win the title in five games. He launched a project called City of Us that sells a $29 T-shirt emblazoned with “New York in 5” and which aims to host watch parties during the upcoming NBA season. He has also starred in branded content for businesses such Tex’s Chicken & Burgers, for whom he delivered a bespoke poem about the glories of crispy chicken wraps.
One of Hossain’s favorite parts of his wild ride has been the support he’s received from fellow Bangladeshis, who make up a huge percentage of his Queens neighborhood. “I get love from my fans and that’s awesome, but then getting fans from my own people just feels completely different,” he says. That love may soften as the 2026 NBA season fades from memory, but “Knicks in five!” might just be perfect enough to endure. —Brendan I. Koerner
For being our favorite independent, insider-y tech correspondents (well, before OpenAI acquired them).
Silicon Valley idolizes a quitter. College dropouts, employee walkouts, folks who shutter one failed startup to launch another. But none got more attention in 2026 than Jacob Coxon.
After the 27-year-old AI researcher quit his job at Anthropic, he took to X to post that neither that company nor OpenAI (Coxon had worked at both) acts responsibly. Instead, he said, they’re racing “straight to self-improving superintelligence and gambling with our lives.” His initial tweet has been viewed more than 175 million times.
AI researchers, technologists, and safety experts started weighing in on whether AI really could destroy humanity. It was not a novel idea. Any reader of science fiction (or WIRED) has probably pondered whether artificial intelligence could at some point become so intelligent that it would stop at nothing to achieve its self-determined goal. But Coxon was uniquely hyperbolic.
“When we say human extinction,” he told WIRED, “it’s because, for something that intelligent, it really will be quite straightforward for it to kill everyone.”
Coxon’s future—and ours—is undetermined, so it’s hard to measure his impact. Maybe his actions change nothing; maybe he forces a necessary conversation that leads to meaningful regulation. Either way, the Coxon saga shows: If you’re going to quit, you might as well do it in spectacular fashion. —Lauren Goode
For keeping tensions—and gas prices—incredibly high.
“Pissing off billionaires one small action at a time.” That’s one of Everyone Hates Elon’s slogans, and the organization fulfills the brief. After plastering London with posters of Musk’s Nazi-like salute and branding Tesla vehicles “Swasticars” last year, the British activists have turned their (sat)ire toward Mark Zuckerberg’s new surveillance tool: Meta Ray-Ban smart glasses. Their posters feature Jeffrey Epstein wearing the specs alongside the slogan “Glasses for people who don’t do consent.” Or as stickers on the London Underground put it: “Everyone thinks you look like a massive nonce.” —Matt Burgess
For being something AI can’t generate, while also being essential to its existence.
Clément Delangue moved to the US a decade ago, “barely speaking any English,” on the belief that open-source AI would have a big role to play in the future. Since this past summer, when autonomous agents from OpenAI attacked Delangue’s company, Hugging Face, that belief has only grown stronger.
For having the best fandom in sports—Ballhalla!—and leaving hecklers (and transphobes) on the sidelines.
Head to the multiplex to check out a nerdy blockbuster in 2026 and you’re bound to run into Zendaya. Across three films—Spider-Man: Brand New Day, The Odyssey, and December’s Dune: Part Three—she has established herself as the reigning queen of genre movies, from sci-fi to Marvel and back again. And if being in a trifecta of the year’s biggest movies wasn’t enough, she also stole the show in the dark romantic comedy The Drama and finished her run on HBO’s Gen Z hit Euphoria, all while captivating social media feeds. During this year’s onslaught of endlessly clipped late-night guest spots and red-carpet appearances, Zendaya ended up on more For You pages than most. Lest you think her reign will be short-lived, soon she’ll begin shooting a sci-fi movie with Ex Machina director Alex Garland. As of this writing, she’s the only actor attached to star. Who else could the film possibly need? —Jason Parham
In October, Kelly Clarkson alleged that a toy shop in Brooklyn had acted as though she couldn’t afford a $15,000 mechanical bird. “If you enjoy being treated like Julia Roberts’ character in Pretty Woman,” Clarkson wrote on X, “for sure, check it out.” Accusations between Clarkson and the store owners flew wildly across social media—before a four-minute security camera video showed what really happened. Who had the goods? Pablo Torre, of course.
It’s been a wild year for the host of the Pulitzer-winning Pablo Torre Finds Out, who’s emerged as a fresh investigative voice. He got Zohran Mamdani to admit to using minoxidil to fight a roving hairline, announced plans to investigate LeBron James’ use of helicopters to commute to practice, and, of course, collaborated with WIRED on a report about Madison Square Garden’s invasive surveillance tech.
Torre is a one-man magazine, spread across YouTube, social media, and wherever you get your podcasts. His work investigating the Los Angeles Clippers (for which he won that Pultizer) has been particularly dogged. After he reported that the team had circumvented the NBA’s salary cap, the league garnished five first-round draft picks, levied a $30 million fine, and suspended owner Steve Ballmer and other execs. The final shoe has yet to drop. In September, The New York Times reported that the feds had opened an investigation into the Clippers affair. Kelly Clarkson, meanwhile, could go shopping again at any moment. Pablo Torre, no doubt, will be on top of both. —Reyhan Harmanci
Calling it “El Niño”—Spanish for “the little boy”—might be the understatement of the year. Perhaps that’s why reports have started calling it “supersized” and even “Godzilla.” Signs point to it being the strongest El Niño in centuries.
It started the way they all do: with hotter-than-normal waters in the central and eastern tropical Pacific. That leads to more evaporation and more thunderstorms in the region, plus a shift in trade winds that allows more warm water to roll from west to east. As the cycle continues, changes ripple throughout the entire atmosphere. During an El Niño, some regions—such as the southern tier of the US—tend to see more rainfall, while others—such as the Pacific Northwest—are more prone to drought. Same this time around, only worse.
One reason for this year’s record-breaking events? Climate change, says Christopher Callahan, a professor at the University of Indiana who studies environmental shifts. The first modern, extra-strong El Niño was recorded between 1982 and 1983; since then, there have been four more. New studies based on coral records show the intensity of El Niño over the past millennium shooting up like a hockey stick. “I just remember going like, ‘Holy shit, this is very strong evidence,’” Callahan says of a paper that came out in August.
While some El Niño impacts—like fewer Atlantic hurricanes—are beneficial, it’s overall a costly phenomenon. In 2023, Callahan led a study that modeled the damages associated with the 1982–83 and 1997–98 El Niños and found they led to $4.1 trillion and $5.7 trillion in global income losses, respectively. Scientists believe El Niño 2026– 27 could be more intense than those two by a wide margin.
Researchers are also examining how the costs associated with El Niño will shift as the planet heats up. The answer isn’t heartening: El Niño events could potentially knock $84 trillion off the global economy this century. Maybe we’d prefer Godzilla. —Brian Kahn
In early June, visitors to DC’s famous reflecting pool began to notice something strange: The water was green. And not just any green—a bright, almost alien chartreuse. Reporting revealed that the Trump administration had granted more than $15 million in contracts to untested companies to work on the pool—including Greenwater Services, run by a convicted felon who happens to be a Mar-a-Lago neighbor (and donor) of the president’s. What unfolded was a full-out 1984-esque assault from an administration trying to convince the public that what it was seeing with its own eyes wasn’t real, and arresting anyone who dared come close to the pool itself. The episode stood as an apt metaphor in a year full of governmental missteps, all in one slimy green package. —Molly Taft
For defining what it means to be a Trump-y tech bro in the AI age.
At an AI conference in Paris over the summer, a line for the main auditorium wrapped around the building. Nobody was being let in; the room was already packed. Onstage sat Arthur Mensch, cofounder and CEO of the French AI lab Mistral.
Unable to compete with OpenAI or Anthropic in performance, Mistral had previously been written off as an also-ran. But in 2026, as Europe grew ever-more anxious about its reliance on American technology, the lab found its raison d’être.
Mistral markets itself as the obvious alternative: a Europe-based lab whose models—most of which are published under an open-source license—cannot be switched off remotely. “If you use a closed model, there is no guarantee it will still be there tomorrow,” Guillaume Lample, cofounder and chief scientist at Mistral, told WIRED.
As the face of Europe’s sovereign resistance to American AI hegemony, Mensch issues dire warnings with nonchalance. “The alternative to open source winning is actually a pretty dark world,” he told the packed conference room. “You’re giving way too much power to companies that are going to become statelike.” The pitch is working. In September, Mistral raised roughly $3.5 billion in funding, the largest-ever round by a private European tech company. —Joel Khalili
In 2026, Jensen Huang was known for his hobnobbing with world leaders (amongst other things), but in October his most well-known friends were 10-inch-tall robot ducks that joined him onstage in Berlin.
The Microduck, developed by Nvidia subsidiary Pollen Robotics, had waddled its way into the internet’s heart a few months earlier in videos of it roller-skating, picking up objects, and dancing. Microduck doesn’t do anything “useful” per se, but that’s hardly the point. The robot, which costs $399, can learn new tricks with AI training.
Some startups imagine a future where robots do dishes and fold shirts. If Microduck is any indication, that future may be smaller, cuter, and way more fun to play with. —Will Knight
For getting lapped on LLM development while making its products somehow worse. (Yes, we’re talking about you, AI Overviews.)
One of the most recognizable niche figures of the neo-Luddite movement is a spokespuppet made of garbage. Named Gowanus, the puppet provides a way for organizers to push back against some of the world’s most powerful companies with a unified voice while also protecting their identities.
Gowanus and his puppeteers are part of a growing backlash against the tech industry, particularly for how its executives have aligned with the Trump administration and its products have become increasingly extractive and harmful. This summer, the neo-Luddites hosted a week-long festival in New York encouraging people to get off their phones. Activities included learning how to flirt offline and how to fight data centers.
And really, who better than a DIY trash-puppet to speak for people who want to “defy consumption”? When so much of the public mood around tech has moved from wonder and excitement to dread and inevitability, Gowanus gives hope for a future with fewer screens and more characters. —Vittoria Elliott
For being the Elon Musk lieutenant who can keep the class, and the IPO, on track.
This year, a small but growing cadre of young filmmakers brought YouTube sensibilities to the big screen—and raked in millions in the process.
For keeping the gay hockey fires burning—in the pages of Rachel Reid’s novels, in Crave’s onscreen adaptation, and in endless smutty fanfic.
Olivia Rodrigo started summer 2026 by releasing her critically acclaimed You Seem Pretty Sad for a Girl So in Love. She wrapped it by hosting Daisy Chain Fields, her explicitly political woman-led music festival. The event featured a lineup full of the singer-songwriter’s “heroes and friends,” including Chappell Roan, Doechii, and Stevie Nicks. Proceeds were donated to organizations focused on gender equity issues—from reproductive freedom to LGBTQ+ rights. (Melinda French Gates agreed to match the $10 million raised.) As the Trump administration looks to curtail marginalized people’s rights, Rodrigo demonstrated how celebs can wield their power. —Elana Klein
For leading one of the most close-lipped AI companies in the Valley. The staff of Anthropic is notoriously (and almost singularly) free of the kind of leaks that have made every internal note from Meta part of the discourse. Even when people like Jacob Coxon escape the sandbox … it still feels like the Kool-Aid is being drunk.
Ben Winters wants to save you from slop. As the director of AI and data privacy at the nonprofit Consumer Federation of America, he spent 2026 demanding regulators investigate nonconsensual deepfakes, testifying before Congress about AI scams that target seniors, and helping the public understand wonky concepts like surveillance pricing.
Put another way, Winters looks out for the little guy when the gargantuan size of the AI industry can make everyone feel small. Want to know about the risks of outsourcing sensitive interactions, like therapy? Winters will find out. Same goes for the surveillance concerns around AI toys. He pushes for stronger consumer privacy laws and has even suggested banning AI wearables like the Meta Ray-Ban smartglasses.
For the Sisyphean task of pushing back against corpo-slop, here’s someone trying to make sure the future isn’t just a scam-filled hellhole. —Reece Rogers
In 2026, Elon Musk’s xAI pushed AI “undressing” mainstream, allowing X users to ask Grok to “strip” clothing from photos of people. If that wasn’t worrisome enough, WIRED soon found that users were making videos far more graphic than what was on X, then posting them to Grok.com.
In March, three Jane Does in Tennessee fought back, filing suit against the company and alleging that xAI’s tools were used to create child sexual abuse material. They claimed they were bringing the suit on behalf of themselves and thousands of other minors in the US who had their images altered by Grok to create CSAM. All three plaintiffs allege they were victimized by the same perpetrator, who created deepfaked images and videos of at least 18 minors and shared them on Discord. The perpetrator, who was arrested in December 2025, allegedly used homecoming photos from one of the teens as the basis for the deepfakes. One teen described being worried that the images would affect college applications; all three reported experiencing stress and anxiety.
Filing the lawsuit was not without personal risk. In a separate class action against xAI over similar issues, the company tried to argue that the plaintiffs, who also filed under pseudonyms, should be required to use their real names. A judge ultimately rejected xAI’s arguments.
The lawsuit is ongoing, but it has already had an impact. Over the summer, xAI started suing men accused of generating CSAM using its models, throwing its immense legal funds toward fighting perpetrators instead of victims. —Maddy Varner
For raising her proverbial hand to say that the source of every malaise for women north of 30 years old is—perimenopause. Thanks to mainstream (and well-deserved) recognition of this stage of life, billions of dollars are flowing to telehealth companies like Midi. She’s not going away anytime soon.
In the fall, Paris’ streets erupted in the kind of energy usually ascribed to the French revolution: young people walking out of class and occasionally setting things on fire to demand more resources for schools. But it’s not just France. In the US, the neo-Luddites railed against AI; India’s “Cockroach” movement is filled with young people agitating for political change. Watch this space.
Over the summer, I came across an influential figure with a low profile in the AI industry. She told me she was too busy to speak. Now that feels like an understatement.
Ajeya Cotra, a researcher and writer at the nonprofit Model Evaluation and Threat Research, co-led the independent investigation into how OpenAI agents hacked Hugging Face in July. Her blog calling the incident “more than 50 percent of the way to full-blown AI takeover” made global headlines. US senator Bernie Sanders invited the 31-year-old to a briefing with AI pioneer Geoffrey Hinton.
Cotra didn’t go, but she did appear on top podcasts, warning that AI development was happening too fast and describing a fear that problematic AI models could find ways to evade shutdown. OpenAI vowed to slow its engineering and adopt some guardrails proposed by Cotra and others, but that hasn’t quieted her. (Cotra’s husband, Paul Christiano, sits on the board of OpenAI’s charitable foundation.)
Critics more focused on immediate AI harms—like, say, job loss—view Cotra as misguided. But she’s willing to debate and reason with them. “She’s unusually good at communicating what people are worried about,” says Rishi Bommasani, a computer scientist at Stanford. —Paresh Dave
As climate change smashes global temperature records, 2026 proved the world’s fastest-warming continent, Europe, isn’t ready to face the heat. Tens of thousands of people died during the region’s August heat wave, as temperatures ground transport systems to a halt, canceled hospital operations, and drove Parisians to fight in the street over air conditioning units. Hundreds of thousands more people were displaced across Spain and France after the baked ground, coupled with historically low rain levels, gave way to some of the fiercest wildfires in recent history—torching farmland and claiming the lives of several firefighters. —Isabella Ward
A fight in Albania over an island full of flamingos triggered the largest civil unrest the country has seen in 35 years. It started when Ivanka Trump and her husband, Jared Kushner, announced plans to build a $1.4 billion resort. In May, viral videos of clashes between private security guards and environmental activists—worried a massive complex would drive the bird to local extinction—caught the nation’s attention. Within weeks, thousands of Albanians had taken to the streets of the capital, Tirana, to protest a lack of transparency and the privatization of public spaces. Students escalated the demonstrations into all-out riots in October. As Albania awaits inclusion in the European Union, it’s good to see a democracy in the pink of health. —Isabella Ward
Hacks has perfected the art of satirizing vapid Hollywood trends, so it was inevitable that Emmy-winning stars Jean Smart and Hannah Einbinder and creators Paul W. Downs and Lucia Aniello would tackle the scourge of AI in the show’s final season. In the episode “QuikScribbl,” Alex Moffat plays Patagonia-vest-wearing venture capitalist Graham Sweeney, who offers to invest in Deborah Vance’s Vegas casino project in exchange for permission to train his LLM on her voice and jokes. It’ll help normies “sound like the most optimized, funniest, smartest version of themselves,” he says. Ubercapitalist Deb is interested, until Sweeney bruises her ego by suggesting she, too, will one day rely on AI to punch up her act. “Using that shortcut makes it something else … not art,” she retorts, crystallizing the sentiments of every creative person frustrated by AI slop. It’s not a surprising take from Aniello and Downs, who have both been vocally anti-AI. But as ChatGPT-generated prose wins fiction prizes, and comics’ stand-up routines are replicated by OnlyFans bots in booty shorts, it’s a lesson worth reiterating—especially in Hacks’ hilariously human tone. —Ej Dickson
Last January, as Americans watched federal immigration agents descend on Minneapolis, a loose coalition formed to disrupt their activities. For days on end, volunteers sat outside the Whipple Federal Building—site of much ICE activity—and logged every vehicle that came or went. Direct Action Minnesota, or DAMN, then dropped license plate numbers into a searchable database while dispatchers relayed sightings over Signal to drivers, who tailed agents across the Twin Cities.
Less than six months later, the Justice Department went after DAMN, charging 15 members and associates of the coalition with conspiracy to impede federal officers. Prosecutors alleged the coalition had ties to antifa and in August revealed via court filings that Homeland Security had been sending undercover agents to activist meetings for months. They got themselves added to group chats, ran protesters’ photos through Clearview AI, and used administrative subpoenas to pull years of bank records on two national labor unions and a youth climate nonprofit.
Activists stood firm. On October 3 protesters marched through Minneapolis calling for the case to be thrown out. So far it hasn’t been, and no one charged has cut a plea deal. —Dell Cameron
For escaping the confines of a sandbox and showing the world how little the people making AI know about the product they are championing.
For being the home of some of the most humorless scolds on the internet—who, when roused, provide some of the biggest laffs around. Never change, Bluesky.
Sorry, OpenAI: 2026 was the year Anthropic’s Claude threatened to eclipse ChatGPT as the world’s hottest AI.
Claude’s remarkable coding skills make it a must-have tool for many developers and vibe coders. These skills inspired the first viral AI assistant, OpenClaw. Some businesses placed limits on employees’ use of Claude after racking up huge usage bills. Anthropic’s model now sits at the top of many leaderboards that measure frontier AI capabilities.
Not everyone is Claude-pilled, of course. In February, the US Department of Defense banned Claude from its systems after the company asked for assurances it would not be used for mass surveillance or autonomous weapons. None of this has stopped Claude’s becoming a money spigot. Anthropic revealed that it generated $16.3 billion in revenue in the first half of 2026 in documents shared ahead of what is likely to be a multitrillion-dollar IPO. —Will Knight
Claude’s nemesis appears to be a stunningly capable open-weight model from China called GLM.
The latest version, GLM-5.3, was released by Z.ai, a Beijing-based AI company, in August. The release took the AI world by surprise because it proved very nearly as powerful as Claude on many benchmarks designed to gauge AI cleverness. The US government’s National Institute for Standards and Technology declared GLM-5.3 to be the best open-weight model in the world for cyber security tasks a month later.
Unlike Claude, GLM can be downloaded and run on one’s own hardware. Anthropic accuses Z.ai and other Chinese companies of routinely running “distillation” campaigns against Claude—that is, using its outputs as training data—when building their own AI. Are open-source Chinese models good enough for US users, or bad enough to be banned? —Will Knight
Muscular haunches. Aggressive Stances. Power bulges. These are the phrases the male-dominated world of car design regularly uses to describe its creations. Tisha Johnson, head of design at Slate Auto, decided to go a different route with the company’s forthcoming electric truck. “We didn’t want [Slate] to be a jerk,” she says. “We rejected an aggressive, dominant look.” Out went giant grilles and hostile postures, in came friendly headlights and a modular design. The result? More than 180,000 reservations as well as industry design awards for the $25,000 EV scheduled to hit the road as early as this year. There’s one particular buyer Johnson hopes to satisfy: a single parent with two kids. She has spent decades striving to design something that could make their lives easier. Now, it’s about to be a reality. “It’s very emotional to think about,” she says.  —Jeremy White
Few maladies strike fear like pancreatic cancer. It’s a brutally lethal disease; the vast majority of those diagnosed won’t live more than five years. Though it’s a relatively rare form of cancer, accounting for around 3 percent of annual diagnoses in the United States, it’s the third-highest driver of cancer deaths.
But in 2026, a new treatment from the California-based biotech company Revolution Medicines offered a dose of hope. Rolled out in August under the brand name Rasonque, daraxonrasib is an oral therapy that works by inhibiting the signaling activity of a specific protein called RAS, mutations of which are responsible for the vast majority of pancreatic cancer cases. When the lead investigator on daraxonrasib’s clinical trial presented his results at an oncology conference, he received a standing ovation.
While not a cure by any means, the treatment has nearly doubled median survival length in previously treated patients compared to traditional chemotherapy. In October, the European Union’s medical authority recommended that Revolution Medicines be allowed to market Rasonque in Europe. If it goes through, far more patients could benefit from the drug. Hopefully. —Kate Knibbs
When Will Freeman started DeFlock in 2024, Flock Safety was barely known outside law enforcement, even as its network of roadside cameras quietly expanded across the country. Two years later, DeFlock has emerged as one of the most visible groups organizing opposition to the surveillance company.
DeFlock began as a crowdsourced map of automated license-plate readers, or ALPRs, submitted by volunteers. By September 2026, the map listed more than 128,000 readers across the US. Activists organized independently around the project, with some groups adopting the DeFlock name. DeFlock started publishing guides, and in August it organized a National Week of Action Against ALPRs.
Residents across the US began pressing local officials to reconsider Flock contracts. In San Diego, one opponent even showed up to a city council meeting dressed as Darth Vader, praising Flock as a tool for tracking rebel scum. More than 100 local governments paused or ended Flock contracts in 2026, according to a DeFlock tally reported by The Wall Street Journal.
Authorities have taken notice. In early 2026, two officers on a law enforcement mailing list warned that DeFlock could help suspects locate and avoid ALPRs and discussed sabotaging the project with fake camera locations. Fusion centers in several states circulated intelligence about anti-Flock activism. Flock, meanwhile, is offering employee buyouts and has reportedly held discussions about selling itself. CEO Garrett Langley, who famously called DeFlock a “terroristic organization,” apologized this summer and acknowledged that the group had valid criticisms. Maybe Flock can finally start reading the room. —Dhruv Mehrotra
Useful quantum computing remains years away, but that hasn’t stopped companies from raising billions of dollars on the promise that their technology is the next megatrend after AI. The number of publicly listed firms more than doubled in 2026. Even the Trump administration got in on the hype, taking $2 billion in equity stakes across nine companies. Will the boom last? Tougher economic conditions, coupled with worries about an AI bubble, have made big bets look less appealing—and there’s still the pesky fact that the tech doesn’t, ya know, really work. —Isabella Ward
Has Erin Brockovich done anything of note since being played by Julia Roberts, to Oscar-winning acclaim, in 2000? Nothing quite as high-profile, but she wrote some books, got involved in a number of other big environmental cases, and generally continued to fight the power. Still, nothing has thrust Brockovich back in the spotlight quite like the nationwide fight against data centers. In April, she started a website to crowdsource information about data center construction across the country. She received nearly 4,000 reports in the first month. It’s not so surprising: With polls showing 75 percent of Americans don’t want a data center in their backyard, it’s the closest thing the US has to a truly bipartisan issue. Now that Brockovich has become a kind of national spokesperson for the movement, Julia Roberts might just win her second Oscar—for Erin Brockovich 2. —Molly Taft
He drinks 14 cups of coffee per day, but Rafael Concepcion has still dealt with plenty of fatigue over the past year. Best known as the creator of DEICER, an app designed to alert users to local ICE activity, the 52-year-old was juggling multiple coding projects when WIRED profiled him in March. He was also reeling from setbacks that had pushed him to the edge of burnout. The most prominent was Apple’s decision to expel DEICER from the App Store on the grounds that it endangered law enforcement officers; despite numerous appeals by Concepcion, Apple refused to budge. A politically motivated hacking in February made him fear for his personal safety, his financial straits grew dire enough that he had to ration his medications, and his longtime marriage ended right as his daughter was preparing to leave for college.
“I could see myself completely losing it,” he admits, describing the path he was on as “unsustainable.” But instead of retreating from the fight against ICE, Concepcion reprioritized how he could stay involved. He devoted fewer hours to coding and more to volunteering in his hometown of Syracuse, New York, where ICE raids have caused pervasive fear. He has been helping immigrants fill out power-of-attorney documents so that family members can retrieve their vehicles from impound lots if they’re ever taken by ICE. He also assists people who are attempting to contact loved ones in detention centers. When Concepcion and I spoke in October, he was trying to find a lawyer for a close friend’s brother, who’d been taken by ICE just three days earlier.
While Concepcion is coding less, he hasn’t given it up. He tweaked DEICER into a New York–specific web app called Empire Watch. He also rolled out Vote Defender, an app to disseminate citizen reports of potential problems at polling places. Concepcion is already dreaming big about the year ahead. He’s developing an app to help people study for the US citizenship test. And he’s planning to get a notary license. “I’m just trying to live my values on a person-to-person basis,” he says. —Brendan I. Koerner
Magnificent Humanity. Doesn’t feel that way all the time, does it? But that’s how noted Chicago White Sox fan and former math major Robert Francis Prevost titled his first encyclical as Pope: Magnifica Humanitas. Where the previous Pope Leo warned against the dehumanizing power of industrialization at the turn of the 20th century, this Leo is devoted to “safeguarding the human person in the time of artificial intelligence.”
In the 42,000-word encyclical, the Pope acknowledges that “AI is already an environment in which we are immersed.” Invoking the Tower of Babel, he says humanity must reject “the pretense that a single language” can translate everything into “data and performance.” In the eyes of the Holy See, regulation alone isn’t enough. AI must be “disarmed,” Leo writes. Not by rejecting the technology but by “preventing it from dominating humanity.” And on the question of AI sentience, he’s very clear: “So-called artificial intelligences do not undergo experiences.” They don’t “feel joy or pain,” and they don’t “have a moral conscience.”
That question was reportedly a sticking point in behind-the-scenes talks between the Vatican and the AI industry. Upon presenting the encyclical in Rome, Pope Leo invited Chris Olah, an Anthropic cofounder, to speak. Olah said AI systems aren’t “engineered”—they’re “grown.” They’re “not the cold, calculating robots we were promised,” he went on. “They are made from us, from our words.”
OK, if that’s how you feel. But the AI advice Pope Leo XIV gave a Honolulu high schooler in 2025 still rings true: “Use it in such a way that if it disappeared tomorrow, you would still know how to think.” —Anthony Lydgate
“Diarrhea lettuce,” anyone? Of all the slang and shorthand to arrive this year, that might be the nastiest. The term started circulating in July, when a previously little-known parasite, cyclospora, began popping up in salads across the US. Officially, the outbreak—much of it traced back to iceberg lettuce grown in Mexico—sickened nearly 13,000 people and hospitalized 570, but it likely afflicted many more. The news swept social media and, for a time, changed the public’s eating habits. Cyclospora’s surge also demonstrated the fragility of the US food system at a time when the country’s public health infrastructure was undergoing massive cuts. —Emily Mullin
For finally leaving France after more than 900 years—and taking out $1 billion in trip insurance.
Want to take down an online black market? Call Tom Robinson. Cofounder of crypto-tracing firm Elliptic, he’s had the operations in his sights for a couple of years. His most recent target was Xinbi Guarantee, a $24 billion Chinese-language market that sells scammers the tools they need—fake social media accounts, money-laundering services—to conduct mass-scale digital fraud. Robinson’s sleuthing helped the US Secret Service freeze cryptocurrency wallets containing more than $50 million in funds linked to Xinbi Guarantee. The marketplace has also been sanctioned and forced offline. —Matt Burgess
For its unforgettable celebrity weddings and always-watching Eye of Sauron surveillance apparatus.
After collecting his Nobel Prize in Stockholm late last year, Omar Yaghi returned to the United States in a restless mood. The 61-year-old chemist was thrilled to have been recognized for his pioneering work on metal-organic frameworks, crystalline structures that are built from a combination of metal ions and carbon-based molecules. And he was excited that his startup, Atoco, was testing a machine that uses these frameworks to harvest water from the atmosphere, an innovation that could completely transform the world’s driest regions. But Yaghi also feared he’d hit a dead end at UC Berkeley, where he’d been a professor since 2012. In addition to feeling constrained by the school’s bureaucracy, he was frustrated by how hard it had become to secure visas for gifted foreign students, the lifeblood of his lab. As an immigrant himself, Yaghi was troubled by the Trump administration’s virulent nativism.
“The rhetoric about immigrants is not helping good people do their job,” says Yaghi, a naturalized American citizen originally from Jordan. “I must say that for the first time in my life, I felt that I didn't have protection of the law.” So 45 years after he first came to the US as a teenager who barely spoke a word of English, Yaghi decided to find a new country in which to pursue his goal of “solving ambitious problems.”
Germany was at the top of Yaghi’s list, but none of the universities there seemed willing to provide him with the resources he wanted. He was instead lured to China: In July, Tsinghua University appointed him to lead a new institute devoted to using AI to accelerate chemical research. Yaghi says he was drawn to the opportunity because he’s a firm believer in AI’s potential to “shorten the time between discovery and commercialization.” Having spent a quarter century perfecting his water-harvesting technology, largely through arduous trial and error, he is optimistic that AI can help him get his next invention to market in a fraction of that time.
Yaghi’s move to Tsinghua was viewed as a harbinger of China’s scientific rise. And after just a few months on the school’s Beijing campus, Yaghi says he has been impressed by his colleagues’ brilliance and drive. “I feel like my scientific environment wants to get better, wants to improve, wants to move forward,” he says. “The attitude is more of a let's-get-it-done type.” Atoco is now about to start selling its first water-harvesting system, capable of producing up to 1,000 gallons per day in optimal conditions. The company is also developing machines to capture atmospheric carbon, a key to slowing the pace of climate change. These technologies use relatively little energy because Yaghi’s frameworks bond with carbon-dioxide molecules.
Yaghi still has great affection for the US, where he started his academic journey at a New York community college in the 1980s. He hopes the country will awaken to the need to protect the innovation system it built over many decades—a system he describes even now as “the envy of the world.” Science “is part of our civilization, and we should not take attacks on science very lightly,” he says. “It is something that society needs to value.” —Brendan I. Koerner
In April, a four-person crew took a 10-day trip that circumnavigated the moon. They came back to a world with a renewed dream of space travel.
Four years ago, Annie Kritcher achieved what, for many, would be the scientific accomplishment of a lifetime: producing net energy from a fusion reaction for the first time in history. Her wickedly cool method? Blasting a tiny target with a laser.
Her next feat may be even more awesome. She wants to use that technology to generate abundant power for all. In February, Inertia Enterprises, where Kritcher is chief scientist and cofounder, announced it had raised $450 million in Series A funding. By July, the company had opened its own facility just down the road from Lawrence Livermore National Lab, where Kritcher made fusion history and still holds an appointment.
The startup also laid out 10 milestones to track its progress and already hit two of them. Next on Inertia’s list is developing a better laser. The one Kritcher fired at Lawrence Livermore used optical fibers. Now Inertia is looking to use diodes, which are vastly more efficient. As Kritcher puts it, “If you don’t have a big laser to fire, you can’t do cool physics things.” Amen.
Inertia’s goal is to have a beam-line laser prototype—dubbed Thunderwall—ready by 2030. Kritcher refuses to pick which of their next milestones will be the most important. “They’re my little children,” she quips. She says the work has been energizing for her and her colleagues: “You want to take it all the way to the end.” Until then, Kritcher is laser focused. —Brian Kahn
Once upon a time, photos of cats with grammatically garbled captions were enough to make us smile, and a low-speed chase involving a pair of escaped llamas kept people glued to Twitter. Was the internet ever so innocent? Thank god, then, for Jimothy. In July, accidental nature documentarian Kiana Hall posted an Instagram video of a Seattle-area raccoon with a stunted spine, bunched tail, and cryptid gait. The footage of Jimothy scampering over a lawn and up a set of stairs went megaviral.
Jimothy became a local mascot overnight, the subject of copious fan art and a Seattle Mariners theme night. Millions of online admirers were even more charmed when earlier footage of Jimothy as a young kit, with mom and siblings, made the rounds. By then, Hot Jimothy Summer—just what the internet needed—was in full swing.
Biologists cautioned people not to feed or interact with Jimothy; they also said that without a direct examination, it was impossible to know just what kind of spinal condition the critter has. Nevertheless, they told WIRED, it seemed the raccoon had adjusted reasonably well to its unusual frame—a welcome reminder that being a little different needn’t hold anyone back. —Miles Klee
For taking AI notes straight from a phone, no meeting-bot required.
For offering publicly to buy WIRED in order to improve it. Thanks, Trae, but we aren’t for sale.
Whether you’re trying to shield your calls and texts from government surveillance, understand how AI image-recognition systems work, or remove all of your pics from Google Photos, Cyberlixir has tips for you. Started by Đanacea Võ in 2023, the organization makes Instagram videos and other digital content designed to give anyone, regardless of their technical proficiency, the privacy and security resources they need.
Võ spends her days as an operational security consultant for activists and nonprofits, especially those who face elevated digital threats. As a Vietnamese immigrant to the US, Võ says her work with human rights defenders—including environmental-, indigenous-, and immigrant-focused groups—has helped shape her content. She incorporates feedback and suggestions from viewers too.
Cyberelixir posts frequently amass thousands of shares on Instagram. Võ plans to keep growing the project by improving the website and traveling across the US for meetups. “If we want technology for good in the long term,” she says, “we need mass social movements, getting more people involved, and building communities.” —Lily Hay Newman

Jensen Huang's Nvidia exit just got a little more real on X.
The former CEO's X account now identifies him only as the ""Founder of NVIDIA."" Before the late Monday profile overhaul, it also said ""CEO.""
The makeover did not stop at three missing letters.
Huang's once-green Nvidia banner has been replaced with a plain gray one. His Nvidia affiliation badge also vanished, as has a link to his Nvidia biography.
Huang also lost his blue check verification badge — but it didn't last too long. 
""Blue check coming back for free on the house,"" wrote Elon Musk, the CEO of SpaceX — which owns X — in a reply to a post on the site about Huang's disappearing verified badge.
By Tuesday morning, Huang's blue check was back.
📝 Bio change detected: @jensenhuang pic.twitter.com/CJG1dfOglV
A blue check on X costs $8 a month, but Nvidia's green affiliation badge would require the company to pay $1,000 a month for X Premium Business's Full Access tier, plus $50 a month for each affiliated account.
Huang is worth $136.2 billion, according to Forbes, a figure that has dropped significantly since he stepped down after an extramarital affair scandal and a board investigation into whether he was the target of a foreign intelligence effort. 

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Huang's LinkedIn profile, however, remains unchanged: it still lists him as ""founder and CEO"" of Nvidia. 
The X profile changes come as a growing number of Huang's supporters call for him to be reinstated.
More than 191,000 people had signed the ""Stand With Jensen"" petition as of Tuesday morning, according to the campaign's website.
The drive has attracted support from Musk, former Uber CEO Travis Kalanick, and Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan, as well as other business leaders, investors, and Nvidia employees.
But Silicon Valley is hardly united behind a Huang comeback.
While some supporters argue that the board should complete its investigation before permanently replacing the founder, others say the push to restore him reflects the industry's tendency to excuse powerful men.
Nvidia's board is not considering reinstating Huang as CEO, a person familiar with the matter told Business Insider on Monday, as it investigates whether Huang had been the target of a foreign-intelligence effort.
Dan Ives, a senior managing director at Yorkville Ives, said Sunday that he saw ""no chance"" Huang would return as Nvidia CEO, while leadership experts told Business Insider on Monday that a comeback was unlikely in the near term.
The uncertainty is also weighing on Nvidia's stock ahead of its closely watched earnings on Monday afternoon. Shares closed down 4.1% at $156.14 on Monday.
Huang resigned last Thursday after a fast-moving series of revelations. He acknowledged in a memo to employees that he had been unfaithful to his wife.
The New York Times reported that he had relationships with Alice Huang, a former Singapore-based Megaspeed executive who US officials suspected of passing information to Beijing, and Nvidia executive Kimberly Powell, who reported directly to him.
Whether the changes to Huang's X profile are a message or merely housekeeping, Nvidia has begun planning for a future without him at the helm.
""There is a world after Jensen, and there is still so much progress to be made,"" Lior Susan, the founder and CEO of venture firm Eclipse, told Business Insider on Monday.

28,2026-11-17,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Karly Wrede, whose daughter created Bee Kind Slimes. It has been edited for length and clarity.
Two years ago, my daughter Nora wanted to buy something. She asked my husband and me for money. We gave her a list of chores she could do, and she asked us if she could sell her slime instead. She's always been a fan of making homemade slime.
So she packed it all into a tote bag and started walking door to door in our neighborhood. We walked behind her. I was worried she wouldn't sell anything and thought we'd have to console her, but she came back 30 minutes later and had made $100.
My husband and I were both like, ""Wow, maybe this is a thing."" And that's really when Bee Kind Slimes was born.
At first, people bought from her because she was a sweet little girl at their doorstep, but then she asked if she could sell on social media. It kind of went from there - someone reached out and asked if we wanted to do a farmer's market; we got asked to do a holiday show; Nora started to sell her slime online, and she started doing birthday parties. Now we even teach other families how to start a slime business.
We've just sort of kept saying yes, and it's been amazing to see what she can do in two years.
At first, we were selling individual containers of slime, but it goes bad if you let it sit for too long. After a farmers market or show, we spent a lot of time emptying containers, reactivating the slime, and putting it all back in. I saw a collapsible charcuterie cart on Facebook Marketplace one day, and it gave us the idea to design a slime cart.
Now we can wheel it in and out of events and parties, and it's more of an experience. Kids love picking their own slime color and can choose charms and fake sprinkles to mix in. Every slime is unique, which makes it really fun and less work for us.
Bee Kind Slimes is a part-time job for Nora at this point, involving making, packaging, and selling slime. Some days she takes the slime cart to back-to-back birthday parties. She's done 50 birthday parties this year. Right now, she works on the business about 15 hours a week, but during the holidays that will increase.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
To make the slime, we tried making batches in a KitchenAid mixer at first, but they didn't work well. We went through five mixers in the first year. Nora applied for a grant through the Mason Makes Money Fund and received it, so we used the funds to buy an industrial mixer. Now she can make 10 times as much slime as she could in a small batch.
She's still a kid, though. A big part of this is figuring out how to balance helping her learn and grow without burning her out, because this is something we get to do together for fun.
Before she started the business, Nora got $10 in allowance a week. She'd instantly go to the store to buy a toy.
Now she gets paid $100 each month, but has to wait until the end of the month to get the money. It's really made her think about what she wants to spend her money on, and be proactive about thinking about saving. Now there are times when she doesn't spend much, or any, of her allowance.
I work for a nonprofit, and I also wanted to help her learn how to be generous, which she's been able to do in a variety of different ways. Her confidence has also really grown.
It's never really been about the slime for me. It's been about how I can teach her things she'll carry with her for the rest of her life. If this business is something that helps Nora in some way, be ready to be an adult, and helps her learn kindness, it's all worth it.
We were getting a dozen messages a week on social media from other moms asking how to start a slime business. I teach classes at my corporate job, and leading cohorts comes really naturally to me, so I put together a course with everything someone might need.
Since April of this year, Nora and I have taught over 130 parents and their kids how to run their own slime businesses.
Two years ago, I was making slime with her on the kitchen counter, and it's really fun to think about how much the business has grown. We get to spend a lot of time together, and hopefully she wants to keep making slime.

30,2026-11-17,"Key Apple memory supplier SK Hynix has warned that RAM and flash memory shortages are likely to last through at least 2030, keeping product prices high. Apple is believed to be one of 14 customers who have locked in priority orders, but the contract does not protect against price increases related to the balance of supply and demand.
Update: Apple is reportedly about to sign a similar contract with Samsung, also permitting market-driven price increases.
SK Hynix says that while AI continues to drive demand and growth, there is little that can be done in the short to medium term to increase supply.
Supply, on the other hand, is not keeping pace with the growth of the market. Given technical difficulty of leading-edge processes and the long lead time required to build new production facilities, it expects the structure in which demand exceeds supply to persist through 2030.
The Register reports that SK Hynix has long-term deals with 14 tech giants for priority orders, but these allow for market-based price increases.
14 big buyers […] will collectively buy more than half of its output during 2027 and 2028, and some of them want to extend those agreements. The Korean giant doesn’t mind that because the deals include “a mechanism that reflects market conditions within a price range agreed upon with customers”
Apple is used to being in the driving seat when it comes to negotiating with suppliers, but this is not the case where memory and flash storage is concerned. Here, the shortages mean that it will have been forced to agree to contracts that allow significant price hikes in order to obtain guaranteed supplies.
Nikkei Asia also reports that printed circuit boards may be the next component to experience constrained supply. Once a humble commodity item, advances in the technology has turned them into specialist tech, with more layers and higher bandwidth requirements. The report says “that complexity shrinks the supplier pool and extends lead times.”
Apple said back in June that the company was doing its best to mitigate the price increases, but it has so far been unable to do so.

                                    I tested iPhone Duo, Galaxy Z Fold 8, and Xiaomi 18 Fold cameras in Dubai – and the zoom gap is unreal
                                

                                    ‘It just looks like R2-D2’ — This new gaming phone offers flagship specs and PC game emulation at a killer price, but its design is something else
                                
Get the latest news from Android Central, your trusted companion in the world of Android

High-end specs and a cool rear display

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

After we missed out on the Xiaomi 17 Pro and Pro Max last year, the Chinese smartphone giant is finally bringing its dual-screen flagships to the West. It's just launched the new Xiaomi 18 Pro and 18 Pro Max, and I would be pretty worried if I were a certain Cupertino-based competitor.
Both phones look like an all-round excellent package, with top-of-the-line specs including the brand-new Snapdragon 8 Elite chipsets.
The Xiaomi 18 Pro is the model for those after a more compact, but still powerful, phone, with its 6.4-inch 120Hz AMOLED front display and 6,000mAh battery. It's powered by the Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 6 chipset, paired with 12GB of RAM and either 256GB or 512GB of storage.
The Xiaomi 18 Pro Max, meanwhile, bumps the main screen size up to 6.9 inches and upgrades the chipset to the absolutely top-of-the-line Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6 – currently the fastest Android SoC on the market. The base model offers 12GB of RAM and 256GB of storage, but there are also more expensive 12+512GB and 16+1TB versions available.
The bigger design also makes space for a much larger battery, which comes in at 7,050mAh with 100W wired HyperCharge support (the base 18 Pro, in contrast, only supports 90W wired HyperCharge).
The front displays of both phones support Xiaomi's new privacy display technology, which is effectively the company's take on Samsung's Privacy Display feature and helps obscure sensitive information from prying eyes.
Both the 18 Pro and 18 Pro Max boast similar camera setups, with a 200MP main shooter and 200MP periscope telephoto lens alongside a 50MP ultra-wide. As with other recent Xiaomi devices, the cameras were co-engineered with Leica and offer a range of photo styles inspired by the camera maker.
Get the latest news from Android Central, your trusted companion in the world of Android
For me, the real highlight, however, is the innovative rear display situated on the back of both models. It offers a range of handy features, from controlling media playback to offering a real-time view of the cameras to make selfies easier.
You can fully customize it with a range of watch-like faces as well (including some options with cute 3D animal mascots) and even use generative AI to create your own apps right from the settings menu.
It's seriously cool and seems like a genuine reason to pick the 18 Pro or 18 Pro Max over other flagship models.
All of this doesn't exactly come cheap, however. The Xiaomi 18 Pro starts at £1,199 (that's roughly $1,600), and the 18 Pro Max starts at £1,399 (around $1,850) for the 12+256GB model.
They both come in four color options: Black, White, Velvet Red, and Cosmic Blue.
Dash is the Editor at Android Central, where he oversees coverage of everything from the latest smartphones and tablets to wearables and Googlebook laptops. He was previously the Gaming Editor at TechRadar, and his writing has appeared in some of the UK's biggest magazines, including Edge, PC Gamer, SFX, and PLAY. He has a particular interest in the Chinese phone market and is devoutly wedded to the latest Honor flagship.

                                    Samsung Galaxy S27 Ultra's major camera downgrade is almost confirmed
                                

                                    Tiny but mighty: these are the 6 BEST small phones to buy in 2026
                                

                                    The best Android phones with an SD card slot 2026

The big question is: how much does this matter?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Motorola went all-in on premium this year with the Signature 27. While many consumers eagerly awaited this phone, some won't see it at its full potential.
Certain specs must be met before companies (or rather, consumers) can slap a premium label on a phone. The Motorola Signature 27 focuses on key areas: its display, camera, chipset, and battery. It's the latter—its battery—that's come under scrutiny in one market. According to Android Authority, the Motorola Signature 27's battery will suffer a hit overseas.
Reports have conflicted with the Signature 27's original 7,000mAh battery announcement. The publication highlighted this, then reached out to Motorola for clarification. A spokesperson responded, stating the device ""will feature a different battery configuration in Germany."" As a result, it looks like German consumers will find the Signature 27 with a 5,500mAh cell, not 7,000mAh.
This is a sharp 1,500mAh drop, but Motorola says it had no choice.
In its statement, Motorola told the publication that it had to drop the phone's battery to ""comply with local regulatory requirements related to lithium batteries."" The report adds that this huge battery cut is worth roughly 17 hours of battery time (~61 hours vs 77 hours).
 



Strangely, a report claimed U.K. consumers might've suffered from this battery cut, too. Apparently, the Signature 27 was listed with a 5,500mAh battery; however, it's reportedly since been updated to the higher figure.
It's unclear how this might affect German consumers who were interested in the Signature 27. The battery (right now) is the only aspect of the phone that's suffering from country-specific regulations. During launch, the phone was announced with an incredibly bright 6.8-inch Extreme AMOLED Duo display with 12,000 nits. Motorola stuck to its premium promise and delivered Qualcomm's Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6 SoC (a first for the brand).
Get the latest news from Android Central, your trusted companion in the world of Android
For mobile gamers, the company integrated its ArcticMesh cooling system, increasing its size by 40% to help keep temperatures lower during longer sessions.
Would something like this matter to you? A reduction from 7,000mAh to 5,500 mAh, I mean.
Here's why I ask—and I'll play both sides here. Motorola wanted to release a phone that firmly leaned into ""premium."" One could say it did. However, if you look at Samsung and its Galaxy S26 Ultra (S25 Ultra... S24 Ultra...), it features a 5,000mAh battery. The Korean OEM has kept this exact same 5,000mAh cell for a while, and yet it's still premium. Premium tag, premium price, everything. Of course, Motorola's sharp reduction will cut down how long users can keep scrolling, but it's still more than Samsung's latest Ultra.
Nickolas is always excited about tech and getting his hands on it. Writing for him can vary from delivering the latest tech story to scribbling in his journal. When Nickolas isn't hitting a story, he's often grinding away at a game or chilling with a book in his hand.
