34,2026-11-17,"Former Nvidia (NVDA) CEO Jensen Huang says he would be ""honored"" to return as chief executive of the company he cofounded.In a statement Tuesday, Huang apologized to his family again and commented on the support he's seen among former employees, customers, an…"
35,2026-11-17,"""The Social Reckoning"" is finally hitting the home market. It will become available to buy or rent on digital starting November 24, 2026, prior to its physical media release on January 12, 2027.
In case you're unfamiliar with ""The Social Reckoning"" writ large, here's the gist. Almost two decades after he won an Academy Award for writing the screenplay for ""The Social Network,"" a 2010 dramatization of the founding of Facebook directed by David Fincher, Aaron Sorkin decided to make a sequel that focuses on what Facebook has become in the 2020s. Jesse Eisenberg, who earned an Oscar nod for playing Facebook founder Mark Zuckerberg in ""The Social Network,"" declined to return to the role and was replaced by ""Succession"" veteran, Emmy and Tony winner, and Sorkin's ""The Trial of the Chicago 7"" collaborator Jeremy Strong. With Mikey Madison, Jeremy Allen White, Bill Burr, Wunmi Mosaku, Betty Gilpin, and Billy Magnussen starring alongside Strong, the project seemed almost too big to fail before it premiered in theaters on October 9, 2026.
Fail it did, though. Though it reportedly only lost a couple million for Sony (per Deadline), the film absolutely underperformed at the box office, and its rating on Rotten Tomatoes is a middling-to-bleak 54%. So what happened here? Well, from my perspective, ""The Social Reckoning"" is just a bad movie ... and /Film's chief film critic Chris Evangelista agrees with me. What went wrong as Sorkin tried to make a legacy sequel to one of the best films of the 21st century? Quite a lot actually!
I'll circle back to the ""Social Network"" of it all shortly, but the bottom line here is that ""The Social Reckoning"" just ... stinks to high heaven. From the embarrassing prologue featuring Portia Doubleday as a caricature named Kayleigh (I'm not kidding) to the fact that Aaron Sorkin keeps forcing Oscar winner Mikey Madison into outfits that would make her title character from ""Anora"" blush (in the name of corporate espionage), I found ""The Social Network"" to be a uniformly dreadful watch from start to finish. Not only is the script clunky as all hell — unfortunately, an ongoing problem with many of Sorkin's recent screenplays — but the story is attempting to make some sort of larger point that doesn't just fall flat but thuds directly to the floor. We already know social media makes everyone angry and insane, and we also know that social media has contributed to right-wing radicalization. Sorkin making a movie that beats us over the head with these two ideas and also ends in a full recreation of the January 6, 2021 insurrection is just as bad as it sounds in theory.
It's also incredible to me that Sorkin managed to cast a lot of great actors and coach them into giving some of the worst performances of their careers. Madison is an enigmatic and compelling performer, and Sorkin transforms her into the robotic Facebook whistleblower Frances Haugen, a deeply unlikable person who doesn't seem to understand human behavior. Jeremy Allen White's journalist Jeff Horwitz is nothing but an exposition machine, and Jeremy Strong's Mark Zuckerberg has no interiority and sounds like Kermit the Frog. The only person who's good in this movie is Bill Burr. Think about that for a second.
""The Social Network"" might be one of my very favorite movies of all time, but I can tell you, from a completely unbiased perspective, that ""The Social Reckoning"" is a wholly undeserving sequel to that 2010 masterpiece. It's clear now, with plenty of hindsight and movies like this, that ""The Social Network"" was good because of director David Fincher and despite Sorkin. To be fair to Sorkin, he basically admitted that to The Hollywood Reporter after ""The Social Reckoning"" got soundly panned.
""I would have rather David Fincher directed it — I would rather David Fincher direct everything — but David has an exclusive deal at Netflix, and this was owned by Sony,"" Sorkin told the outlet on their ""Awards Chatter"" podcast. Not only did Sorkin also admit that he's ""very worried that the parts of directing that [he's] not good at are those parts that you can't learn,"" he also said, ""I was surprised by how little of the science of filmmaking I'd managed to absorb [from other directors] ... I have no visual sensibility, it turns out.""
I have to agree with him. Despite the same cinematographer — Jeff Cronenweth — working on both ""Network"" and ""Reckoning,"" the sequel is visually bland, unimaginatively shot, and even recycles a couple reaction and establishing shots throughout its run time. Basically, ""The Social Reckoning"" is a poorly made film that I cannot in good conscience recommend; in fact, I'd recommend you just watch ""The Social Network"" instead. But if you're still interested, you can check out ""The Social Reckoning"" at home once it's available."
36,2026-11-17,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
Clipping turns your podcasts and keynotes into hundreds of short-form posts across social media, and it’s one of the most cost-efficient marketing channels most brands still aren’t using. 


		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
Nobody wants to sit through a two-hour keynote anymore. I don’t, and I’m guessing you don’t either. What people will watch is the 40-second clip of the one moment that mattered. That shift in how we consume content is why clipping has become one of the most cost-efficient ways to market a brand right now. It’s also one of the most misunderstood.
Clipping started in streaming about three years ago. Music picked it up next, the way music picks up every new marketing method first. Then TV and film distributors got into it, then direct-to-consumer brands, and now B2B companies, including some of the biggest names in fintech and enterprise software. Even so, most marketing teams I talk to still aren’t sure what it is or where it fits.
Most people already understand influencers and UGC creators. They use their own face and record their own content. A clipper is different. A clipper runs their own social media page on Instagram, YouTube, TikTok or X, but instead of making something from scratch, they take content that already exists, pick the moment they think will hit and cut it down into a short clip for their page. The content is already there. The clipper’s job is getting it seen.
So as a brand, you hand over your long-form content. Maybe that’s an hour-long podcast episode or a conference keynote. Clippers cut it into short pieces, post them on their own accounts and get paid based on views. Right now that’s usually somewhere between $1 and $5 for every thousand views.
When you scroll social media, around 10% of what you see is an ad, and it’s labeled as an ad. The other 90% is organic content. You can run ads, but that only gets you a small slice of the time people spend on these apps. If you want the rest, you need organic content and a lot of it.
That’s where clipping comes in. Instead of deciding in a conference room which moment from that podcast will hit, you put the content in the hands of clippers, each of whom picks their own moments. You end up with hundreds of different cuts in the span of four to eight hours, and the algorithm decides what works best. That’s A/B testing at a speed no internal team can match.
Clipping can’t save bad content. If people don’t want to watch it, putting it in front of more of them doesn’t help.
The easiest way to figure out what good content looks like is to go see what your competitors and the brands you look up to are posting. Then mimic it and improve it.
When you do that research, don’t trust third-party data. Don’t go on ChatGPT and ask what the top people in your industry are making content about, because then you end up making the exact same content everybody else is posting. You need first-party data. Go on YouTube, search it up and look with your own eyes. If you do it, you’re already doing what everybody else isn’t, and that’s an edge.
People don’t purchase from brands. They purchase from people. So either you become the ambassador for your brand, or you put the right ambassadors in front of it and have them help you make the content.
That’s why I tell brands not to stop doing UGC just because they started clipping. Keep doing UGC. And instead of only paying for short-form clips, pay someone in your space who has real authority to make a long-form video, maybe 10 or 15 minutes. Then feed that into your clipping campaigns too.
Keep it raw. People don’t want the perfect, super official, branded clip. They want something that looks organic and doesn’t feel like it’s trying to sell them something every second. People hate being sold to.
Before you launch a campaign, decide what it’s for. Awareness and conversion are different campaigns. For most brands, clipping is about awareness. It gets more people hearing about you, and it’s one more touchpoint in the mix alongside your ads, your creators and your influencers.
Once you see clips working, milk them. I’ve had clients tell me they’re seeing the same clip posted too many times and they want more variation. Think about it like ads. If you’ve tested 10 different creatives and one is doing really well, do you stop it and go make more variations? No. You keep putting money behind it. With clipping, you double down by having more accounts post that same clip. That’s how you get that feeling of being everywhere, so don’t be afraid of it.
Not every clipping partner is the same, and the wrong one can hurt your brand. Here’s what I’d look for.
Attention spans keep going down; everyone is competing for the same eyeballs, and CPMs on ads are only going up. Clipping won’t replace the rest of your marketing, but it’s one of the most cost-efficient ways to get your best moments in front of a lot more people. Right now, that’s an arbitrage opportunity most brands are still sleeping on.
Nobody wants to sit through a two-hour keynote anymore. I don’t, and I’m guessing you don’t either. What people will watch is the 40-second clip of the one moment that mattered. That shift in how we consume content is why clipping has become one of the most cost-efficient ways to market a brand right now. It’s also one of the most misunderstood.
Clipping started in streaming about three years ago. Music picked it up next, the way music picks up every new marketing method first. Then TV and film distributors got into it, then direct-to-consumer brands, and now B2B companies, including some of the biggest names in fintech and enterprise software. Even so, most marketing teams I talk to still aren’t sure what it is or where it fits.
Most people already understand influencers and UGC creators. They use their own face and record their own content. A clipper is different. A clipper runs their own social media page on Instagram, YouTube, TikTok or X, but instead of making something from scratch, they take content that already exists, pick the moment they think will hit and cut it down into a short clip for their page. The content is already there. The clipper’s job is getting it seen.

Nvidia‘s board of directors “does not intend” to reinstate Jensen Huang as CEO, its board chair said during the company’s earnings call on Tuesday, hours after the co-founder said he’d be “honored” to come back following his resignation last week over a series of affairs.

	“Jensen Huang voluntarily resigned his positions as president, CEO, and board member,” Harvey C. Jones said during the company’s third-quarter 2027 fiscal year earnings call on Tuesday. “The Nvidia board unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO.”

	Jones’ remark came after Huang released his first public statement since his resignation, revealing he’d be open to returning to the company he co-founded in 1993 at the board’s discretion.

	“Since my departure, I’ve seen an outpouring of calls from Nvidia’s employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO,” he wrote in a statement, which he posted to X. “Many continue to reach out to me directly as the company navigates complex technology, market, competitive, and geopolitical dynamics. Nvidia is a once-in-a-generation company. If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.”

	The statement was first issued to the Wall Street Journal, which posted its story around 10:55 a.m. ET. Nvidia’s stock price at the time hovered at $150.09, though it shot up to $155.36 by 11:15 a.m. ET. Nvidia reported its third-quarter earnings for its 2027 fiscal year on Tuesday, beating Wall Street expectations with $115.8 billion in revenue and $65.3 billion in profit.

	










	The company’s board of directors said on Thursday that Huang had resigned over the cascade of scandals that arose from his affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy and reportedly two others with Nvidia employees. Colette Kress, Nvidia’s chief financial officer and executive vice president, stepped in as interim CEO as the board began a search for a permanent replacement.

	The board hired Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to lead an independent inquiry into the relationship with the alleged spy, which is coinciding with an FBI investigation. In a statement last week, the board thanked Huang for “his immeasurable contributions to the company and the industry.”

	Jones and Kress did not discuss in detail the nature of Huang’s resignation or plans for the company’s future following his departure, though Kress said the company would focus on its employees and customers during the transition. Jones also said the search for Huang’s will be “thorough,” considering both internal and external candidates.

	“From a business perspective, it’s business as usual,” Kress said.

	The reported affairs with company employees include one with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia’s vice president of health care, and a second unnamed employee. Nvidia’s code of conduct prohibits employees from “situations where our actions might create a conflict — actual or potential—between our personal gain and our obligations” to the company. It also advises that “personal relationships” may pose a potential conflict.

	 Huang, who had led the company as CEO since he cofounded it in 1993, said on Tuesday he was “committed to cooperating” with the board’s investigation.

	“I did not live up to the standards expected of everyone at Nvidia, and I have made serious mistakes in my personal life,” he said. “I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret. I own my mistakes completely.”

38,2026-11-17,"In 2024, Anker's sub-brand Eufy launched the Omni S1 Pro robot vacuum, which was the best that I had used up until that point. Then, earlier this year, it replaced it with the S2, which was even better. So, Eufy's new Omni S2 Pro robot vacuum should be an easy recommendation, right? It is, but for $1,699.99, the Omni S2 Pro is also the most expensive machine that Eufy sells, and it arrives while the Omni S2 is being discounted to $999.99. So, for me to recommend the Eufy Omni S2 Pro, it needs to justify that $700 price difference.
I'm pleased to say that it does. Eufy has put a 6-LED UV-C array in the cleaning dock, an electrolyzed water system for the mop in the robot vacuum, and says the S2 Pro is the first robot vacuum to use both to keep your home cleaner than ever. Alongside that, the S2 Pro offers 40,000 Pa of peak suction, a 290mm roller mop that can press down at 16 N, and a base station that washes the mop in boiling water and dries it afterwards.
I've spent the past few weeks using the S2 Pro in my apartment across both carpeted and hard floors. I don't know about you, but I always end up with more hair trapped in the carpet than I'd like to admit, so it's fair to say I put the dust-sucker through its paces. In fact, I've defeated plenty of robot vacuums that I've tested in the past, usually with hair wrapping itself in the vacuum's rollers or the device getting stuck somewhere in my home. But the S2 Pro didn't succumb to either of those problems. It's the new best robot vacuum I've ever used.
At 387 x 476 x 670mm, or roughly 26 inches tall and 19 inches deep, the Eufy Omni S2 Pro's base station is rather large. In a small apartment, you might have to rearrange some furniture so it fits, rather than simply being able to tuck it away somewhere. The robot itself measures 3.86 inches tall, which is also on the larger side, since several rivals are closer to 3.1 inches tall. The difference in size often will determine if the robot can fit under a bed frame or the sofa, or if it bumps off the edges of them instead.
Only a few base station components need putting together, and they snap together easily — you won't need any tools or a second pair of hands to get things going. For the price of the S2 Pro, you'd expect a premium device, and you'll notice that immediately from the build quality. Nothing creaks, rattles, or flexes when the robot drives on or off the base, and the plastic it's made out of feels dense rather than fragile. The LCD display on the dock is useful rather than gimmicky, telling you what the vacuum is doing at a glance without you having to pull out your smartphone.
The station's exterior is a bit of a dust magnet, so it joins the list of surfaces you'll have to dust in your house. It's hard to avoid this, but it feels a little ironic for a device that's supposed to clean itself. The side brush — which swings out beyond the vacuum's body to reach the edges up to the baseboards — is the part of the vacuum most exposed and vulnerable to damage when the robot gets near walls. It survived the few weeks I've been using the S2 Pro, but it's the piece I'd expect to have to replace first. If you're considering the S2 Pro against some of the other new smart appliances actually worth buying in 2026, this is worth bearing in mind.
The Eufy Omni S2 Pro uses what the brand calls its CleanMind AI for navigation and obstacle avoidance. The system is built around a 3D ToF matrix laser that recognizes more than 360 different types of obstacles that are as small as 1cm, in daylight or in darkness. The vacuum can also identify rooms and floor types and adjust its suction and water flow automatically.
This system results in the best obstacle avoidance on any robot vacuum I've tested, beating the excellent S1 Pro that I previously used. It worked its way around shoes, charging cables, and random boxes left near the doorway, and never once got wedged. Anyone who has come home to find their robot vacuum stranded on a rug will understand how beneficial that is. My one criticism of the navigation system is that it's too cautious. The robot gives clutter a rather wide berth, which can leave an unswept buffer around anything you've left on the floor. Oftentimes, I'd rather it get closer to the objects and risk nudging them, but there's no sensitivity setting you can use to change this.
The UV-C system is one of the S2 Pro's flagship features, but it's a little harder to assess. Eufy claims a 99.99% disinfection rate in its lab testing against E. coli, S. aureus, influenza, and Aspergillus. I have no way to verify any of that at home. What I can tell you is that six LEDs run at a 275nm wavelength (which is the germicidal sweet spot to damage microbes) across a 120-degree beam to cover the full width of the floor beneath the robot. I have this setting toggled on in the app, and it casts a purple glow under the robot vacuum. Extra disinfection is never unwelcome, and it requires no effort to switch on the setting, but it isn't necessarily a feature you can notice working.
The Eufy Omni S2 Pro can handle 40,000 Pa of peak suction and 100 AW of airflow. Across the weeks I've been using the S2 Pro, I've never found a patch of carpet it had skimmed over. Hair is hard for many robot vacuums to handle, but the S2 Pro handled it with ease. Hair hasn't ended up wrapped around the brushes in the time I've been using the S2 Pro. Most robot vacuums would need to be cleaned manually in this sort of time, often with a pair of scissors to cut hairs away from the brushes. I wouldn't call it foolproof after just a few weeks of using it, but it's a great sign for long-term use.
The HydroJet roller spins at 240 RPM with dual scrapers flushing it around eight times per second, and the S2 Pro lays down clean water at 131 degrees Fahrenheit. The pressure is obvious, and its cleaning results are close to what I could achieve from manually scrubbing the floor. On the lower water settings, it sometimes left behind faint outlines of dried stains, so I often chose to run the heaviest setting. Its vacuum suction power doesn't seem to be quite as strong while mopping; it often left behind crumbs in the kitchen when it was mopping and vacuuming at the same time, but it would collect every bit of dirt if it was only vacuuming. My guess is that debris sometimes sticks to the wet roller and gets moved around rather than sucked up.
Eufy says the five-layer AeroTurbo filtration system locks 99.99% of allergens inside the vacuum and retains 97% of suction after 365 days of use. Of course, after only using the vacuum for a few weeks, I can't speak to this longevity, but it's reassuring to see. The S2 Pro also carries Asthma & Allergy Friendly certification from the Asthma and Allergy Foundation of America and Allergy Standards Limited.
Unfortunately, Eufy doesn't publish the battery capacity or runtime figure for the S2 Pro, which is a strange omission on a robot vacuum at this price. But after using the robovac for a few weeks, I've been able to observe how it performs in the real world. With Max mode switched on. The robot can't quite finish cleaning my apartment on a single charge. It has to return to the base station partway through to recharge, which usually takes somewhere between 45 minutes and an hour to get enough juice to continue.
It also gives the vacuum a convenient time to empty the dust tank, which would fill up if it had been a while between cleans. When it resumed cleaning, it would pick up exactly where it had left off. Noise is reasonable in the standard modes but gets quite loud with Max mode enabled. While I didn't measure the noise, I'd think twice about running it in the early hours if you live in a shared building or have neighbors.
If I used the standard cleaning modes, it could usually finish cleaning my entire home in just one run. That being said, I don't suppose the battery life matters all that much. The recharge and resume cycle is entirely automatic, and since I usually set the S2 Pro off cleaning when I'm not at home, it doesn't really matter how long it takes or how often it has to recharge. But, if you're in a time pinch and want the floors cleaned before guests arrive, you probably want to run one of the lower-powered settings and accept the slightly less aggressive suction.
The dock, which Eufy calls the All-in-One Station, is sort of a self-cleaning machine that looks after the S2 Pro, reducing the amount of maintenance you'll need to do on the robot vacuum. It empties the robot's dust bin, washes the mop at 212 degrees Fahrenheit, dries it at 131 degrees, refills the water tank, dispenses detergent, and generates the electrolyzed water that Eufy says keeps the tank antibacterial for up to five days at a time.
It can handle five or six cleaning sessions before the clean water tank needs filling or the dirty tank needs emptying, and the dust bag hasn't needed changing yet in my weeks of use. Replacement dust bags cost $18.99 and fit the S2, S1, and S1 Pro as well.
There's also a built-in fragrance diffuser, which releases scent continuously while the robot cleans. The S2 Pro ships with Crisp Pine and Fresh Lavender scent options, and I've been using Crisp Pine, which smells quite good. It works better than I expected on carpet, though the scent fades within a few hours after it finishes cleaning. My capsules on the S2 lasted for around 6 months, and Anker told me the S2 Pro uses the same system. I haven't been able to test whether the old scent capsules fit the new machine, so don't assume your S2 stock carries over.
The mop drying cycle is my only complaint about the base station. It often takes quite a long time (a good few hours), and it's rather noisy, somewhere between a bathroom extractor fan and a hair dryer being used in the next room. If the station sits in your living room, you'll probably notice the noise, so you'll want to schedule cleans so that the drying finishes before you're trying to watch something on TV.
Setting up the Eufy Omni S2 Pro is just as easy as assembling the base station. The companion app walks you through setting things up, the robot maps your home by itself on the first run, and it will correctly mark room boundaries and even name them within the app. It also shows you the furniture in your room, so you can see exactly what the robot sees and correct it if necessary. From there, you can merge, divide, and rename rooms, carpets, or floor materials and draw no-go zones.
Some of the more useful settings require a few taps to access, so I'd recommend looking through all the menus before you finish the setup process. I'd make sure the UV cleaning toggle is on, switch on the heavy stain deep mop setting, and switch on accessory maintenance reminders. The app has worked flawlessly for as long as I've had the vacuum, with very little lag time between me tapping the button to set the robot off and it actually starting to clean. This sounds like it should be basic, but if you've ever used a cheap robot vacuum before, you'll know just how bad some companion apps are.
One area where the S2 Pro lacks is in smart home support. There's no HomeKit, so anyone running an Apple-centric house will have to manage this robot vacuum in its own app. At $1,699, that's a real omission, and it's the kind of thing that pushes people toward rival brands, no matter how good the cleaning is. If platform support is your deciding factor, it's worth seeing how the major robot vacuum brands rank against each other before you buy one.
The Eufy Omni S2 Pro is the most capable robot vacuum I've tested, and in the few weeks I've had it, I've stopped thinking about vacuuming entirely. The suction is more than powerful enough to handle thick carpet. The brushes refuse to tangle in hair. Navigation never gets the robot stranded, and the base station handles most of the robot's maintenance and disappears into the background. While combined mopping and vacuuming sessions can leave debris behind, and the obstacle avoidance is often too generous with its clearances, there's not much more I could ask for from a robot vacuum. Plus, with the UV-C disinfection, you can rest assured that your home will be more spotless than ever.
Whether the S2 Pro is worth $1,699.99 probably depends on your home. If you've got pets, lots of carpet, and someone with allergies, the S2 Pro's suction, filtration, and allergy certification make this option hard to beat. But for a small, hard-floor apartment, you're paying a large premium for a device with powerful suction designed for carpets, when the cheaper Eufy models (and several of the other robot vacuums that rank highest in 2026) could handle your home without a struggle.
The most obvious rival is Eufy's own Omni S2, which is often discounted below $1,000 from the launch price of $1,599.99. It has 30,000 Pa of suction and doesn't have the UV-C array, but for $700 less, it's a great option for most homes. The Roborock Saros 20 matches the S2 Pro's price with 36,000 Pa of suction, dual rotating mop heads, and a 212 degree Fahrenheit mop wash. At 3.4 inches, it's noticeably shorter, so it can reach under some items of furniture that the S2 Pro simply can't.
The Dreame X60 Ultra is also on sale from its regular retail price of $1,499.99, with 35,000 Pa of suction, which makes it the best value pick among the flagship options. None of these rivals offer UV-C floor disinfection, which remains the S2 Pro's exclusive feature. If your budget doesn't quite stretch to this flagship level, check out the cheap robot vacuums worth buying in 2026. BGR's list of the robot vacuums that owners say break down the least often is also worth a look. With all of that in mind, if you have pets, carpet, or allergies, and want the best in disinfection and cleaning, the Eufy Omni S2 Pro is one the best robot vacuums available in 2026."

She considers running her own cafe to be her side hustle.

For Sonnie Brown, every dollar counts. She’s pursuing an acting career while running a cafe on the side. 
Brown is a New York resident who lives in Astoria, in the western portion of the borough of Queens. In a new interview with The New York Times, Brown offered a transparent look into her finances and shed light on her priorities. 
For one, she owns a cafe in Astoria called Sonbobs Coffee House, which connects her to the neighborhood. Brown knows her regulars by name and their orders by heart. She spends long days behind the counter making coffee and catching up with her customers. 
However long she spends at the cafe, Brown thinks of it as her side hustle. Her main pursuit is acting. In some years, she has landed up to five film and TV credits, along with work in commercials and theater. 
Her credits include the play What You Are Now, the CBS crime drama CIA and a recurring role as a judge on Law & Order that brings her back about once a year.
Lately, acting jobs have been harder to come by. Last year, Brown earned about $35,000 from theater and television work.
“I’ve been very fortunate in the amount of work I’ve gotten,” she told the Times. “I just haven’t always been able to support myself doing it.”
Her cafe, which offers homemade muffins and sandwiches in addition to coffee, helps fill in the gaps. Brown made $90,000 through the cafe last year and spent $80,000 in operating expenses. The remaining $10,000 profit remained in the cafe’s bank account; Brown did not touch it for her personal expenses. 
Brown said she pays herself by eating food from the cafe. She goes to Costco every week to source ingredients for her recipes and buys the produce for her sandwiches at Elliniki Agora, a market a few blocks from the cafe. 
Her other expenses are tightly controlled. For example, Brown has lived in the same two-bedroom apartment in Astoria for nearly 30 years. The apartment is rent-stabilized and costs $1,600 a month, a sum she divides with her husband and son. 
Brown arrived in New York in the late 1980s. It was a change of pace after spending her childhood in Miami. 
She landed a bartending gig in New York, but life in the city wore her down. She seriously considered moving to Hawaii to escape. 
Before packing up, she wanted to try acting lessons in New York, the one thing that she could “only do here and nowhere else in the world.” That decision changed her plans. She loved acting and began setting goals for herself. Each time she reached a milestone, she found another reason to stay in New York. 
“I’ve had very good years, and when you have a good year, if you’re smart, you invest it or you save it,” she told the Times. “And that’s what I do, because otherwise who knows what’s going to happen next year?
For Sonnie Brown, every dollar counts. She’s pursuing an acting career while running a cafe on the side.

42,2026-11-17,"
            POPULAR SEARCHES
          

            BROWSE BY
          
When the quarterbacks are good this year, they’re really good.
Every week, it feels like a different QB is ""best"" — and that guy takes the lead in the MVP hunt. It’s as competitive as I can remember. And this week, it’s more of the same. 
You probably know who’s atop the list.
Welcome back to QB Stock Market. These aren’t your typical quarterback rankings. I focus on contextualizing the fluctuations of starters' performances throughout the season. And every week, I’ll dive deeply into the four most compelling performers.
No one — not even Josh Allen — is safe at the top. No one — not even Carson Beck — is stuck at the bottom. There is room for quick ascent — and rapid decline. One question holds the most importance: What have you done for your team lately?
Previous: Offseason | Preseason | Week 1 | Week 2 | Week 3 | Week 4 | Week 5 | Week 6 | Week 7 | Week 8 | Week 9 | Week 10
Previous ranking: N/A
There’s still an outside shot that Beck can make himself relevant for the Cardinals in 2027. But in all likelihood, he’s going to play in a manner that puts Arizona at the top of the draft, where it can take a QB.
Previous ranking: N/A 
McCord is a former sixth-round draft pick who is on his third team in two years. And he played exactly like a former sixth-round draft pick who is on his third team in two years.
Previous ranking: N/A
Hear me out: Jimmy Garoppolo. Now that Drake Maye is likely to miss the rest of the year, the Patriots could choose to tank. But that’s not Mike Vrabel’s way. So if you’re going to try to win, get Jimmy G back in the building.
Previous ranking: 31 ↑
Don’t look now, but the Eagles can still win the NFC East. And that means, in theory, Milton could help them do it.
Previous ranking: 28
I am glad the Vikings traded for Kirk Cousins and not Daniel Jones. Let’s just leave it at that.
Previous ranking: 27 
At this point this year, it’s a good thing Mayfield got a bye — in part because it could help with his thumb injury, but mostly because, well, he’s hard to watch this year.
Previous ranking: N/A
His performance helped put into context what Kirk Cousins did in Minnesota. With twice the time to prepare and learn the playbook, Brissett looked out of sorts early. Which is fair. In-season transitions are hard. Brissett got better in the second half versus the Chargers — but this Ravens offensive line is putrid.
Previous ranking: 30 ↑
He did some nice things, particularly on scrambles. But the biggest thing was, he didn’t make any flagrant mistakes — except, perhaps, for letting his annoyance with Jim Harbaugh show in the locker room after the team’s first win.
Previous ranking: N/A
Las Vegas coach Klint Kubiak seemed most interested in avoiding disaster for Mendoza — so much so that a win seemed secondary. The Raiders simplified their offense. They ran a designed QB draw to get Mendoza a TD. They successfully got him through his first start against a terrifying Seattle defense without catastrophe. To me, that’s a success. We’ll know more about Mendoza in the coming weeks.
Previous ranking: 22 ↓
At his worst, Penix is just an arm.
He doesn’t have a good feel for his micro-level decisions through progression — nor does he have a good feel for the macro-level decisions that help QBs manage games. He needs ample time to get the ball to his receivers. With the exception of go-balls to the sideline, he has a habit of waiting to confirm whether a receiver is open. And that’s why a defense like the Chiefs proved so difficult for him. Penix even had issues with swing passes, failing to complete two in this game.
He was 23 of 43 for 286 yards and an interception against a defense that was really solid. But receiver Drake London actually made matters worse for Penix, failing to haul in two of his best throws. That didn’t stop Penix from going to London after that. But it did contribute to the massive deficit that the Falcons quickly fell into. 
The silver lining for the Falcons is that Penix’s injuries have killed all urgency around a potential contract for the third-year QB. Because Atlanta won’t be in a rush to sign him long term, even as he’ll be eligible this offseason along with the likes of Caleb Williams, Jayden Daniels, Drake Maye and Bo Nix. Unlike them, he has yet to make a case for a deal.
I’m not saying he can’t. But I’ve yet to see him play at a sustained level that makes me think he’s the guy. He needs more time. And he needs to stay healthy.
Previous ranking: 17 ↓
The Steelers aren’t going to be a dangerous team in the playoffs. They seem like — on paper — the exact team that should succeed in the playoffs: smart QB, power run game, good defense. But Rodgers is so washed up that the offense is a total mess when the Steelers need him.
Previous ranking: 24 ↑
He still has a weird habit of throwing like a pitcher on the mound. But he’s also still playing better than he ever played for the Vikings. So let’s let this experiment ride.
Previous ranking: 21 ↑
If you’re waiting for signs of life from Ward, well … they’re here!
This might be the best two-game stretch we’ve seen from the second-year QB. And his duel with Joe Burrow’s Bengals looks all the more impressive after what we just saw from Cincy. Ward’s Week 10 performance against the Jaguars wasn’t quite in that same league. But it was impressive nonetheless.
First of all, Ward looks like a guy who knows what he’s doing. I know that’s a low bar to clear, but go back to the preseason and September, and you won’t see that.
Second, he’s not making as many unforced errors. Did he miss an easy throw in this game? Yes, in the first half. But there were fewer of them. When someone’s open, he’s finding him. When someone’s not open, he throws it away. And I’ll even excuse his interception, which was actually a crafty piece of QB play. It just happened to also be a mind-boggling piece of cornerback play from Travis Hunter — the type of INT that only Hunter, who moonlights as a wide receiver, could pull off.
Ultimately, the game pivoted around one other play — and it was Ward’s worst.
Down 10 points on third-and-5 in the third quarter, Ward was almost in the red area. The Jaguars showed all-out blitz, so Ward adjusted his protection accordingly. He had to know he had about two seconds to get the ball out. When he dropped back, he looked right at his hot route — who was open. All Ward needed to do was rip it. Instead? Sack.
This is why I’m not going overboard with Ward’s progress. He’s learning how to play quarterback. I’ll give him that. But there’s a lot of distance and development that separates him from the top players on this list. Ward has all the talent to get there.
Previous ranking: 19
Smith has quietly been really solid. He has thrown for 2,226 passing yards, 12 touchdowns and three interceptions. He’s playing in close games, and it feels like the Jets' losing record is more a (dis)credit to coach Aaron Glenn than to Smith.
Previous ranking: 16 ↓
It has felt like Young has had a heavy hand in whether the Panthers won or lost this year. But in their win over the Saints, Young turned the offense over to RB Chuba Hubbard. It was Young's first game without a touchdown (even though it looked like he missed a clear opportunity at one below). But on the bright side, he also had his first performance without an interception since Week 6.
Previous ranking: 14 ↓
After seeing what Drew Lock did to the Raiders — and frankly what every QB has done to the Raiders — Nix and Sean Payton must be amped for Week 11.
Previous ranking: N/A
Watson is doing all the right things to help his team win games — and get out of the way when his teammates, particularly those on defense, are doing it for him. He might be doing it without grace or tact. But he’s doing it.
Previous ranking: 23 ↑
I’ve shrugged at Lock’s numbers for too long. I want to stop for a moment to give him a little love — right before he steps aside for Sam Darnold in Week 11. Lock has been the most efficient QB in football this year, with the highest EPA/dropback (.35), the highest completion percentage over expected (8.3%) and the third-highest success rate (53.4%), per Next Gen Stats. If Darnold struggles, I can’t help but wonder if we might see Lock jump back into the QB1 spot.
Previous ranking: 11 ↓
The Panthers blitzed Shough sparingly — just four times — but they made it count. The Saints QB was 1 of 4 with an interception against the blitz. Aside from that, he was good, using his legs and arms to do what he could for the offense. It just wasn’t enough this week.
Previous ranking: 18 ↑
It’s no mystery why Cousins made the offense hum on Sunday.
It’s because he instantly understood how to wield the playbook at a higher level than Kyler Murray — or J.J. McCarthy last year. You could see — at multiple points throughout the game — Cousins make checks at the line of scrimmage to get out of one play and into another. And when he made those in-game adjustments, it paid dividends.
Look at the fourth quarter when, on third-and-12, he changed the play to an audible at the line of scrimmage after seeing an all-out blitz from the Packers. That gave the Vikings a numbers advantage on the perimeter, where Justin Jefferson got a 16-yard pickup.
And look at the throw to Tai Felton on third-and-15 where Cousins appeared to flip the play to work in the opposite direction — thereby avoiding the deep safety who would’ve made the play impossible to convert.
I thought Cousins would be rusty. I thought there might be issues plopping Cousins into the system. And I thought we should remain skeptical of Kevin O’Connell and quarterbacks.
Turns out, I was wrong.
Previous ranking: 15 ↑
This is going to be a slog for Stafford, who might not have Puka Nacua or Davante Adams until December. But the Rams got themselves calibrated over the first quarter and quickly put up 34 on the Cardinals. It won’t be easy. But I think they’ll figure this out.
Previous ranking: 12 ↑
If Daniels keeps this up, the Commanders could win the NFC East. And no, of course, I’m not kidding. It’s the NFC East.
Previous ranking: 10
His two interceptions were especially awful. But because the Patriots went into utter free fall, Goff's turnovers didn’t bite the Lions this week.
Previous ranking: 9
His gaudy numbers are pretty great for fantasy owners. But I’d bet Cowboys fans would trade in some of those touchdowns for wins. 
Previous ranking: 8
Given what we know about the Browns' defense (it's really good), Stroud was as good as he’s been all year. The Texans, however, were as bad as they’ve been all year.
Previous ranking: 7
Bye-week bounce back?
Previous ranking: 2 ↓
I am still recovering from what the Vikings did to Love. The Packers offensive line collapsed in a manner unfit for the guy who should be the top MVP candidate. And I’m telling you, Love played well, all things considered. I’m serious.
Previous ranking: 5
He’s a robot. Every time you look over, he has thrown 300 yards, three touchdowns and an interception, and the Jaguars are up multiple scores. Every week, it’s just a copy-paste.
Previous ranking: 1 ↓
Week 10 wasn't a trap game in the sense that the Bills were going to lose. But Allen absolutely stumbled, playing down to the Jets level in New Jersey. 
Previous ranking: 4 ↑
Purdy put together a special performance, where he and his playmakers just toyed with the Cowboys. I can’t believe he’s only at No. 3, but that’s where we are with all these QBs playing so well.
Previous ranking: 3 ↑ 
Five touchdowns. Five!
Previous ranking: 6 ↑
Forgive me. I couldn’t get all that excited about Burrow's shootouts with the Titans and the Falcons in the past few weeks. Those defenses are profoundly bad. But this?
THIS!?
Burrow's big day against the Steelers was the type of performance that MVP voters will remember when they're thinking about who to vote for after Week 18. It was partially because he threw for 409 yards and four touchdowns. But it was also because it came against Pittsburgh, which currently sits in the top 10 in points allowed this year. That's a great defense.
Burrow is the only QB on pace to exceed 40 passing touchdowns. He has the best receiving tandem in the NFL, and he’s making them every defensive coordinator’s biggest problem. It has been like this every week, too, with Burrow putting up crazy numbers — in part because the Bengals' run game has been so inefficient. (Burrow is like Jordan Love with an offensive line.)
I’ve been saying it for weeks. Look out for Burrow and the Bengals to secure a first-round bye. And if they do, he could quickly lock up the MVP. More importantly, though, look out for Burrow and the Bengals to make a legit run at the Super Bowl.

50,2026-11-17,"Juggling between three languages on a daily basis, Iris is known for her love of linguistics, arts, web design and typography, as well as her goldmine of …
More about
Iris ↬
Weekly tips on front-end & UX.Trusted by 182,000+ folks.
AI is changing how we design and build, and it isn’t slowing down. On December 1, 2026, SmashingConf is hosting the AI 2027 Conference, a live online half day event to take stock of where AI stands today, what we can expect in 2027, and where teams are getting it right.
With AI maturing, it’s clear we need to learn how AI can elevate your workflow as a UX designer and Design System engineer. Most of us are still figuring it out and trying to work smarter in this new world, so we invited industry experts who have been doing this for a while. They show their experience and tell you what works and what doesn’t, so you don’t have to figure it all out yourself.
Every session is practical. Speakers show how they actually work: their tools, their setups, and the small things that are easy to miss. The event ends with moderated breakout rooms, where you can bring your own workflow questions. See you there?
This conference is dedicated to designers and engineers (front-end, UI, UX) who want to understand how AI can improve the way their team works, without the hype. If you’re interested in AI-ready design systems, AI and Figma, harness engineering, agentic AI UX patterns, and AI workflows for design and engineering teams, this conference is right up your alley!
Showing, not telling: All sessions will be highly practical, focusing on how to integrate AI in your daily work and what to expect in 2027.
For our AI 2027 Conference, your friendly host Vitaly will welcome you and quickly explain how the event will run. We can’t wait to meet you!
AI is moving fast. With it come new workflows in product teams, new design patterns, new user expectations and new ways of thinking about building and designing for AI.Let’s look at where things stand today. A lot changed in 2026, and there are things we can now design and build that simply weren’t possible a year ago. We’ll cover the patterns taking shape heading into 2027, from multi-modal experiences to accessibility, and what tends to go wrong along the way. A big part of that is designing for trust, so people feel confident using what we make.What makes AI products reliable in practice? We’ll look at how teams cut down on hallucinations with better context, a well-built harness and sensible guardrails. Then we’ll finish with what’s coming in 2027, including new experiments, regulations and a few takeaways to bring back to your team.Roll up your sleeves for a packed session, full of real-life examples and design patterns.
Great AI-powered products live at the intersection of engineering depth, design craft and product thinking. In this talk, Anna Arteeva shows how to build real, working AI experiences, not just one-shot prototypes that fall apart the moment someone uses them.You’ll learn how to design and prototype an AI-powered experience from scratch, with functionality built on LLMs, voice and image generation logic. Anna will also show how to add that little bit of delight to your app, and how it can respond to different types of input, whatever the person on the other side of the screen gives you: text, voice or images.
What coding agents need from your design system docs, and how to write it.Documentation is the part of the design system teams are least happy with, and it’s been that way for years. Luke Murphy looks at how we ended up with docs nobody reads and why agents change the picture. Then, drawing on DS ARC’s tests of real design systems, he walks through the small writing changes that make docs work for agents and people alike.This is a talk from our friends at zeroheight. Thank you for joining in!
The big question for many teams is how to get your code and your Figma file talking to each other. In this session, Christine Vallaure shares how she has set this up in practice: going from code to Figma instead of the other way around, moving back and forth between the two for the best result, and what that means for how we work.You’ll hear why design system basics suddenly matter a lot more, why naming things well pays off, and why AI builders so often produce code that looks like your design system but still has to be rebuilt. She’ll also explain the engineering side that designers don’t always see, like the checks and files your developers set up, so you know how it works and what to ask for. Along the way, she covers the problems you’re likely to run into, what to keep in mind, and where Storybook fits in.Expect plenty of small tips and tricks you can take straight back to your team.
AI flipped the script on the traditional, linear design system workflow. That’s okay. We’re all experimenting, and nobody has this figured out yet.TJ Pitre has spent the past two years working with dozens of teams to find the flow that fits them. He’ll show three alternative workflows live. Which one fits depends on you: how big your design system is, how big your team is, how much effort you’re willing to put in, and how well it needs to scale.The goal is to get you thinking, and to send you back to your team with a few workflows worth trying.
Brad Frost shares his actual workflow for building products with AI and a design system. He’ll show the setup, architecture, and tools he uses to keep AI on the rails of his components, tokens, and patterns.Expect live building, real design decisions, and plenty of practical tricks along the way. You’ll see how it all works together and leave with ideas to apply to your own design system and workflow.
Designing & Building AI Experiences in 2027The way we design and build is changing massively, and we are all adapting to the new reality. In this session, we’ll talk about how to design and build AI experiences that actually work, and are a delight to use. Bring your questions, post your problems and work with Vitaly and Anna, and all the other attendees, to solve them together. We’ll look at everything from interaction patterns and prototypes to adding those little touches of delight, and have some fun while doing it!Have a specific UX AI problem? Ask it in the Google doc, and get your question answered!
Design Systems & AI in 2027Let’s be honest: work is messy. But let’s see how we can make it work, and have some fun while doing it! Ask all your questions, post your problems and work with Christine, Brad & TJ, and all the other attendees, to solve them together.Have a specific design system headache? Bring your questions, and let’s discuss!
We will be emailing you with the video recordings, the slides, the collaborative docs, the takeaways and more. Everything in one place, so you can rewatch the talks you loved and catch the ones you missed.
We’ll open the doors 15 minutes early! Make sure to check your timezone 🌍 so you make it on time. We look forward to seeing you there!
On December 1, 2026, you’ll get to learn from and share your UX AI problems and design system headaches with six experts: they’re all very excited to share their personal practical tips, clever tricks and valuable insights that you can put into action right away. Expect plenty of inspiration, useful takeaways, and, of course, a little fun along the way!
Vitaly Friedman loves beautiful content and doesn’t like to give in easily. When he is not writing or speaking at a conference, he’s most probably running front-end/UX workshops and webinars. He loves solving complex UX, front-end and performance problems.
Anna Arteeva is a product design leader turned agentic coding instructor, and one of Maven’s top-ranked educators, formerly head of product design at Payoneer. With a technical background (frontend), a career in product design, and a healthy obsession with building things. She found her calling in helping designers unlock their superpowers with coding agents, helping designers move beyond AI hype and one-shot prototypes, and develop the technical fluency, practical skills, and good judgement to make agentic coding a powerful part of professional design practice.
Christine Vallaure is a designer and educator with twenty years in product work. She is the founder of Moonlearning.io, a learning platform that helps designers blur the line between design and code, and the author of The Solo. Christine spends most of her time in the developers’ weeds and brings back what designers actually need, then turns it into far too detailed articles, hands-on teaching and talks at conferences around the world.
TJ Pitre is the Product Architect for AI and Design Systems at Figma, and Founder/Owner of Southleft, a design systems consultancy. He works with teams to bridge design and engineering using structured context, modern tooling, and practical experimentation.
Brad Frost is a design system consultant, web designer & developer, speaker, writer, teacher, musician, and artist located in beautiful Pittsburgh, PA. He helps people establish & evolve design systems, establish more collaborative workflows, and design & build software together. Brad is the author of the book Atomic Design, which introduces a methodology to create and maintain effective design systems. He co-hosted the Style Guides Podcast and has helped create several tools and resources for web designers, e.g. an online course, AI & Design Systems, with experts from the industry.
We’re excited to have our friends at zeroheight on board. The folks at zeroheight have created a platform for creating, managing and publishing design system documentation. Designers and developers get to work in the same place, so docs are more than a handoff. Find out more at zeroheight.com.
Join zeroheight’s Principal Design Advocate Luke Murphy for a partner talk on why nobody reads your design system documentation, and what coding agents need from your docs. (Thank you for joining in, dear Luke!)
With a commitment to quality content for the design community.
Founded by Vitaly Friedman and Sven Lennartz. 2006–2026.
Smashing is proudly running on Netlify, TinaCMS and Swell.

51,2026-11-17,"This video can not be played
Carney eyes 'other options' for embattled Canadian auto sector on China trip
Canada's car industry has become one of the clearest stress tests for Ottawa as it grapples with US tariffs on the sector.
Now Prime Minister Mark Carney has signalled that he is looking to China, where he is on a visit for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit (Apec), as part of the solution. 
But that avenue is not without controversy, and is once again drawing the ire of those close to Donald Trump. 
While in Cambodia earlier this week, Carney told reporters that Canada could turn to European and Asian carmakers to save its auto manufacturing sector if a trade deal with the US could not be reached.
If Canadian auto workers are ""not fully utilised in a North American context, that provides other opportunities. 
""Canada's going to move forward either way,"" he said.
He went on to meet Chinese President Xi Jinping in China on Tuesday for their second head-to-head in less than a year. The two talked of the importance of developing their relations, and Carney said they had agreed to further tariff cuts.
Neither side mentioned vehicles specifically, though analysts suggest the issue will have been prominent in their minds.
Carney and Xi met on Tuesday to discuss cooperation and trade ahead of this week's Apec summit
In January, Carney said that opening the door to China's electric vehicles, external (EVs) could result in investment into Canadian-owned companies and the creation of manufacturing jobs in the sector. 
American tariffs on imports of Canadian-made vehicles have led to thousands of job losses in Canada and anxiety over the future, as 90% of cars made in Canada are shipped to and sold in the US.
At the same time, China has been eyeing Canada as a potential entry-point to the North American EV market. Earlier this year, Canada announced that it would let in 49,000 EVs from China at a low tariff rate, with plans to raise that quota gradually to 70,000 by 2030.
The move was a significant break from the US, which tariffs Chinese EVs heavily to protect domestic manufacturing.
Peter Navarro, Trump's senior counsel for trade and manufacturing, warned the prime minister against cosying up to China in a post on X, external.
""Are u really threatening to play China off the US? This is offensive in the extreme,"" he wrote on Monday. ""You should be far more worried China is going to eat Canada alive.""
His comments were reposted by Pete Hoekstra, the US ambassador to Canada.
Canada's international trade minister Maninder Sidhu toured a Chinese EV plant earlier this month
Chinese EV manufacturers like BYD and Chery have already been lobbying the Canadian government for market access.
Last week, Canada's Minister of International Trade Maninder Sidhu toured a Chery plant in China, external. He told reporters afterwards that Canadians could expect to see affordable Chinese EVs in Canada by early 2027.
These moves come as the toll of US tariffs on Canada's auto sector has become harder to ignore.
Stellantis, a multinational car manufacturer that made models like the Dodge Challenger in Canada, recently announced it would sell its assembly plant in Brampton, Ontario.
The facility had been idle since 2023 for a planned retooling, but what was supposed to be a temporary shutdown became permanent after Trump's tariffs.
The sale has led to speculation that a Chinese EV manufacturer could move into the facility, after Canadian media outlet CTV News reported, external, citing anonymous sources, that Stellantis was planning to lease back 25% of its plant to assemble ""affordable"" EVs.
The idea is unpopular with Ontario Premier Doug Ford. In a letter to Carney, he warned against opening the door for Chinese EV kits to be assembled at the Brampton plant. 
China overwhelmingly builds its cars at home and ships them whole - or to be assembled in kits - overseas.
Canada's deal with China signals it is serious about shift from US
How China made electric vehicles mainstream
Lynette Ong, director of the China Governance Lab at the University of Toronto, said introducing Chinese auto manufacturing in Canada would be ""controversial"" due to fears that Canadian jobs could be lost and that the North American industry could be eroded.
""We want to make sure they're hiring Canadian workers, that they are helping train Canadian workers and that they are transferring the technology over,"" Ong said.
Carney noted there are ""clear guardrails"" in how Canada deals with China, including barring work on sensitive matters like artificial intelligence and critical minerals.
Canada has also eyed other partnerships. It is notably pursuing associate member status with the EU, and had signed a free trade deal with the Philippines earlier this week. 
Just how much Canada is willing to embrace Chinese manufacturing, however, still depends on the outcome of ongoing US-Canada trade talks.
Progress has been made in negotiations, Carney recently said, but differences remain. It is unclear if Trump and Carney will speak on the sidelines of the Apec summit later this week.

Samsung  Android Rumors

Apple's 20th anniversary iPhones, expected next year, are rumored to bring a quad-curved front glass with extremely slim bezels, but it now looks like Samsung may want to get there first. According to a new rumor out of Korea, the company is considering launching some of its upcoming Galaxy flagships with an ""extremely bezel-less"" design, at almost the same level as the display a ""certain company"" is preparing.
The source doesn't name that company, but it's not hard to guess it's Apple. And since Samsung Display is one of the main suppliers of OLED panels for iPhones, Samsung would be in a good position to know exactly what's coming, and to make something similar for its own phones.
The display itself is reportedly progressing without any big issues, with the source saying the problems are only with ""other technical parts"" for now. Samsung is said to be reviewing whether to introduce the design first in ""some"" of its future flagships, though which ones and when exactly isn't mentioned.
The source itself rates the accuracy of this rumor at 50%, so this is very much a take it with a big grain of salt situation. Still, it wouldn't be the first time Samsung has beaten Apple to a new phone design - the Galaxy S25 Edge arrived a few months ahead of the iPhone Air, after all, while the Galaxy Z Fold8 launched before the iPhone Duo more recently.

56,2026-11-17,"Some states across America need to pick up the homebuilding pace — or housing prices could catch up to them.
From January to August 2026, the US national average for housing permits — indicating how many housing units are approved to be built — was 2.83 units per 1,000 residents, according to an October research report from the Lied Center for Real Estate at the University of Nevada, Las Vegas.
The report provides insights into what the housing market could look like in 2028 and 2029, as these approved permits come online as available housing units, said Nicholas Irwin, coauthor of the report and research director at the Lied Center for Real Estate. Across the country, some states lag behind the national average, which could drive up housing prices in the near future as housing supply fails to catch up with demand.
""If you don't add supply, and demand keeps growing as more people move in and more households are formed, it just puts upward pressure on prices,"" Irwin said.
Here are the states that are issuing permits at the lowest rate per 1,000 residents.
The states lagging in issuing building permits could face growing affordability concerns, especially those falling short of projected household growth, another measure included in the report.
""If we are already at a lag in some of these states — your Nevadas, your Connecticut's, Maryland's — when it comes to building, that's just going to exacerbate currently experienced affordability concerns for each of those states' residents,"" Irwin said.
Alaska was the state with the lowest ranking, standing at 0.72 permits per 1,000 residents, after only 528 units were approved from January through August 2026, 75% below the national rate, the report said. Its permit acceptances have also declined year-over-year, down 8.3% from the same period in 2025 and 12.3% from 2023.
Connecticut was ranked the second-lowest state in number of permits per resident in the US, as it approved 3,799 permits, 64% below the national rate. This rate is 17.7% less than during January to August of 2025, and 9.3% lower than the same period in 2023. It was the lowest-ranked state in the report's overall housing supply pipeline rankings — performing poorly across four metrics, including the permit rate per 1,000 residents.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Regarding the overall ranking, Irwin said some states are struggling on all fronts.
""Nevada, Alaska, Connecticut, North Dakota, Maryland are all doing really poorly, and they're doing poorly no matter how much we weigh our different four pillars,"" Irwin said.
West Virginia was ranked the 10th-lowest state in building permits per resident, at 1.55, 45% below the US average. Overall, it ranked 22nd in the report, with mixed performance across metrics.
While its overall number of permits per resident lagged behind other states, its pace of permits relative to household growth was the third-highest in the nation — meaning permitting is outpacing its projected household growth by 5.72. This is because the number of households is projected to shrink in West Virginia, according to the report.
Irwin said there are some states that ""no matter how you weigh"" them consistently end up at the top or bottom of the rankings.
""No matter how you look at the data, the building permit regime and how housing is being constructed in these areas is falling way behind of what's needed,"" he said. ""Our main thing, if we can give anything, is just build more housing, build all types of housing.""

""Secretary Mullin is required by law to travel on a government airplane. This is to ensure access to secure communications and to comply by his constitutional duty of continuity of government should a national emergency arise. Any flight not related to his service as DHS Secretary he pays for personally,"" a DHS spokesperson said in a statement.
“Secretary Mullin abides by all ethics requirements,"" the spokesperson added.

62,2026-11-17,"The M-Vave FM-1 was a pretty unremarkable little synthesizer “toy” until the open-source synth hackers took it over. Now, it’s a sweet platform for exploration, the incredible work done by the community has encouraged the manufacturer to release an open SDK, and it has recently taken the global synth nerd community by storm. (It has sadly also gotten about a 50% markup in the last month due to this popularity.)
It’s the perfect storm of inexpensive hardware, vibe-coded software, and easy-to-use tooling that enables synthesizer firmware-hacking for the mainstream. It is also the fashionable box of the moment in a nerdy niche of the synthesizer world at the moment and it’s something that we’re going to see in non-synthesizer contexts as well, so buckle up.

The funny thing about the FM-1 is that most of the good stuff inside it was open-source all along. As it is sold, it is a Yamaha DX7 emulator, and it appears to be running the same sound engine as Dexed, which is an open-source DX7 clone based on the even older music synthesizer for android (msfa).
Dexed, and its smaller brother MiniDexed, has given birth to many sweet synthesizer hacks that we’ve seen here, including a personal favorite: a Yamaha TX816 clone based on a Raspberry Pi. So it certainly looked to everyone at the time as if M-vave wrapped a not-entirely-inspiring user interface around this sound engine and shipped it.
And that would have been the end of it too, if it weren’t for those meddling kids [AL-255] who did a thorough reverse-engineering of the machine about four months ago, which laid the groundwork for everything else to come. Today, we’re looking at around 250 firmwares for this little beasty, so it’s no exaggeration to say that this opened the floodgates. And along the way, he verified signatures of the msfa code, so everyone’s suspicions proved correct.
A quick note on the LLM / agentic coding elephant in the room here. From reading through [AL-255]’s work, it was most almost certainly worked on and documented by the robots, and we have no idea of the relative contribution of man and machine. Publishing a reverse engineering effort like this enables others in the community to point their robots at the repo, and gives them a tremendous leg up in the next steps along the path. We have just as little clue about how much of any of the subsequent synthesizer and sequencer routines were written with LLM assistance, but at the rate they’re popping up, we’d guess a lot.
[Baud Girl]’s sequencer looks great!About two months after [AL-255]’s work, [Madeline] wrote one of the first reworks of the firmware for the FM-1, working as “Baud Girl”. It added some virtual analog to the cold DX7 tones, but notably wrapped it up in a much nicer UI than the original. It has so far been kept closed-source, but as a proof of what’s possible, it certainly inspired many others.
And then along came [Leo Kuroshita]’s open-source Felucca. This program added a yet fancier sequencer, multiple instruments, drums, and even sound effects. It turned the single-instrument FM-1 into a miniature music-production workshop – what the kids from the 1990’s would call a “groovebox”. [isod89]’s Sloop would refine on Felucca, and is probably the standout application running on the FM-1 platform at the moment.
The Firmware Hub started collecting a list of applications written by the community, and there are 250 of them in about six weeks’ time. Suffice it to say that we can’t even begin to scratch the surface, but they range from serious musical interface explorations, to rainbow-colored kids toys, to fart machines, and then to recreations of iconic synthesizers of the past. Something for everyone? Certainly more interesting than Dexed-in-a-box!
There is no guarantee of quality or of suitability for purpose. On some of them, pitch bend won’t work because the bot or person doing the coding just didn’t get around to it. On others, there will be USB problems. Some have audio glitches, which is a serious issue for a sound-making machine. It’s hard to judge the signal-to-noise ratio of the software output for this device (pun intended); it’s the wild west. And at the rate that applications are piling in, you can only hope to review a snapshot anyway.
If you want a guided tour of some of the best applications as of last week, we love [AudioPilz]’s Tier List, embedded below, that also comes with a heavy dose of synth-nerd inside jokes. [Floyd Steinberg]’s video is full of thoughtful commentary on the state of the art.

Then the story takes a cool turn. M-vave, the company that makes the FM-1, decided to open-source its firmware and its entire SDK. Well, kinda. The strangest turn of events is that they opened up everything except the sound engine itself, which they release as a binary blob. Why? Nobody knows. Some have speculated that it’s because it is based on the GPL-licensed Dexed and are covering their tracks, which is possible. But if M-vave went further back than that, and based it exclusively on the Apache 2.0-licensed msfa, then they’re in the clear.
Either way, there is now official open-firmware support for this little gadget, and a growing community of folks writing new software for it. Because of this official support, musicians who have never flashed an Arduino are overwriting their stock bootloaders. For their part, M-vave is selling a lot more FM-1s than it would otherwise. It’s a win all around.
If you watch a number of video reviews of the FM-1, they range from “Game Changer: The Industry is Doomed!” to “The Only Synth You’ll Ever Need” to “Robot Slop”. We think that’s all a little bit overblown. There have always been awesome open-source projects in the synthesizer space. Mutable Instrument’s [Émilie Gillet] gave us countless amazing open examples, many of which are implemented for the FM-1. Major synth manufacturer Korg flirted around with encouraging users to code their own synthesizers in the Minilogue series. None of the FM-1’s capabilities are new, and not all of it is even musical. As a pure synth, it sounds fine, but it’s nothing to write home about.
But what is interesting about the FM-1 story is the sheer velocity at which things are progressing, the positive manufacturer response to being “hacked”, and the empowerment of a community to write its own oddball sound machines. There is more new firmware coming out for the FM-1 than any one person can hope to keep up with, even though the tooling to do so has never been easier. It’s a microcosm of the creation-overflow that we live in. Will this be seen as the beginning of a new era of custom firmware for commercial devices? Fun times!
>to fart machines
Good to see 4th graders getting in on the act!
All kidding aside, getting kids excited about tech is a good thing.
Jokes about the sounds that people make with their vast, expensive modular synth projects are a standard.   Hence…
I have two of them … received them last friday. It took me 3 hours to code a python programm controlling the fm1 using ble midi, creating all those robot sounds we know and love from the movies.  The fm1 will now be integrated into my mobile robot. Marvelous 😊
I’m curious and a little terrified to see how this sort of extremely rapid, crowd/AI-sourced development model will proliferate into all sorts of other fields. Artists definitely have a feel and need for “I’ve got this box, wouldn’t it be cool if it did X? Then I could do Y!” and then quickly spin up AI-assisted modifications, which is generally cool.  I’m nervous to see what happens when other totally different industries that were never intended to support this activity start seeing similar behaviors driven by insanely fast crowsourced development.  Don’t like how your legacy industrial control system is maintained?  Spin up your own firmware!  Want your in-dash navigation to do XYZ? Spin up your own firmware! Frustrated that feature Z isn’t implemented on your low-cost CPAP machine? Spin up your own firmware!
I feel like we’re just starting to see a new arms race between the intended (and regulated) user experience of the OEM’s IP and the insatiable feature appetite of the people.  Is this safe?  For most cases, probably?  Is this good for industry?  Probably? I don’t know!  
I guess my biggest concern is that Joe User now has the ability to inject modifications for a whole slew of things with very little feel for if it’s a good, safe implementation (or even if it’s a good idea at all) and then everyone can point their AI at it and freely iterate on that in turn without a real version control strategy.  It’s fine if Joe User bricks his own $1,200 Octatrack, but it’s another thing entirely if Joe User is the producer of a mod to automotive infotainment systems that is found to have a 1 in 100 chance of completely locking up the vehicle’s high speed CAN bus after hitting 100% CPU utilization and has now become the common ancestor to thousands of modifications 5 years later.
I guess I just hope that we the people can tell the difference between a Good Idea and a Bad Idea and the industry will have a good, safe, healthy response to “you did WHAT with our product?!” So far I think we’re still good, but for how long?
I agree with you that this is literally a brave new world and there will be both good and bad that comes from this new capability.  It’s also worth noting that as good as AI tools have become, they are no where near done improving…..
On the other side, from the standpoint of people spinning there own, this has often been true.  When the Ford Model T came out, ranchers were turning them into trucks, tearing down wrecked ones and making pumps and wenches, etc, etc, etc…   The hacker subset of humans has always been modifying things, with both good and bad impact.
Wasn’t there a case recently where some some AI company disliked people making crazy total-conversion game mods (like putting Counter Strike mechanics in Skyrim setting) and set its model to go I'm sorry, Dave. I'm afraid I can't do that. when told to recompile and mod existing games? Suddenly many YouTube content creators had to go on siucide watch because they lost their ability to create content.
That’s the future. Just like with drug dealers their business model is to get people hooked on “infinite possibilities” of AI and then cripple it all the way unless used with a paid subscription. I suspect there will also be “tiers” introduced, like: $10/month for “basic” usage where AI only teaches you how to wipe your ass or make instant noodes; $30/month for a “premium” option where AI can now make funny cat pictures, play rock music and help with your kids math homework; $50/month for “pro” users, like CS students who need to write basic python code (without OOP though, not allowed) or create a static, CV-like webpage and then $250/month for “extreme” variant which is almost unlimited and also allows adult content, like generating lesbian yaoi videos featuring lactation fetish.
Or just run a local model and keep doing whatever you want.
A little late to the game.  
What game?
Yes, it could use some more cowbell.
Hey AL, thanks for the careful and precise remarks regarding machine assistance, I really appreciate it!
there was only like 100 firmwares when i think i submitted a tip about this one…  but in the mean time i took delivery of mine and am about 40% done with my own custom firmware. will i release it and make it 251… probably not. the best thing about the robots doing the “heavy” lifting is that i can make this just for me and not feel like i need to share it for the effort to matter… and i get one that works just the way i want, with a proper “song mode” just because the robots can write the code there is still a lot of work to make something worth using, even for yourself. so i wouldnt look down on the efforts folks doing this stuff just because a robot is helping.
there is something that a lot of the comments about these things dont mention is that the soc that powers this has both wifi and bluetooth. both disabled in the default firmware… so not only can you tweak the thing to have different sounds or a new workflow or.. of course play doom… you can literally turn it into a wildly different class of device. 
its extremely fun and cool to see.
I was working on reverse engineering the SMK-37 Pro (same manufacturer, DX7 engine, MCU, DAC and screen, more physical controls) when all this kicked off. I’d done a quick scan to see if anyone was working on something similar, didn’t find anything, so I put my head down and got to work. By the time I had anything worth sharing, Baud Girl had already released her very polished firmware.
My interest waned somewhat when the flood of FM-1 firmware started, but I stuck around a while, trying to share a bit of knowledge here and there. It’s been fascinating seeing how easily misconceptions/hallucinations propagate when you’ve got a lot of people without much dev experience using LLMs to implement DSP code on a microprocessor.
It took a while for everyone to realise that the MCU is overclocked by the stock firmware, and then there was a lot of reluctance to increase the core voltage to match (per the datasheet), leading to a widespread belief that it was a deeply unreliable part being pushed to the edge by the stock firmware.
Another common misconception was that there’s no FPU, leading many firmware projects to strictly avoid any floating point code, when all they needed to do was add a compiler flag.
To be clear, as a professional software engineer, I reckon this is all kind of wonderful. Some may see a bunch of people taking a shortcut and writing code they don’t understand. I think of it as a bunch of people who may never have done something like this, getting excited about digging into what makes the tech around them work and learning at least something about how they can take control of that.
Meanwhile, if anyone wants to take control of what a Roland BMC does (think any mid-to-high-end Roland synth released since 2015 or so)… https://github.com/edco/mc-101-re
There was a guy at SuperCon with one of these M-Vave FM1 showing off a standalone USB Host firmware flasher made from an adafruit board.  
Maybe he sees this and shares that project info??
