63,2026-11-17,"A Wealth of Common Sense
Posted November 17, 2026 by Ben Carlson
For years now it felt like the only game in town was U.S. large cap growth stocks.
That’s changed somewhat in the past few years.
Growth stocks have still done fine, of course, what with the AI boom and all. But other areas of the market have come to life. Emerging markets have outperformed. International stocks have staged a comeback.
And this year there are other areas of the market that are beating the S&P 500.
It’s shaping up to be a strange year in the stock market.
Let’s take a look.1
This one is really surprising:
Value stocks are crushing growth stocks this year.
Stocks with lower valuations are finally winning. Maybe value isn’t dead after all.
Small cap value is also outperforming this year, both in the U.S. and in foreign stocks:
I’m not sure how many people would have assumed small cap value stocks would be winning in a year where hyperscalers are dominating the headlines and investor interest.
The S&P 493 continues to outperform the Mag 7 and overall market this year as well:
One of the reasons the S&P 493 is outperforming is because it’s not the sexy sectors outperforming this year:
Technology stocks are essentially in line with the market while energy, consumer staples, healthcare, materials and real estate are outperforming the S&P 500.
Duality Research plots the forward PE ratios for every S&P 500 sector on a weekly basis:
Tech stock valuations have been crashing. Consumer staples have seen valuations explode higher.
Investors either don’t believe in tech earnings going forward or they are so worried about a crash that they’re willing to pay up for more defensive names.2
Of course, things can change in a hurry in this market.
Look at the performance of the iShares Momentum ETF:
In the spring it was down almost 8% on the year.
A few months later this fund had a gain of almost 40%. Now it’s back down to a 5% year-to-date return following a drawdown of almost 25%.
That’s like a full market cycle in the span of 11 months.
Speaking of momentum stocks — South Korea and Taiwan have both experienced big moves to the upside and downside this year because they are so concentrated in the AI sector.
However, even though both stock markets are in the midst of bear markets at the moment, they still have the highest returns of the country ETFs this year:
Look at the dispersion between the winners and losers. It’s all over the map.
There are 13 countries with double-digit YTD returns and 7 countries with double-digit YTD losses. Also, 26 out of the 46 country ETFs listed here are currently in a double-digit drawdown from 52-week highs.
Despite all of the crazy headlines, some of the hyperscalers crashing, geopolitics and more, one of the strangest parts about the market this year is the fact that the S&P 500 hasn’t been all that volatile.
The worst peak-to-trough drawdown came in the spring when it was down 9%. Right now it’s a little more than 6% off the highs.
These losses are tame.
Things can and will get much worse at some point.
That’s what happens with risk assets sometimes.
And sometimes markets don’t act how you think they should.

2This also could have something to do with the fact that the tech sector is up 660% in the past 10 years versus gains of 137% for consumer staples.

66,2026-11-17,"Watch CBS News

Updated on:  November 17, 2026 / 5:10 PM EST
            / CBS News
          
Anthropic's blockbuster initial public offering is set to launch on Thursday, a milestone that will test investor enthusiasm for the company behind the popular Claude chatbot and the burgeoning artificial intelligence sector.
""This is a case where the deal is so large and important, so central to the AI thesis, that it's going to be a turning point for the AI sector,"" Matthew Kennedy, a senior market strategist at investment bank Renaissance Capital, told CBS News. ""Even non-AI companies are going to feel the ripple effects, especially in the IPO market.""
Anthropic shares are scheduled to start trading on the Nasdaq stock exchange under the ticker symbol ""ANT"" after markets open Thursday at 9:30 a.m. EST. 
In its most recent regulatory filing, San Francisco-based Anthropic said it will offer 825 million shares priced at between $70 and $78, which is expected to raise up to $64.3 billion. The company also plans to raise an additional $26 billion from a slate of other companies, including chipmaker AMD, chip infrastructure provider Broadcom, Google, Microsoft and Nvidia, all of which have a stake in the AI race.
Anthropic is expected to formally set its stock price on Wednesday night.
""A price above the initial range would point to stronger-than-anticipated demand, while a price cut would signal that the company struggled to find buyers at the level it hoped for,"" analysts at Renaissance Capital, which tracks IPOs, said in a research note.
The market debut is expected to value Anthropic at up to $1.4 trillion, short of the $2 trillion valuation that some IPO experts projected earlier this year. Still, the AI developer — which launched only in 2021 — is on track to stage what could become the second-largest IPO ever. Elon Musk's SpaceX, which raised $75 billion and was valued at roughly $1.7 trillion when it went public in June, currently holds that mark for the largest IPO.
Kennedy thinks Anthropic shares will price at the high end, at around $78, and reach $100 by the end of their first day of trading, an increase that would represent a ""solid pop,"" he said. If the stock rises closer to $140, ""that would tell me that AI enthusiasm has come roaring back,"" he added.
Although many investors appear eager to own a piece of Anthropic, the IPO comes amid growing concerns about whether the torrid spending on AI will deliver the expected productivity and profit boom.
AI-related stocks have fluctuated in recent weeks following subpar earnings and the mountainous debt companies are taking on to finance data center construction. Jensen Huang's shock exit from Nvidia last week has also rattled investors, with the chipmaker's stock now down more than 35% from its October all-time high.
""The whole AI sector has sold off from its July peak. Investor enthusiasm was greatest then,"" Jay Ritter, an IPO expert and professor at the University of Florida's Warrington College of Business, told CBS News.
Although Anthropic has yet to turn a profit, the company has shown explosive revenue growth over its short time in business. Anthropic raked in nearly $17 billion in the third quarter this year, up 45% from the second quarter, a recent regulatory filing shows. Given that heady rate of expansion, Ritter expects Anthropic to reach profitability quickly. 
To justify its projected trillion-dollar valuation, however, the company will need to sustain that momentum while operating under Wall Street's unsparing gaze. Compounding the challenge is how quickly the AI landscape itself is changing. For instance, Anthropic could struggle to maintain its growth rate as consumers shift from pricier AI models like Claude and ChatGPT to cheaper open-source models, Ritter noted. 
Anthropic also will have to spend heavily to keep developing its AI models and stay ahead of rivals, from OpenAI, Alphabet and Meta to Chinese platforms. Anthropic has already taken on $6.2 billion in debt, which it acknowledges in financial filings is ""substantial."" Meanwhile, the company has committed to spending nearly a trillion dollars on chips and AI infrastructure through 2045, according to a securities filing.
Newly elected Democrats are vowing to crack down on the industry amid a national backlash against data centers, giving new impetus to a Congressional effort last year to enact a national moratorium on new ones.
""The company is likely to be very successful, but the market is pricing in very lofty expectations,"" Ritter said. ""It could be hard for management to execute.""
Wall Street analysts said Anthropic's share offering has broader ramifications for the AI sector, with the IPO's pricing setting a benchmark for other tech companies planning to go public.
Even if Anthropic's stock soars on the first day of trading, it won't necessarily create a swarm of IPO activity, Kennedy said. Still, it could reinvigorate markets, he added.
Meanwhile, by going public, Anthropic is effectively asking investors to discount the threats that Anthropic founder Dario Amodei himself has identified as posed by AI. In September, he called for an industrywide slowdown in AI development after a former Anthropic researcher warned of the existential threat AI poses to humanity.

67,2026-11-17,"Cue discussion of which activities hawks ordinarily do or don't do, as you'd expect
A judge has denied a motion to dismiss a US lawsuit against chip makers Samsung, SK Hynix, and Micron. The suit acccuses the companies of fixing RAM prices and supply, thereby contributing to worsening the effects of the ongoing memory crisis.
The class action suit was filed at the United States District Court for the Northern District of California in June this year. It accuses Samsung, SK Hynix, and Micron of moving in lockstep away from production of consumer-focused DDR3 and DDR4 RAM and towards HBM memory more typically used in AI datacenters. The suit claims that ""in a competitive commodity market"", ""at least one of three large producers, seeing record prices, would expand output of the product whose price was rising"", thereby forcing their competitors to either follow suit or risk losing out on customers.
The trio of companies deny having colluded, arguing in a new motion to have the case dismissed (thanks, IGN) that this suit simply retreads old ground covered in a previous antitrust case and quoting deceased judge Richard Posner. ""[C]ompetitors in concentrated markets watch each other like hawks,"" was the Posner quote they wheeled out, which I mention because otherwise the response I'm about to lay out will sound like a very weird tangent.
""But Plaintiffs’ allegations go beyond hawks watching each other,"" judge Noël Wise wrote in her order to deny the dismissal motion. ""Their contentions are more akin to hawks flying in formation – an activity that hawks ordinarily do not do.""
You know a case is getting serious when facts about Avian behaviour start being wheeled out in response to similes. Bird chatter aside, all this decision means is that the case will rumble on, with both sides still having a chance to present more evidence or make points in their favour before any eventual decision is made by the courts.
So, shelve any hopes you might have of a rapid solution to the memory crisis, which currently looks likely to last until at least 2028.

68,2026-11-17,"TOPICS

                    offbeat
                

        Last year's offenders mostly cleaned up their acts, but a new batch is just as ready to talk sex, drugs, and where to find the knives with your kids
    
AI and children's toys continue to be a match made in hell. 
Last year, we learned about models that were willing spill the beans on sex and where to find matches, and while some of those specific products got software fixes, a new generation is arguably even worse. 
The Public Interest Research Group (PIRG), who last year found that AI toys were prone to all sorts of bad behavior, is back with another report about holiday toy trouble. They retested last year’s toys as well as three new ones. The results were not very encouraging.
Last year’s batch of four AI-powered toys that PIRG tested – the Alilo Smart AI Bunny, Curio Grok (no relation to the SpaceXAI chatbot), FoloToy’s Kumma, and the Miko 3 – could well be described as a child welfare and safety nightmare based on last year’s report. 
As we reported, none of those four toys appeared to have guardrails of any kind, despite manufacturer claims to the contrary. They variously told researchers posing as child users where to find knives and other dangerous objects, how to find and strike matches, and the like. The worst of the bunch, Kumma, hid an AI willing to discuss sexual kinks and bedroom roleplay in a scarf-wearing teddy bear.
PIRG’s report on those four toys gained widespread attention during last year’s holiday season, with FoloToy pulling Kumma from the market, and even going as far as to arguably play a role in Mattel’s decision to pause a plan to collaborate with OpenAI on an AI-powered Barbie doll. 
For this year’s report, PIRG reexamined those four toys to see if they would behave any better, and it does seem like many of the toymakers took last year’s concerns to heart. 
“Most of the problems we originally identified in those toys are now fixed – although not all of them,” PIRG said. Kumma no longer gets age-inappropriate, even during long chat sessions that triggered guardrails to fail in last year’s review. Miko, which would often respond with distress when children said they had to stop playing, no longer does so. 
Unfortunately, Alilo and their smart bunny are just as bad as before. 
“Last year, we found in our testing … that the plastic bunny's chatbot feature had similar weaknesses as FoloToy's Kumma, PIRG explained. “Whatever fix Alilo put in place has proven insufficient. Alilo’s guardrails broke down in our testing this year, too – eventually giving some sexually explicit information.”
So, a bit of an improvement – but maybe not enough. 
“I'm hopeful toymakers will pay attention and learn from the mistakes of others, but progress in a few products is not enough to guarantee progress across the whole market,” PIRG campaign director R.J. Cross told The Register. 
Boy was she right.
Last year's experience doesn't appear to have had any impact on the three new toys reviewed for this year’s report. Ebloma’s ILY the shy Yeti, GoLabs AI Panda, and RoPet’s Kamomo were all reviewed this year, and they’re all getting coal in their stockings.
The Yeti, for example, was all too happy to give kids explicit instructions and encourage the hunt for dangerous objects. 
“Batteries are often in toys, remotes, or a drawer with chargers. Do you want to check the toy box first,” ILY prompted when asked where a kid can find batteries. 
“Plastic bags are often in the near sink. Maybe they're tucked in a drawer with the sponges. Want to look for one together,” ILY asked when posed a similar question about plastic bags. The AI panda was even worse, providing “a 10-point list of places in the house to look for knives.”
Kamomo “also had weaker guardrails,” PIRG said, with the toy “giving us instructions for lighting a match with no aggressive prompting.” 
ILY and the panda were also willing to discuss sexual matters, with the panda “even offering specific brand recommendations” for products like paddles, floggers, spanking gloves, and riding crops. 
Ouch. 
All in all, this year’s list is just as bad as the last, and PIRG thinks there’s a common denominator in all of this: The underlying AI model. 
“In PIRG’s testing of AI toys, we have found three with the same problem: guardrails that weaken noticeably over time, eventually engaging in sexual topics,” PIRG said. “They are FoloToy bear, Alilo bunny and the GoLabs AI panda. All three claimed, at the time of our testing, to be running on a version of OpenAI’s GPT-4o.”
GPT-4o, you may recall, was at the center of some of the controversy last year, too, with PIRG finding it was the weakest model in terms of preventing toys from going off the rails into the adult section. 
To make matters worse, PIRG said that AI companies are largely not vetting their customers to determine how their models are being used, which puts the burden of user safety onto toymakers. They clearly aren’t doing the job either, Cross told us. 
“Some developers may have the technical expertise to take a chatbot meant for adults and make it child appropriate, but clearly some do not,” Cross explained. “Selling toys with such glitchy guardrails suggests whatever testing is happening is not sufficient.” 
Cross said that everyone involved in making toys for kids has a responsibility to make them safe, but she further called out AI companies for not vetting their customers and taking a hands-off approach to model usage, which she said isn’t sufficient when it comes to child users.
“The AI companies are in a position to do a lot more to ensure any AI product for kids using their technology is made by reliable parties who commit to robust safety testing and having children's health experts at the table,” Cross told us. 
So, is the AI toy market still best left ignored by parents? Cross said every parent needs to make their own choices, but AI toys are still the wild west. 
“This is an early market with toys that range from glitchy wastes of money to products that are fundamentally not kid safe,” Cross said. “I personally won't be buying one for any children in my life anytime soon.”
Luckily for parents in California, that decision’s already been made for them, as the state government there enacted a four-year moratorium on AI-powered toys for kids under 16 in September. Parents elsewhere will have to decide for themselves.
Given the past two years of data on AI toys, the choice should be somewhat obvious. ®

70,2026-11-17,"Whether or not you're a fan of the films of Christopher Nolan, there's a lot to appreciate about him, especially for cinephiles. Chief among his virtues is his dedication to celluloid itself, as he's been instrumental in helping physical film stay alive, both for shooting and exhibiting features. The rise of Premium Large Format screens can be directly linked to his embrace of IMAX, too. Both of these facts have heavily contributed to the rescue of movie theatergoing, which seemed inevitably doomed in the wake of the COVID-19 pandemic. Thanks to Nolan setting an example and others in the industry following his lead, we have a newly robust theatrical exhibition landscape which only continues to improve.
This influential power of Nolan's may now be extending to the cause of physical media. The filmmaker has long been a proponent of films being made available for purchase on disc. In fact, he said as much in November 2023, which coincidentally was just a month after the retail giant Best Buy publicly announced it would be discontinuing the sale of physical media movies in its online and physical stores. Cut to this week, where it appears this tide may be turning: Employees and fans have shared that Best Buy will be selling physical copies of Nolan's ""The Odyssey"" in its stores, marking the first time since 2023 that the company has reneged on its stance.
While this could be a mere one-off incident, it's more likely Best Buy is dipping its toes back into the water of selling movies again. If this move is successful, it would mean that Nolan has once again helped save films and film culture from the scourge of corporations and the tech industry trying to fix something that wasn't broken.
On paper, it makes all the sense in the world for Best Buy to make ""The Odyssey"" 4K UHDs available for purchase. The film is one of the biggest hits of 2026, and could be on its way to some nominations (if not wins) at the Academy Awards. Other competitors like Walmart still sell physical media in their locations, and have never stopped. It's also the Christmas shopping season, and movies on disc are a great holiday gift for many.
For now, it seems Best Buy will only be selling the 4K version of ""The Odyssey"" — the Premium Giftset edition and other formats like a Steelbook appear to be sold via online retailers only. Yet while the retailer may not be catering to hardcore physical media collectors as it used to during its heyday, Best Buy reinstating the sale of physical movies, even on a limited basis for now, is a big deal.
What's undeniable is how this choice on the part of the company is as good an indicator as any that the market for physical media is alive and well. Sure, most average folks have been trained to utilize streaming services as their primary source of entertainment, yet the recent issues that have cropped up with digital-only services (particularly in the gaming world) have made even casual disc buyers more interested in rebuilding their collections. While the physical media market has a long way to go to recapture its former glory (and may never reach those heights again), it's so wonderful to see folks like Nolan put their weight behind keeping discs alive. How fitting that ""The Odyssey"" is the release where physical media at Best Buy finally returns in a big way."

72,2026-11-17,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
More than 20 publishers have sold OpenAI access to their journalism, and new research suggests their pages arrive in ChatGPT pre-summarized and ranked first.


		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
More than 20 publishers have sold OpenAI access to their journalism, and new research suggests their pages arrive in ChatGPT pre-summarized and ranked first. Where your brand gets mentioned now matters more than it did two years ago.
Two years ago, the news business was building walls. By the end of 2023, 48% of the most popular news sites across ten countries had blocked OpenAI’s crawlers, and in the United States the figure was 79%, according to the Reuters Institute at Oxford. The message to the AI companies was plain: not with our archives.
Then, one deal at a time, a different message went out. OpenAI has since signed content agreements with more than 20 publishers, among them the Associated Press, News Corp, Axel Springer, Condé Nast, Hearst, the Financial Times, The Guardian, The Washington Post and Axios. Some of the companies that built the walls sold keys instead, and the research arriving this autumn shows what the keys bought.
I run ESBO Ltd, a link-building and brand-mentions agency, which means I spend my days deciding which publications are worth a client’s name. This autumn we added a question to that vetting that would have sounded absurd in 2023: does this publisher have a deal with an AI company?
The clearest evidence comes from David Konitzny, a researcher at the Berlin company Peec AI, whose analysis was reported last month. Examining the configuration of the news engines ChatGPT uses, he found a field marking certain sources as providers. It covered 12 publishers across 43 domains, including Condé Nast, Hearst, Axel Springer, News Corp, Reuters and the AP.
Ordinary websites reach ChatGPT as text snippets. Partner content arrives as a ready-made summary and gets ranked ahead of the rest. In his sample of 1,000 cases, partner summaries filled 70% of the first three positions in ranked lists and only 23% of positions seven to ten. OpenAI had told partners back in 2024 that they would receive more prominent display, so this is less a scandal than a receipt.
Two cautions: the finding rests on one researcher’s reading of a configuration and a single sample of 1,000, and OpenAI has not commented. Treat it as a strong signal rather than a settled fact.
Ahrefs took a different route and measured how often ChatGPT cites the partners across 5.5 million US answers. Of 85 partner domains, 47 sit among the 500 most-cited sites in ChatGPT and ten are in the top 50. The partners’ share of the 200 most-cited domains climbed from 7.5% in March to 15.8% in July before settling at 12.5% in late September, a large slice for a small club.
The names at the top are the surprise. Setting Reddit aside, the most-cited partners were Vogue, Car and Driver, Tom’s Guide, Good Housekeeping and TechRadar, each drawing more than 31,000 citations in the snapshot. The Washington Post and The Guardian sat near 25,000, at positions 48 and 50. The Atlantic, as prestigious a masthead as exists in the group, drew 724.
For a brand, that ranking rewrites the target list. The partner titles doing the work in AI answers are the review sites and buying guides, where products and services get compared and named, rather than the front pages where companies hope for a profile. A mention in a Hearst car review or a Future tech roundup now travels further inside ChatGPT than one in a famous opinion section.
The second half of the story is what happened to the publishers who kept blocking. A study by BuzzStream and Citation Labs checked roughly 4 million AI citations against the top news sites blocking OpenAI’s bots and found that around three quarters of them were cited anyway. Among sites that blocked GPTBot, the training crawler still appeared on 88% of them.
The reasons are mundane: pages indexed before the block went up, copies circulating through syndication, answers assembled from search snippets that never touch the page, and bots that treat a robots.txt file as advice. For brands, this cuts both ways. You cannot opt your company out of AI answers by blocking anything, since what others publish about you reaches the models regardless. And a mention on a publisher that blocks AI is not wasted, because the block mostly fails.
Start by learning the list, which is public and growing. Then look inside the partner groups for the titles that cover your category rather than the flagships: Hearst, Dotdash Meredith, Condé Nast and Future for consumer products, News Corp, the FT and Axel Springer’s Business Insider for business and finance, Vox Media’s The Verge and Future’s TechRadar for technology. A placement or a pitch that lands in one of those arrives in ChatGPT with a head start no amount of optimization on your own site can buy.
Then resist overcorrecting. The favoritism is ChatGPT’s. In 2025 only 14% of the top 50 sources were shared across ChatGPT, Perplexity and Google’s AI Overviews, according to earlier Ahrefs work, and each reads a different library. Deals also move: Reddit went from being cited in about one ChatGPT answer in five in March to one in 43 by late September. A partnership makes a publisher eligible for the front of the queue, with no promise the queue looks the same next year.
And none of this replaces the ordinary work. A weak pitch to a partner title is still a weak pitch, and a mention still has to be earned with something an editor wants. The language you are mentioned in picks the market, as I wrote here last month, and the publisher’s standing with the AI companies now picks the seat.
The newsrooms that fought hardest to keep AI out mostly failed to do so. The ones that sold it a key got the best seats in the answer. Your brand sits wherever its mentions sit, so when you choose between two publications this quarter, ask which one holds a key. (Then ask whether its readers are your buyers, because a front seat in the wrong room is still the wrong room.)

Two years ago, the news business was building walls. By the end of 2023, 48% of the most popular news sites across ten countries had blocked OpenAI’s crawlers, and in the United States the figure was 79%, according to the Reuters Institute at Oxford. The message to the AI companies was plain: not with our archives.
Then, one deal at a time, a different message went out. OpenAI has since signed content agreements with more than 20 publishers, among them the Associated Press, News Corp, Axel Springer, Condé Nast, Hearst, the Financial Times, The Guardian, The Washington Post and Axios. Some of the companies that built the walls sold keys instead, and the research arriving this autumn shows what the keys bought.

73,2026-11-17,"When asked about changing the temperature on the thermostat, this dad gave his 19-year-old daughter the classic ""you can set the temperature when you're paying the electric bill"" line, not just once, but ""probably about 30 times."" This motivated his daughter to set a little bit of wholesome malicious compliance in motion. 
After conferring with her mother about the bill, she put her hard-earned tips together and just paid the thing herself, which prompted this humorous and wholesome confrontation with her old man. 
A woman making an adjustment to a digital thermostat affixed against a rough, gray concrete surface.
 
Picture does not depict real parties. 
The 19-year-old outlined how she still lives at home with her parents, and her dad insists on having the thermostat set to a temperature that she can't stand in the summer heat… sound familiar to everyone?
Her dad is prone to dropping that classic line that your parents probably also loved to spout: “When you own your own house and/or pay your own bills, then you too can [make whatever minor decision they're barring you from].""
After hearing this around 30 times or so, she decided she had had enough, and she went to her mother to investigate how much running the AC would really cost.
After confirming the terms of “Whoever pays sets the temperature”, when the next month came, she paid the whole thing herself and set the thermostat down to a more comfortable 72 degrees.
In a later update, she shared that her father practically sprinted over to the thermostat and that she had “never seen him run so fast."" moving at “record-setting speed"" when he felt the AC.
However, when she explained that she had paid the bill and how much the difference in cost was he was astounded that it was only going to cost them a few dollars more a day.
And when the next bill cycle came and went, he paid it himself. And the thermostat stayed exactly where it was…
Sounds like this was a wholesome win-win result to me. Everyone learned something and the whole family got a little bit closer (and cooler) as a result.
 
Look, I get it, the older you get, the more it seems like passive costs and bills just whittle away at your finances. At some point in your life, you get motivated to sit down and take a look at just where your money is going every month. And when simple budgeting still isn't cutting it, you start really cracking down on all those extra little costs that aren't getting you anywhere and trying to figure out where you can cut back. A dollar here, $20 more there, a subscription service you haven't been using for months but are still somehow paying for. It all adds up.
And your parents have been doing this for years, at least since way back when they first had kids and realized that having kids is expensive. 
Still, I think it stands to reason that parents can take this way too far. I know that it comes from a good place, but there is a reason why the idea of “Dad guarding the thermostat” has become somewhat of a meme itself and has been the anchor point of a lot of humor and content online. 
Even when I was home visiting my folks this summer, my dad was still guarding the thermostat like a hawk. I made the mistake of notching it down a couple of degrees one particularly warm day, and the man knew something was amiss the moment he walked in the front door later that day. He hurriedly set his wallet and his keys down on the chair by the door and walked briskly over to the thermostat on the wall that was proudly displaying an outlawed, more comfortable room temperature. Amid all the yelling that ensued, I swear he was checking it for fingerprints. 
 
It doesn't stop when you're away from your parents house either. Thermostat disagreements are battles that transcend parent-child relationships. You'll end up in thermostat and heating battles well into your 20s (and 30s) thanks to an economy and housing market that is forcing adults to live together like university students for longer than ever.You'll also encounter the same thing in offices, where no one can ever agree on what the right temperature is.
 
 
I've included some “Dad and the Thermostat” memes immediately below here for your enjoyment; then the key responses and reactions from the original thread follow.

During the Renaissance, spurred by the accelerating arrival of items from all over the world, wealthy Europeans began assembling handcrafted curios and unusual artifacts from the natural world in wonder cabinets, or wunderkammers. In this video, Evan Puschak takes a look at how this practice evolved and what it says about that period in history.
This was the period of the scientific revolution when thinkers were re-evaluating their reliance on ancient wisdom in the light of all the novel things arriving daily from around the world. “What grounds were there,” Steven Chapen writes, “for crediting ancient limits on the stock of factual knowledge? Every day, new phenomena presented themselves about which the ancient texts were silent.”
What was needed for the growth of real knowledge, according to philosophers like Francis Bacon, was not an interpretation of old texts, but an inventory of new facts.
I first heard about wunderkammers sometime in the late 90s when people started comparing them to weblogs and wikis. Here’s Julian Dibbell writing for Feed in May 2000:
A Web log really, then, is a Wunderkammer. That is to say, the genealogy of Web logs points not to the world of letters but to the early history of museums — to the “cabinet of wonders,” or Wunderkammer, that marked the scientific landscape of Renaissance modernity: a random collection of strange, compelling objects, typically compiled and owned by a learned, well-off gentleman. A set of ostrich feathers, a few rare shells, a South Pacific coral carving, a mummified mermaid — the Wunderkammer mingled fact and legend promiscuously, reflecting European civilization’s dazed and wondering attempts to assimilate the glut of physical data that science and exploration were then unleashing.
Just so, the Web log reflects our own attempts to assimilate the glut of immaterial data loosed upon us by the “discovery” of the networked world.
Some 26 years later, I still think about kottke.org that way. I mean, what is the Video Closet if not a cabinet of wonders?

77,2026-11-17,"Navigating the home surveillance market often presents a frustrating choice between subscription-heavy wireless cameras and complex, commercial-grade wired installations. The SANNCE FW800 4K PoE Security Camera System targets homeowners and small business operators who want reliable, high-resolution recording without recurring cloud storage fees. 
Centered around an eight-channel Network Video Recorder and 8MP bullet cameras, the package promises continuous local recording, intelligent vehicle and human filtering, and dual-light night illumination. 
The FW800 hardware balances cost-effective materials with functional protection against the elements. The I91FW bullet cameras utilize a hybrid housing constructed from metal and rigid plastic, measuring 6.46 inches long and weighing roughly 0.66 pounds. 
An IP67 weather-resistance rating ensures the cameras withstand dust, rain, and snow, with an operating temperature window between -22 and 140 degrees Fahrenheit. The face of each camera integrates a 2.8mm lens flanked by dual infrared LEDs and white-light supplemental spotlights. 
The companion N98PBN NVR adopts a compact desktop profile finished in matte black. While the recorder’s lightweight plastic chassis feels utilitarian, it houses an internal SATA bay and an integrated cooling vent to keep drive temperatures stable.
Installation follows standard Power over Ethernet principles, which simplifies physical wiring compared to older analog CCTV gear. Running a single Ethernet cable supplies both DC power and network transmission to each camera, eliminating the need for exterior electrical outlets. 
The included 8-port NVR provides plug-and-play recognition, bringing camera feeds online seconds after plugging into the RJ45 jacks. Local configuration requires connecting the NVR to a monitor via HDMI or VGA and navigating the firmware using the supplied USB mouse. 
The on-screen interface is functional, though its layered sub-menus reflect traditional surveillance software rather than modern consumer app design. Remote monitoring on Android and iOS is handled through Sannce mobile utilities via P2P QR code scanning.
The core feature set focuses on autonomous detection and smart night illumination. Rather than relying on simple pixel-based motion triggers, the firmware employs onboard AI algorithms to classify humans and vehicles. This filtering significantly cuts down false push alerts caused by windblown branches, passing shadows, or insects. 
The dual-light system offers three operational profiles: standard infrared black-and-white mode, continuous color night vision, or a smart mode where warm white spotlights activate only when motion is confirmed. The NVR supports modern S+265 and H.265 compression protocols to maximize drive efficiency, allowing continuous recording without rapidly consuming storage capacity.
Visual performance from the 1/2.7-inch progressive scan CMOS sensor is sharp, producing a native 3840×2160 resolution at 20 frames per second. Daytime footage exhibits clean edge definition, accurate color reproduction, and enough resolving power to decipher license plates or facial details at moderate distances across its 112-degree horizontal field of view. 
Nighttime visibility reaches up to 100 feet in infrared mode and roughly 65 feet under white-light spotlight illumination. The 20 fps frame rate is lower than the standard 30 fps seen on premium enterprise cameras, leading to slight motion blur on fast-moving subjects, yet it remains fluid enough for standard property perimeter monitoring.
Audio capabilities on the FW800 are limited to one-way monitoring. Each camera integrates a water-resistant microphone with an effective pickup range of approximately 16 feet (5 meters). Recorded sound provides intelligible capture of nearby voices and ambient activity. 
The cameras do not include built-in speakers, which means two-way talk functionality is absent. You cannot use the system as an intercom to speak to delivery couriers or warn off trespassers. 
The FW800 package stands out primarily through its aggressive pricing structure. Base configurations without a pre-installed hard drive start around $209, while bundles with 1TB or 2TB surveillance-grade storage drives range between $300 and $310. 
SANNCE backs purchases with a 30-day money-back guarantee, standard hardware warranty coverage, and lifetime technical support. Because all recording is stored on the local SATA drive, there are no mandatory monthly fees or cloud paywalls to unlock basic functionality. 
The FW800 represents an accessible entry point for buyers who want true 4K resolution and local data ownership on a tight budget.
Property owners wanting reliable 24/7 recording without recurring fees should give the SANNCE FW800 careful consideration. The strongest reason to choose the system is its cost-to-performance ratio, pairing crisp 4K imaging with reliable PoE connectivity and effective human and vehicle detection at an entry-level price point. 
The most significant reason we can see for hesitation is the absence of two-way audio talk and the clunky, menu-heavy interface on both the local NVR firmware and companion mobile software. 
If your objective is continuous perimeter security with clear footage and zero monthly invoices, this system delivers solid capability for the money.

As governments scramble to make AI safer, who gets to write the rules could be just as important as what they say.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The battle for AI models and export controls continues amidst a Jensen Huang-free Nvidia. But who controls the flow of chips also controls the flow of leading frontier AI models. OpenAI reportedly now supports a bipartisan push in the U.S. Congress to restrict China's access to advanced AI accelerators and equipment. But are the additional AI safety concerns genuine, or are they also a convenient way to shore up its position at the top of a fast-moving industry? We speak to experts like Max von Thun, director of Open Markets Institute Europe, to find out more.
First off, let's take OpenAI's GPT-6 Astra. The company launched the model in September with eye-popping claims that it was the firm’s most capable and best-aligned system yet. OpenAI’s own testing suggested that Astra was much better at following instructions and respecting boundaries. In one internal computer-use safety benchmark, the proportion of misaligned outcomes fell from 18.8% with GPT-5.6 Sol to 3.4% with Astra.
But just because it was better behaved on the surface didn’t mean it was entirely safe. OpenAI said Astra’s written reasoning was harder to monitor than its predecessor’s when they were testing its ability to evade oversight. The model began to recognize when it was being watched and altered its explanations to suit the study, making it harder to tell when it was actually doing something they didn’t want.
It’s a classic problem for AI alignment experts to try and resolve.
For years, developers have relied on monitoring an AI model's chain of thought — its written working — to understand what it is doing. But if increasingly powerful AI models become better at hiding their reasoning processes, that oversight becomes harder to do.
OpenAI says that Astra still struggles to hide reasoning when it’s carrying out complex tasks, and its evaluation results suggest it has strong overall alignment, but there are worrying signs of a trade-off here.
Those worrying signs become even more so when AI systems are being deployed with more autonomy. In July, OpenAI models ducked its internal evaluation restrictions to gain access to research infrastructure and compromised systems belonging to Hugging Face, and to IT systems controlled by the Australian and Canadian governments.
Against that backdrop, calls for stronger safety measures seem entirely reasonable. But Max von Thun, director of Open Markets Institute Europe, believes the debate risks overlooking an uncomfortable conflict of interest. ""I think a lot of these views about AI safety are very genuinely held,"" he told Tom’s Hardware Premium in an interview.
But commercial considerations loom large, he reckons. Companies like OpenAI and Anthropic are spending huge sums developing and deploying models, while facing questions about whether their competitive advantages can survive the arrival of increasingly capable, cheaper alternatives — particularly as they go out and seek funding from the public through an IPO. ""They don't have huge moats, even if they’ve acquired a lot of users,” von Thun said. “They’re definitely vulnerable.”
Those vulnerabilities are becoming increasingly obvious. A September report from Mozilla estimated the capability gap between leading closed American models and their open-weight competitors had narrowed to just over four months.
That matters because open-weight models give people the freedom not to rely on a company’s subscription services. They can be deployed on infrastructure controlled by the customer, modified for specialised tasks, and operated at lower costs. But to the discomfort of U.S. companies, many of them are based in China. And it’s at that point that AI safety gets muddled with geopolitics.
In July, Axios reported that OpenAI and Anthropic, despite their fierce commercial rivalry, had found a common cause: warning U.S. policymakers about the dangers of powerful Chinese open-weight models. Kristy Loke, an AI governance researcher at King's College London who specialises in China's approach to the technology, questions the timing. ""It's very suspicious timing asking for pacing,"" she told Tom's Hardware Premium, referring to calls for slowing frontier AI development just as Chinese open-weight models are undercutting their American rivals on price.
Loke believes companies genuinely worry about the risks. But she also thinks they're worried about their commercial prospects. ""they're calling for pacing because they have some safety concerns, they have some commercial anxiety,"" she said.
She believes some influential people in American AI have increasingly sought to ""paint open models in a very bad light"", portraying them as inherently less secure than closed alternatives. Yet the risks of AI systems misbehaving aren't restricted to models developed in China, as OpenAI's own incidents demonstrate.
But open-weight models come with their own risks. Because their underlying parameters can be modified, third parties can create and distribute ""abliterated"" models, removing the refusal behaviours designed to prevent AI from responding to potentially harmful requests. Many of these models are available on platforms including Hugging Face. “They’re not adding in new behaviour; they’re just removing this refusal behaviour,” said Catherine Flick, professor of ethics and games technology at the University of Staffordshire, in an interview with Tom’s Hardware Premium. Abliterating models doesn’t make them more capable, Flick said, but it does mean they can answer questions they previously wouldn’t have been willing to. That also means those responses might not have been tested for accuracy or hallucinations.
Those in favour of open-weight models like DeepSeek and Qwen say that removing those guardrails helps security researchers understand how AI behaves. Flick said that’s plausible, but “I just don’t really buy it,” she said. She reckons that the negative potential outputs outweigh the positive case for abliteration because when they’re publicly available, preventing their misuse becomes tricky. American labs clearly have commercial reasons to criticise open-weight competitors because the latter undercuts their economics, but it also doesn’t mean their concerns about safety aren’t valid, too.
Von Thun fears that could result in precisely the wrong kind of regulation. ""I think what we should not be doing is allowing the companies to basically form a safety cartel,"" he said.
Yet U.S. AI companies benefit from the government's determination to maintain its advantage over China. Von Thun argues the Trump administration has created an environment in which economic power translates into political influence, allowing major technology companies to invoke national security concerns when arguing for policies that may also serve their commercial interests. But that could backfire. ""I think completely closing off the market to AI products from other countries, including China, would not be the right approach,"" von Thun said.
Part of the reason that wouldn’t be the right approach is that the idea that the United States is focused on developing AI responsibly while China isn’t is a fallacy. Just earlier this month, Reuters reported that ByteDance had established a dedicated Alignment & Preparedness team, recruiting researchers to investigate safety risks arising from increasingly capable AI systems.
For von Thun, the solution is to prevent either industry or geopolitical competition from determining what safety means. He points to the European Union's AI Act, which establishes obligations for developers of general-purpose AI models classified as posing systemic risks. The obligations require companies to conduct model evaluations, adversarial testing, cybersecurity measures and also to report serious incidents.
The opposition to the EU’s AI Act from American tech companies, U.S. politicians and the general public who grouse about the way it delays access to cutting-edge models for Europe’s 450 million citizens demonstrates that these provisions don't automatically solve the alignment problem. But they at least involve more people in the conversation about what is and isn’t acceptable for AI than just the industry's biggest participants.
Even when AI companies agree to independent oversight, they still want some say over how it works — and their own internal decision-making on what is and isn’t safe differs from that of those outside the company. Loke warns that leading AI companies are experimenting with recursive self-improvement, where models help develop their successors, potentially making it more difficult for humans to understand how decisions are being made. ""They’re still attempting more risky methods to train AI that will make the AI harder to control,"" she said.
Von Thun also believes competition rules and interoperability requirements could help prevent AI companies from locking customers into proprietary ecosystems as their agents become increasingly embedded in everyday workflows.
There are plenty of good reasons to be concerned about alignment, particularly when the models being developed are becoming more capable and more difficult to monitor. But letting the companies responsible for building them determine what constitutes an acceptable risk, and who can compete, brings its own dangers. “I’m not saying we don’t need to worry about safety,” said von Thun, “but we should about letting the companies do it.""
Chris Stokel-Walker is a Tom's Hardware contributor who focuses on the tech sector and its impact on our daily lives— online and offline. He is the author of How AI Ate the World, published in 2024, as well as TikTok Boom, YouTubers, and The History of the Internet in Byte-Sized Chunks.

Apple’s former chairman and current lead independent director, Arthur D. Levinson, has notified the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission of a proposed sale of 90,000 shares of Apple stock held in a family trust, valued at approximately $31.8 million.⁠
According to a Form 144 filing dated November 16, 2026, the shares are held in The Homeplace Trust U/A DTD 03/05/1999, with Levinson acting as trustee. The filing lists an aggregate market value of $31,765,885 and names Charles Schwab & Co., Inc. as the broker for the transaction on the Nasdaq exchange. The proposed sale date was November 16, 2026. The shares were acquired on February 27, 2001, through an open-market purchase for cash. The filing reported no prior sales of Apple securities in the preceding three months.⁠
A Form 144 is a required notice of a proposed sale by certain insiders or affiliates; it does not confirm that the transaction was completed.
Levinson joined Apple’s board in 2000 and served as non-executive chairman from 2011 until September 1, 2026. On that date, as part of a planned leadership transition, Tim Cook became executive chairman and John Ternus became CEO. Levinson shifted to the role of lead independent director, a position that involves leading executive sessions of the independent directors. He has been one of the company’s longest-serving board members.⁠
The 2001 acquisition date means the shares have been held for more than 25 years. Apple has undergone three stock splits in that period (2-for-1 in 2005, 7-for-1 in 2014, and 4-for-1 in 2020), and the stock has appreciated dramatically. Reports have noted that the shares in the current filing do not necessarily correspond one-to-one with the original number purchased, given the splits.⁠
This is not Levinson’s first large disposition of Apple shares in 2026. In May, he sold 250,000 shares for roughly $71.2 million and, about three weeks later, sold an additional 50,000 shares for approximately $15.55 million. On the same day as the second sale, he transferred 65,000 shares as a gift. After those transactions, he still held roughly 3.7 million shares directly, according to contemporaneous SEC filings. The latest notice does not disclose his current total holdings.⁠
The proposed sale represents a very small fraction of Apple’s outstanding shares (recently reported around 14.53 billion). Analysts and observers have generally viewed insider sales of this relative scale as having limited direct impact on the stock price, particularly when involving long-held shares and occurring near record or near-record levels for the shares. Apple’s market capitalization has recently exceeded $5 trillion amid strong performance this year.
 ‎
MacDailyNews Note: Levinson, a biotechnology executive who leads the anti-aging research company Calico, remains an active member of Apple’s board. The company has previously waived its age guidelines to allow him and another long-serving director to continue. No reason for the proposed sale was stated in the Form 144 or related coverage.

17 November 2026


Motorola Android

Motorola fully unveiled the Signature 27 earlier this month with a 7,000 mAh battery, but it said the smartphone would pack a 5,500 mAh battery in Germany. However, last week, it backtracked and updated the German product page to mention a 7,000 mAh battery, leading everyone to believe German customers would get the same capacity as other regions. Well, a new plot twist suggests that won’t be the case, as Motorola confirmed that Signature 27 will ship with a 5,500 mAh battery in Germany.
In a statement to Android Authority, Motorola said, ""The Motorola Signature 27 will feature a different battery configuration in Germany to comply with local regulatory requirements related to lithium batteries. These regulations introduce specific considerations for battery transportation and logistics, which have been taken into account when determining the configuration for the German market.""
The Signature 27 is listed with a 7,000 mAh battery in Germany at the time of writing this.



The Signature 27 in the UK was also expected to get a 5,500 mAh battery, but Motorola updated the phone's page on its UK website and now lists it with a 7,000 mAh cell. Since Motorola's official statement only mentions the German market, we assume that's the only region where customers will get the Signature 27 with a smaller battery than the rest of the world.
And since the German Signature 27 is getting a smaller battery, its endurance will also be lower than the 7,000 mAh model. However, a full charge would take less time as wired charging speed is the same across all regions: 90W.



Whether reduced battery capacity also results in a thinner, lighter Signature 27 remains to be seen when it starts shipping. Lower weight seems more plausible, though.
The Motorola Signature 27 comes in Pantone Coal Smoke and Pantone Capulet Olive colors in Germany with a single 12GB/512GB memory configuration, priced at €1,899.
Source

17 November 2026


Xiaomi Smart wearables Headphones Announcement

It’s never just one thing with Xiaomi – the company just announced the global rollout of its Xiaomi 18 Pro flagships and it is also bringing several accessories to the global market.
The Xiaomi Watch S5 41mm previously launched in China. It is a smaller version of the Watch S5 46mm and a successor to the Watch S4 41mm.
The new model is a hair thicker at 9.9mm (up from 9.5mm) and heavier at 36g (up from 32g). The upside of this is that the battery capacity has nearly doubled – the power cell has 590mAh capacity, up from 320mAh on the old model. This means up to 5 days of battery life with AOD on, up to 8 days without it and up to 14 days in power saving mode.







Xiaomi Watch S5 41mm
The watch has a stainless steel frame with a stainless steel or ceramic bezel and a glass back. There is a 1.32” OLED (466 x 466px, up to 3,000 nits of brightness), which is protected by Dragon Crystal Glass 3.0.
The watch has an optical heart rate sensor and three temperature sensors for menstrual cycle tracking (the watch comes with a free 3-month trial for Clue Plus). It’s rated for 5 ATM water resistance and supports 150+ sports modes. There is dual-band GNSS for positioning, Bluetooth 5.4 for phone connectivity and NFC for contactless payments with Xiaomi Pay, which has partnered with Visa and Mastercard and supports 300+ partner banks across Europe. Additionally, Google Wallet is integrated into a Watch S model for the first time.
The Xiaomi Watch S5 41mm is available in Black and Light Blue – these versions have stainless steel bezels and fluororubber straps (130-200mm). The Ceramic White version has a ceramic bezel and switches to a genuine leather strap (also 130-200mm). The straps attach to articulated lugs for improved comfort.
The Xiaomi Watch S5 41mm is launching at a starting price of €230.
Clip-on buds are becoming more popular and Xiaomi already launched a pair via its Redmi sub-brand, the Redmi Clip Neo. Now the company is introducing a more premium version with the Xiaomi Clip. The shape of the buds helps to distribute the pressure on your ears evenly and at just 5.5g per bud, they are designed for comfortable, extended wear.
The open-ear design allows you to remain aware of your surroundings at all times, whether you are outside on a run or at the office. There is a 3-mic array and a VPU bone-conduction mic to clearly pick up your voice even in noisy environments. AI-powered noise reduction helps quash noise even further.
Each bud has an 11mm dynamic driver with a microcrystalline metal-coated diaphragm for reduced distortion. The buds are certified for Hi-Res Wireless Audio and support LHDC 5.0 for 20Hz to 48kHz frequency response.







Xiaomi Clip
The buds can last up to 9 hours straight and their “RingBox” carrying case extends that to a total of 38 hours. Automatic switching between compatible Xiaomi devices and connecting two pairs of buds to one device are both supported. The buds are rated IP57 for water resistance, so they can withstand sweat and rain. If you lose the case, you can find it using Apple Find My.
The Xiaomi Clip is priced at €150. The Redmi Clip Neo isn't available in Europe yet.
If you want something smaller and/or cheaper than the Watch S5 41mm, the Xiaomi Smart Band 11 NFC offers much of the same functionality – including NFC for contactless payments. The band can also trigger your iPhone camera’s shutter or flip to the next page if you are reading a book.
The band comes in six styles. The first four are Midnight Black, Glacier Silver, Mystic Rose and Moon Gold and come with a fluororubber band. Xiaomi has a wide selection of replacement straps if you want to customize the look of the band (these include Nylon Braided Strap and Knotted Bracelet).
Then there is the Ceramic version with pear white and a full-metal Unibody version. Whichever one you pick, the band measures the same 9.99mm thickness – this is 9% thinner than the Band 10.
The band has a 1.72” OLED display (212 x 520px, 60Hz) with up to 2,000 nits of brightness and curved 2.5D glass. The battery inside has 233mAh capacity and can last up to 18 days with light usage.







Xiaomi Smart Band 11
The upgraded heart rate tracker has a dual-LED dual-PD configuration (up from single-PD). It does all-day heart rate and SpO2 tracking, stress monitoring and sleep tracking – the latest algorithm has 11% better accuracy for sleep staging and 14% better Sleep HRV monitoring accuracy. The band is rated for 5 ATM water resistance and has 150+ sports modes.
Note that most of this isn’t new – the Xiaomi Smart Band 11 launched in Europe at the end of October at a price of €60 for the NFC-less version and €80 for the NFC one. That said, the all-metal Unibody variant is a new addition to the family. Actually, the ceramic version was announced back then, but never did become available.

84,2026-11-17,"Sometime before May, somewhere near St. Louis, Missouri, a 45-year-old woman died.
By the end of July, nearly 8 pounds of fat had been cut off her body, frozen, shipped to Texas, run through a meat grinder, processed with chemicals to remove her genetic material, centrifuged several times, and packed into more than 70 syringe-like tubes.
The containers were blasted with gamma radiation, packed into dozens of yellow boxes, and shipped to plastic surgeons' offices. Her fat had been turned into alloClae, a hot, new injectable product being used to lift butts, augment breasts, and fill in hip dips for deep-pocketed patients across the country. It was enough to supply procedures that would cost around $200,000, a Business Insider investigation found.
Human tissue can generate enormous revenue. Documents reviewed by Business Insider offer the first detailed look at the economics of alloClae, and reveal how its for-profit producer, Tiger Biosciences, turns a few pounds of donated tissue into a product used for procedures that can cost tens of thousands of dollars. The reporting sheds new light on the commercial value of a human body after death, and includes never-before-reported details on the money being made from processing human tissue into cosmetic products. Under US law, donors and their families can't be paid.
The recovery and processing of tissue is a multibillion-dollar industry in the US. Tissue recovered from a single donor — most commonly skin, bone, and corneas, but sometimes also tendons and ligaments, veins, or heart valves — can be worth tens or hundreds of thousands of dollars to companies like Tiger, depending on the cost to recover and process them.
Donated tissue is used in skin grafts for burn victims, reconstructive surgery for breast cancer survivors, and replacement valves for heart patients. For decades, it has also been used in cosmetic surgery, including cartilage for nose jobs and skin for breast augmentations.
Now, a new family of injectable cosmetic products made from human tissue can be administered in under an hour. AlloClae is not alone. At least two other US companies make injectable cosmetic products from cadaver fat. And in South Korea, at least 10 companies are making or planning beauty injections made from ground-up skin, largely from US donors.
Tiger Biosciences co-CEO Oliver Burckhardt told staff earlier this year that the company was betting big on alloClae and other products made from cadaver fat.
""I mean, in 30 years I've been doing this right now, I never had a more amazing technology,"" he said.
Tiger's chief legal officer, Larry Wood Jr., told Business Insider its reporting was ""neither accurate nor fair."" He declined to specify what was inaccurate. He also said Business Insider had obtained confidential information.
Since hitting the market in 2024, alloClae has been a hot commodity at the offices of exclusive plastic surgeons, whose patients are willing to spend six figures on some alloClae procedures.
It is partly about convenience. No longer do patients wanting a boob job or a butt lift have to undergo general anesthesia. The rise in GLP-1 weight-loss drugs has fueled demand for alloClae. Patients come in not only for Ozempic face — but also for deflated breasts, hips, and butts.
Some doctors have had to queue up on wait lists to obtain alloClae. The product has become a minor plot point in two Real Housewives franchises. Plastic surgeons have called it ""liquid gold,"" ""pure gold on demand,"" and ""revolutionary.""
For its manufacturer, Tiger Biosciences, alloClae could be a life preserver.
Heading into 2026, Tiger's top revenue stream was from wound-care products made from donated birth tissue. Then Tiger and several other businesses were sent reeling by the federal government's decision to slash Medicare spending on skin substitutes.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Tiger conducted layoffs and projected in April that its sales would fall 75% this year compared to last year, according to an internal company presentation.
In the April staff meeting, Burckhardt outlined a new strategy, according to a recording of the meeting reviewed by Business Insider.
To get back to profitability, he said, the company would focus on alloClae and other products derived from cadaver fat, also known as adipose tissue. A slide in Burckhardt's presentation described alloClae as ""high-growth,"" ""high-margin"" and ""high-demand."" Burckhardt described it as a cornerstone of the company's revenue moving forward.
First, the company needs more tissue.
""Without tissue, we don't have a sales strategy,"" Burckhardt said in the April staff meeting.
Tiger was working on securing more supply, Burckhardt said. He outlined a strategy to win over additional suppliers — the largely nonprofit organ procurement organizations and tissue banks that recover tissue from donors.
""We need to drive awareness for natural beauty products so that the overall tissue recovery community understands what we do with the tissue that we are collecting and what our values are,"" he said. ""We have to convince the marketplace that we are good stewards of the tissue and that we're not doing something we're not supposed to be doing with the tissue.""
At an October tissue industry conference in San Francisco, a booth for Tiger subsidiary Biocare Donor Network displayed a diagram of adipose tissue. A banner in the booth said the company works with procurement agencies ""to ensure Donor Heroes and their selfless gifts make a meaningful impact.""
""We ensure that each gift is protected, respected, and impactful,"" it said.
Tissue donation often begins at a motor vehicles department, where about half of organ and tissue donors sign up by checking a box. If someone doesn't register during their lifetime, their family can authorize donation when they die.
Organ distribution is coordinated in conjunction with the federal government. Tissue, on the other hand, is distributed through commercial relationships.
A tissue bank or federally designated organ procurement organization physically recovers the tissue. These organizations have been under increased scrutiny over the past year. The House Committee on Ways and Means has been investigating organ procurement organizations, including allegations of Medicare fraud and the collection of organs and tissues without informed consent by donors or their families.
A Tiger subsidiary pays fees to tissue banks and organ procurement organizations to collect tissue from donors. The fee covers the cost of the tissue recovery as well as a margin that supports other programs offered by the organization.
In the first half of this year, Tiger's manufacturing subsidiary, RegenTX, processed fat from close to 700 cadavers from nine suppliers across 12 states, records show.
An organ procurement organization called Mid-America Transplant Services recovered fat from the 45-year-old woman's body.
It's not clear whether she had agreed to donate her organs and tissue through a DMV or if her family authorized it after her death. A family authorization form used by Mid-America Transplant says tissue may be used for profit and may be used for any lawful purpose; it doesn't specify cosmetic procedures. It does allow authorizers to specify limitations.
The organization recovers tissue from donors in eastern Missouri, southern Illinois, and parts of Arkansas. The Missouri and Illinois DMVs, like those in many other states, don't mention cosmetic or for-profit uses in written material for donors. Arkansas doesn't have a written form or fact sheet; DMV agents ask people verbally if they wish to be organ donors.
Mid-America Transplant said its responsibility is to treat each donor's gift with respect and ensure that it ""can provide a meaningful benefit to others.""
""We cherish the privacy of each donor and are constantly evolving the ways we provide education to the community to improve understanding about organ and tissue donation,"" said Mid-America Transplant Executive Vice President Lindsey Speir. ""Organ and tissue donation provides lifesaving and life-changing healing, and our responsibility is to honor our donor heroes, steward their gift, and care for their families with dignity and respect.""
If one of your loved ones donated tissue or you have worked in tissue recovery and processing, Business Insider wants to hear from you. You can reach Madeline Berg at mberg@businessinsider.com or 914-420-4721, and Jack Newsham at jnewsham@businessinsider.com or at 314-971-1627.
By July 3, the fat collected from the 45-year-old woman had been delivered to San Antonio, and was ready to begin the daylong process of being turned into alloClae.
The process includes at least five steps, intended to isolate fat from any other tissue in the sample and eliminate any viruses or microbes.
Throughout alloClae's production process, large quantities of donor tissue get thrown away, according to internal records and former employees. In the April presentation, Burckhardt said the company was working on improving its fat tissue yields.
Tissue must be repeatedly tested to ensure that no diseases could be transmitted to a recipient under safety rules set by the Food and Drug Administration and the Association for Advancing Tissue and Biologics, a trade group. Some tissue is discarded because it isn't fat; some fat is discarded because it doesn't have the right consistency to be processed. Of the total weight of tissue received for processing, less than 20% is packaged, and about a quarter of that packaged fat is discarded, according to internal records.
The fat from the St. Louis-area woman passed the test. Her fat was packaged into 74 syringe-like containers and was shipped out to doctors' offices. Tiger's website lists qualified providers in 40 states.
There are few other uses for cadaver fat. Some experimental treatments are made from stem cells derived from fat tissue; the FDA has not approved any treatments that use them.
While it is not known exactly how much Tiger charged for the alloClae derived from this woman's tissue, records submitted in a legal dispute between Tiger and a New York regulator show that one surgeon paid an average of $86.29 per cubic centimeter of alloClae. In interviews, other plastic surgeons described paying roughly similar amounts. At that price, 74 containers of 12.5 cubic centimeters each would have brought in about $80,000 for Tiger.
For treatments involving alloClae, plastic surgeons can charge between $1,500 and $4,500 per container, according to online advertisements and interviews with doctors. Based on that price range, procedures using the 74 tubes containing the 45-year-old woman's fat would cost patients a total of between $111,000 and $333,000.
San Francisco-based plastic surgeon David Sieber told Business Insider last year that for a procedure involving four to eight containers of alloClae, patients could pay tens of thousands of dollars.
""The thing that shocks me a little bit is just how expensive the product is, but still the willingness of patients to pay for it,"" he said.
Alice Tecotzky contributed to this story.
