Google became one of the most valuable companies in the world by revolutionizing how humanity finds information. Now, a new generation of AI rivals is challenging its dominance and pushing Google to spend a fortune to secure its future. Can Google embrace this disruptive technology without breaking the business that made it rich?
Former Google executive Marissa Mayer remembers when the company was just a scrappy startup, working out of a small office above a bike shop in Palo Alto, Calif. She joined the company back in 1999, fresh out of nearby Stanford with a master's in computer science.
"" It's pretty wild. When I interviewed at Google, there were just seven people there,"" Mayer tells Planet Money. By the time she got hired, a month or two later, she became its 20th employee.

                Then Google vice president of search Marissa Mayer in 2009.
                
                    
                    ROBYN BECK/AFP via Getty Images/AFP
                    
                
hide caption

That year, Google brought in just over $200,000 in annual revenue. By 2022, the eve of the AI boom, the company — by then reorganized under a parent company called Alphabet — was generating more than $280 billion. That's more than a millionfold increase.
Google made its fortune selling ads next to its search results. It monopolized internet searches so thoroughly that its name became a verb. And its search business became like an unstoppable money machine.
In 2012, Mayer left and became the CEO of Yahoo, trying to revive a competitor that Google had blown out of the water. But neither Yahoo nor any other rival managed to dethrone King Google. The company's dominion just kept expanding. Its share of the U.S. search-engine market grew from 80% in 2009 to nearly 90% in 2020.
For a time, it seemed like the only forces that could cut this juggernaut down to size were politicians or antitrust lawyers.
But the craziest part? The biggest threat to Google was maybe coming from inside the house! From what it was developing in its own labs — and the industrial race it would unleash.
Google plowed the profits from its search business into research and development of AI. And, in 2017, a team of eight Google employees developed the transformer, a breakthrough technology that made today's AI chatbots possible.
Mayer, who is now CEO of the AI startup Dazzle and remains a Google shareholder, sees Google's pioneering AI work as a reason for optimism about the company's future. ""This is not a technology that's happening to Google,"" she says. ""They helped invent this new technology.""
Yet, today, Google is spending enormous amounts to build its AI capabilities and fend off rivals. And it's overhauling its search business, using AI to answer questions directly instead of just providing links.
Wall Street is getting nervous. In late October, Alphabet released its most recent earnings report. The company's stock price plummeted more than 12% the next day — the biggest single-day percentage decline since the company went public in 2004.
If this whole AI transition goes sideways for the company, it's a bit like a mother giving birth to a baby and then that baby slapping her in the face and stealing money from her wallet.
Which brings us to… a classic business theory we've been thinking about recently: the innovator's dilemma.
The late Harvard Business School professor Clayton Christensen developed the theory in his 1997 book, The Innovator's Dilemma: When New Technologies Cause Great Firms to Fail.
Christensen theorized that the very thing that makes a business wildly successful can lead to its downfall in the face of a ""disruptive"" technology. He helped put disruption in Silicon Valley's vocabulary, long before it became a cliché.
The core innovator's dilemma is basically this: embrace a disruptive technology, and risk the profits you're making today. But if you fail to embrace it and let others run with it, you may end up risking your company's health tomorrow.
Today in the Planet Money newsletter we ask: has Google been caught in the innovator's dilemma? And can it pivot to a new technological era without sacrificing its enormous profits?
Google didn't just invent the transformer. Over the decades, its researchers made computers translate languages, recognize speech and images, drive cars autonomously, and even beat one of the world's best players at the game Go.
The company even developed an internal chatbot called Meena that employees played with years before AI chatbots became a sensation.
But they didn't release it.
Then, on Nov. 30, 2022, OpenAI released ChatGPT and offered a completely new way to find information and a glimpse of a future where people didn't need to go to Google to find answers anymore.
Google scrambled to respond, reportedly declaring a ""code red"" as ChatGPT captivated the masses.
So, first question, why wasn't Google the first out of the gate with something like ChatGPT?
Christensen's theory offers one possible explanation: When you're making tons of money from your existing business in search, why rush to release something that could undermine it? Why disrupt yourself?
His theory wasn't just that companies seek to protect their cash cow. It was that a company can get too focused on the best way to nurture and milk that cash cow. Like, dang, I love this cow. Then when a disruptive technology comes along, they might be like, wait, this won't help me milk my beautiful cow. No thanks.
In other words, a company's focus on its core business can lead it to not see a disruptive technology's market potential. It can create a kind of myopia.
Last year, Google's chief scientist, Jeff Dean, offered an explanation for why Google hadn't released something like ChatGPT sooner— and it suggested the company had fallen victim to the kind of myopia Christensen warned about. Dean told podcaster Dwarkesh Patel that they looked at their existing chatbots through the lens of their search business. You know, their cow.
""From a search perspective, you want to get the right answer 100% of the time,"" Dean said. ""These models were not near that bar."" They were prone to hallucinating and also saying offensive things.
Those were problems ChatGPT had as well. It made tons of mistakes and said racist, sexist, and other incendiary things that made headlines. Yet it took off like a rocket ship when they released it.
And it turned out that people didn't just use ChatGPT as a way to find information or get answers. Dean acknowledged that Google had underestimated how useful chatbots could be for things people wouldn't necessarily turn to a search engine for, like drafting a note or summarizing text.
Christensen warned about this very trap in The Innovator's Dilemma. Companies that keep disruptive technologies ""bottled up in their labs,"" he wrote, risk losing out to rivals willing to run with them, however imperfect they may be.
Mayer, however, questions whether releasing a chatbot first would have gone as well for Google as it did for OpenAI. ""The standards for an established company like Google in terms of what users expect, in terms of quality, how good the search is, how great the product is fundamentally different than for an upstart or a newcomer,"" Mayer says.
We don't know what would have happened if Google had released a chatbot first. Would a Google chatbot have taken off in the same way, giving Google a first-mover advantage? Or would its flaws have created big headaches for a company with an established reputation to protect?
Whatever explains Google's initial hesitation, it's now going all in. It's fought back with its own chatbot, now called Gemini, and by putting AI-generated answers directly onto its search page, even at the risk of undermining the advertising business that made it rich.
The question now is whether all this will leave Google as dominant — and as profitable — in the AI age as it was in the age of old-school web search.
Benedict Evans is a widely read technology analyst who was previously a partner at the Silicon Valley venture capital firm Andreessen Horowitz.
He says that every 10 or 15 years or so, a revolutionary ""platform shift"" shakes up the tech industry, giving newcomers a chance to challenge dominant companies. It happened with the rise of personal computers, then the internet, then smartphones. Now, he says, it's happening with AI.
""The whole table gets reset and the things that meant that they had won and you couldn't take them on suddenly don't matter anymore,"" Evans says.

                Benedict Evans speaks on stage in 2018 in Oxfordshire, England.
                
                    
                    John Phillips/Getty Images/Getty Images Europe
                    
                
hide caption

For Google, the consumer pivot to AI could mean it loses some of the competitive advantages that helped it monopolize search.
The process toward monopolization began with Google building a better search engine than rivals. Then, Evans says, its success became self-reinforcing: more users gave Google more data to improve its results, helping it attract still more users. Economists call this a ""network effect,"" a kind of positive feedback loop that helped Google cement its dominance.
Of course, Google also actively worked to catch and keep customers as well. It, for instance, paid to make its search engine the default in browsers and on devices — deals that a federal judge ruled in 2024 had illegally helped maintain its search monopoly.
Google also cemented its dominance by weaving itself into our lives. It runs the operating system on many of our phones. We use free Google services to send emails, write documents, find directions, and watch a stranger explain how to fix our washing machine. Many of us keep going back to Google search out of sheer habit or because, when we're navigating the Google digital complex, we keep getting nudged to go there.
But a platform shift could loosen Google's grip on us. Why go to Google dotcom to search for information if you're using an AI system made by a rival that can answer your question well enough? And, maybe, those rival AI systems will borrow from Google's playbook and work to intertwine themselves into our lives.
It's still early days in this rapidly transforming market. One big question, Evans says, is whether AI models will develop winner-take-all dynamics like those that helped Google dominate search. So far, it seems like the frontier labs keep catching up with each other. One possibility, he says, is that AI could become increasingly capable of improving itself — what those in the biz call ""recursive self-improvement"" — allowing whichever company gets there first to build a lead that rivals struggle to overcome. But whether the AI race will end with a dominant leader — and whether Google will be the one that emerges victorious — is still a huge question.
Mayer doesn't expect a single winner. Yes, the mammoth cost of the computing power and energy needed to develop and run frontier models creates a barrier to entry, making it hard for smaller companies to compete. But she believes there's room for several giants. ""There will be multiple big players,"" she says. ""I don't think there will be just one.""
Google has a really good shot at remaining one of the big players, but it could lose the dominance it has enjoyed in search. As Evans puts it, ""Google's share of search has really got only one way to go.""
But losing market share doesn't necessarily mean making less money, Mayer argues. She expects AI to attract more people looking for answers and draw more advertising dollars into the market. Sure, she says, Google's share could get smaller, but the pie could get bigger.
Evans points to Microsoft as an example of how a company can lose dominance after a technological disruption but still keep growing. When high-speed internet took the world by storm, it opened up new ways for rivals to compete with Microsoft in software. Companies like, yes, Google developed products like Docs and Sheets that people could use in a web browser, whether or not they were running Windows. Controlling the dominant operating system no longer gave Microsoft the same advantages in the internet era.
But the internet also gave more people a reason to buy computers — and many bought PCs that ran Windows. Microsoft sold more copies of Windows and Office, and its business kept growing because of this and other reasons.
""Well, go look at Microsoft,"" he says. ""Microsoft lost, but at the same time, the market became so much bigger"" that ""the shareholders got a lot more money.""
That said, Microsoft shareholders needed some patience. Its stock price didn't surpass its dotcom-era peak until nearly 17 years later.
Beyond just the threat to Google's monopoly in search, AI is also challenging the advertising model that made search so lucrative.
Google's historic stock plunge in late October suggested that investors are concerned about this.
On the company's earnings call, executives acknowledged that the growing use of ""AI Mode,"" its chatbot-style search feature, was contributing to slower growth of search advertising revenue because it earned less per query than traditional search. Meanwhile, Alphabet said it expects to spend a whopping $200 billion this year alone on infrastructure, including data centers, largely to support its AI push.
Watching Google's stock price plummet that day, it felt like seeing Google struggle with the innovator's dilemma in real time. Like it was willing to risk cannibalizing its core business to feed its ambitions in the AI age, accepting short-term pain in the hope of long-term gain.
Sundar Pichai, the CEO of Google and its parent company Alphabet, has made clear he believes embracing innovation is the only way forward. He was actually asked about the innovator's dilemma on the All-In podcast last year. ""I think the dilemma only exists if you treat it as a dilemma,"" Pichai said. ""All along in technology you have these massive periods of innovation and you lean into it as hard as you can. It's the only way to do it.""

                Pichai Sundararajan, known as Sundar Pichai, CEO of Google Inc. speaks during an event to introduce Google Pixel phone and other Google products on Oct. 4, 2016, in San Francisco. The Google Pixel is intended to challenge the Apple iPhone in the premium smartphone category. (Photo by Ramin Talaie/Getty Images)
                
                    
                    Ramin Talaie/Getty Images/Getty Images North America
                    
                
hide caption

Mayer is optimistic about Pichai's aggressive approach and Google's position in this new technological era. Google helped pioneer this technology, she says, and using it to answer people's questions draws on what has been its core expertise. She even questions whether there is really any dilemma for Google when it comes to using AI to give consumers better answers.
""They actually aren't doing something new by deploying it,"" Mayer says. ""They're just answering questions differently and arguably better.""
She does, however, acknowledge there is a dilemma when it comes to advertisers, who pay most of the company's bills. Google may need to fundamentally revamp its system of clickable ads it has spent decades showing alongside search results.
"" So I think that piece of it is hard,"" Mayer says. ""How do I take that advertising spend and move it to a new format that keeps my customers, my advertising customers, as happy?""
But advertisers will follow users, she argues, and AI could provide the basis for an even more lucrative business.
""I don't see why it can't be as profitable,"" she says. ""In fact, I think it probably, in the end, will be more lucrative than it has been in the past.""
Who knows what advertising will look like as the AI era advances. Maybe we'll see familiar-looking banner ads or clickable links on chatbot screens. Or maybe we'll be confiding in an AI assistant with a disembodied voice, like Scarlett Johansson in Her, sharing our most intimate thoughts, only to hear a word from our sponsor. Awkward.
Mayer sees other possibilities. Her startup, Dazzle, uses AI to analyze people's photographs. Those photos can help the system learn about you — which brands you like, what products you own, and what you might want to buy next. You can also snap a photo of something you like and ask where to buy it.
""If we can identify the product and send you there,"" she says, ""there's an opportunity for advertisements or lead generation.""
The more people share with AI systems, the more those systems learn about them, creating valuable personalized data that could be used to microtarget ads or nudge people to buy things.
For Google, the question is whether AI's new ways of connecting people to products can replace — or exceed — the profits of its existing advertising business.
In a statement to Planet Money, Google said its AI features are already leading to more searches on its platform. ""This past quarter, Search queries grew at their fastest year-over-year rate in five years,"" the company said. It says they're taking a ""deliberate approach"" to developing ad revenue in this new technological era: ""building the best user experience first and then scaling monetization.""
But the AI race is about much more than just building systems that can help people find information and make money from ads along the way. Investors are dreaming of AI models that can do things that once sounded like science fiction, including automating vast amounts of knowledge work. They're betting revolutionary applications will give rise to incredibly profitable businesses.
Evans believes the most exciting market opportunities will be found in entirely new uses for AI — some we haven't even dreamed up yet.
What will those applications be? How profitable will they become? Will they justify the mind-blowing investments Google and other companies are making in this technological revolution?
Evans says the uncertainty around fundamental questions like these helps explain stock market volatility. Investors don't know how much money these companies will ultimately earn — or how long they'll have to wait. And even if AI creates enormous wealth, he says, it's far from clear who will capture it.
One risk Evans sees is that competing AI models become so similar in their capabilities that their developers struggle to charge much for them. The most lucrative businesses could be the products and services other companies build using those models.
He points to cellphone service providers as a cautionary tale. They spent billions to build out the network infrastructure for the mobile internet, allowing people to watch videos, order taxis, and much more on their cellphones.
""All the stuff that they were talking about happened,"" Evans says, ""but it happened 10 years later.""
And when that future finally arrived, the service providers weren't the big winners from all that spending. Ouch! Yes, they owned the infrastructure, but it was the companies making smartphones and building apps and services on top of that infrastructure that captured the biggest rewards of the mobile revolution.
Evans suggests that the same risk hangs over the companies building today's AI models. ""The point is just because it's amazing and cool and exciting and it's going to change the world doesn't mean that you have value capture or pricing power."" In other words, AI could change the world without sending these companies' profits — or stock prices — soaring.
Of course, the fate that befell the cellphone service providers is just one possible scenario. Evans says the AI industry could develop like advanced chip manufacturing, where the rising cost and complexity of making chips left fewer and fewer companies able to compete. Or, he says, it could look like cloud computing, where several big companies make good money supplying the infrastructure that other businesses run on.
Google has a good shot at thriving under more than one of those scenarios. Its empire spans search, AI chatbots and assistants, YouTube, cloud computing, and even driverless cars through Waymo.
Google can make money both from supplying AI infrastructure and building products and services with AI, Evans says. On one side, Google already sells access to its own AI models and supplies computing power to other companies, including Anthropic. Google is also a major investor in Anthropic, and has a stake that could become even more valuable when that company goes public.
But if the business of building and running frontier AI models turns out to be less profitable than so many investors hope, Google could still make money using the technology in its own products. It also has the money and in-house expertise to compete for entirely new AI businesses.
The question is whether it can make enough money from all this to make its huge investments worthwhile — and make up for whatever it might lose in search.
Google helped invent the technology behind this revolution. Now it's betting a fortune that AI can deliver a whole new herd of cash cows — without killing the one that made it rich.

By Jamie Lang | 11/17/2026 10:32 am   | Be the First to Comment!

Disney closed out a brutal fiscal year for its workers with a very good quarter for its shareholders.
In its Q4 earnings report released on Tuesday morning, the company reported $2.6 billion in quarterly net income, nearly double what it earned one year prior, and said it now expects to cut roughly $3 billion from its annual operating expenses by fiscal 2029, including, you guessed it, more layoffs. It also plans to spend another $9 billion buying back its own stock next year.
Disney took in $25.9 billion in the fourth quarter, up 16% from a year earlier. Its profit before taxes rose 71% to $3.5 billion, helped by an extra week of business. For the full year, profit before taxes rose 18% to $14.2 billion. Disney’s net profit for the year fell to $9.9 billion from $12.4 billion the year before.
Disney says it will reinvest most of the $3 billion it expects to save annually by fiscal 2029 and that some of those savings will boost profits over time. The shareholder letter leaves open where Disney will put that money and how much of the savings will come from cutting jobs.
The rest, the company says, is expected to boost profits over time. For workers, the more immediate question is how much of those savings will come from eliminating jobs. As Bloomberg revealed earlier this month, Disney managers were reportedly asked to cut annual costs by 20%, with thousands of jobs expected to go.
For Disney employees, this constantly repeating refrain of huge profits, stock buybacks, and technology investments juxtaposed with job and benefit cuts must surely be getting tiresome. Disney started the year by consolidating its marketing operations in January, then it eliminated roughly 1,000 positions in April, and made another huge round of cuts over the summer that were particularly hard on Pixar, despite that company grossing well over $1 billion at the box office this year with Hoppers and Toy Story 5, both also awards season contenders.
In August, Disney announced it would restrict health coverage for employees’ spouses starting in 2027. A month later, Disney’s top lawyer, Horacio Gutierrez, warned staff that the legal and global affairs department would become “a much smaller organization.” Disney was weighing automation and outsourcing as part of the review, and Gutierrez told employees that some of them would be affected.
And speaking of automation, Disney had egg on its face after its $1 billion deal with OpenAI lasted barely three months before OpenAI pulled the plug on its Sora video generator. Disney moved on to Character.AI, a chatbot company it had sent a cease-and-desist letter over unauthorized use of its characters. Disney hired its then-CEO, Karandeep Anand, as its first companywide chief technology officer. After Character.AI filed for bankruptcy a few days later, Disney offered $20 million for some leftover scraps of the defunct outfit and reportedly budgeted $180 million to recruit its workers.
Just last week, Disney struck a multiyear deal with Adobe to bring bespoke gen AI tools into its production pipelines. Walt Disney Imagineering is already using Adobe’s Firefly Foundry to turn artists’ drawings into rendered concept art.
And let us not forget the shareholders. Won’t somebody think of the shareholders?! Disney has returned $11.6 billion in buybacks and dividends in fiscal 2026. Alongside next year’s $9 billion buyback target, the board declared a $2-per-share dividend, up 33% from what it paid this year.
Disney can tell investors exactly how much it intends to spend buying its own shares and when the next dividend checks will go out. Workers heading into another round of cuts are still waiting to find out whose jobs are on the line, or which benefits will be the next to go.

90,2026-11-17,"Kalshi halts Trump speech bets following CFTC mandate amid concerns of insider trading in US prediction markets.
Save
Share
The prediction market platform Kalshi has ended wagers on whether United States President Donald Trump would use specific words on certain dates in his speeches.
The decision, revealed on Tuesday, comes amid an order from the US Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC), the federal agency that oversees futures trading, which last week called on the company to end the bets.
Earlier this year, Kalshi’s surveillance system flagged to the agency potential insider trading concerns involving these markets. It was later discovered that a White House teleprompter operator had traded on markets related to the president’s comments.
The teleprompter operator, Gabriel Perez, received a three-year ban from the platform and was required to pay $65,000 in penalties, as well as relinquish his $107,000 in winnings to the CFTC. By August, the CFTC had begun an internal review of prediction markets, according to CNBC.
The move comes amid a new wave of scrutiny of prediction market platforms by the CFTC. That marks a shift from the stance of Chair Michael Selig, who had previously championed the emerging industry, but has now adopted a tougher tone, launching investigations into Kalshi and its competitor Polymarket, which is not regulated by the commission.
Kalshi, in particular, had been under investigation for developing new products without notifying the commission, according to a report by Politico earlier this month.
The development also comes as Donald Trump Jr, the president’s eldest son, stepped down from his role as a paid strategic adviser to the company last week.
While Kalshi has pulled markets involving mentions by the president, wagers on what other prominent figures will say remain active.
These include predictions about comments by elected US officials, including Vice President JD Vance and New York Mayor Zohran Mamdani, at the upcoming New York Times DealBook Summit, as well as remarks by executives at Microsoft and SpaceX. Other active markets concern what companies such as Nvidia and retail giant Target will say during their earnings calls.
Kalshi has also stopped listing some markets at the request of the regulatory agency, including certain sports-related markets, according to the Reuters news agency.
Neither Kalshi nor the CFTC responded to Al Jazeera’s request for comment.

In-law drama sometimes feels like the cliche fall-back plan of struggling sitcoms. After all, real people can’t be that petty or unhinged about their kid’s partners. But every once in a while, a story comes around that serves as a reminder to count your lucky stars if you actually have good in-laws.

A woman started to piece together that her mother-in-law might have an agenda when she discovered, by accident, that she was still inviting her son’s ex to family functions. When the woman decided that her MIL was no longer welcome at her home, all hell broke loose.

RDNE Stock project (not the actual photo)





Drazen Zigic (not the actual photo)





Tokuo Nobuhiro (not the actual photo)

MarcellaHarrington
Yaroslav Shuraev (not the actual photo)
Few things light up a comment section like a guest list. Inviting someone to a family dinner sounds harmless until that someone is the ex of a person sitting at the table, and the host is the one who was supposed to have everyone’s back. Plenty of readers felt the woman had every right to be upset. Plenty more thought banning her mother in law outright was a big leap, and the mother in law’s outrage only turned up the volume. Both reactions deserve a closer look, because this situation is far more common than it seems.
Start with the numbers. Cambridge psychologist Terri Apter has found that about three out of four couples deal with significant conflict involving in-laws, and she singles out the mother in law and daughter in law pairing as the trickiest of all. So if this story felt familiar, you are in very large company.
Now to the invitations themselves. A guest tells everyone who belongs, who is welcome and whose feelings were taken into account. A mother in law may sincerely believe she is being inclusive or keeping an old friendship alive, but good intentions do not cancel out the effect. The real sting here is that the couple never got a say. Clinical psychologist Aura De Los Santos explains that boundaries are simply how we let others know how they should treat us and how far they can go. When someone quietly makes that call for you, the message received is that your comfort was not part of the plan.
Then comes the ban, which commenters called either empowering or extreme. A useful distinction comes from psychologist Tracy Dalgleish, author of You, Your Husband and His Mother. She suggests that many people believe they are setting boundaries when they are really making requests. A request depends on the other person agreeing. A boundary is about what you will do when a line gets crossed. By that definition, a ban is a boundary in its purest form, though it usually lands best as a later step rather than a first one, ideally after a plain conversation where the rule was spelled out. Whether that conversation happened is something each reader can weigh for themselves.
https://kaboompics.com/ (not the actual photo)
As for the outrage, a mother in law who feels shut out will often react with anger before she reacts with reflection. That response is common and it is not proof the boundary was unfair. A fair boundary can still land badly. Dalgleish has also talked about why even confident, high performing women struggle to hold this line with a husband’s mother, and the same piece shares a handy approach, which is to pair the boundary with reassurance. Tell the family how much they matter, then explain what will work best for the couple going forward. That framing does not water the boundary down. It simply gives the other person something to hold onto besides the sting.
The husband’s role matters too. Psychologist Dr. Dara Bushman, speaking with Romper, described presenting a united front as the biggest step in setting boundaries with in laws, since a couple works as a team. A boundary delivered by one spouse can look like a personal feud, while one delivered by both reads as a family policy. When the person on the guest list is his ex and the person doing the inviting is his mother, hearing it from him can carry extra weight.
If you are wondering what a middle path might look like, a few options come to mind. A specific rule, such as checking the guest list with the couple first, targets the actual problem without closing the door on the whole relationship. A trial period with a clear way back can give everyone room to cool off. And a calm follow up conversation, once tempers settle, often does more than another round of group chat arguments.

If you’ve spent enough time scrolling through social media, there’s a good chance you’ve seen this smiling little Jack Russell Terrier before. Her expressive face has appeared in countless memes, funny videos, and photos, often accompanied by music and some rather unusual captions.

Many people know her as Aifolou or Wenomechainsama, but her real name was Maya. And behind those familiar images was a Jack Russell Terrier from Ukraine whose owner, photographer Iryna Kalamurza, never expected her beloved pet to become an internet celebrity.
Iryna recently shared Maya’s story with us at Bored Panda, revealing how their nearly 16 years together led to a photography project that would eventually make the little dog famous worldwide. Scroll down to read the full story and see how Maya’s studio photos slowly morphed into online memes and countless examples of fan art.
More info: aifolou.com | Instagram | kalamurza.com

maya_dog_jrt
Their story began in the summer of 2010, when Iryna visited a dog breeder near Kyiv, Ukraine. She had always dreamed of owning a Jack Russell Terrier, and although the breeder kept recommending a different puppy, she immediately felt drawn to Maya. “I just felt that she was my dog,” Iryna recalled.

maya_dog_jrt

maya_dog_jrt
The two quickly became inseparable. At the time, Iryna was working at a photography studio in Kyiv, specializing in portraits of people. Maya regularly accompanied her to work and soon became a familiar face to everyone visiting the studio. Around the same time, Iryna began uploading photographs to stock image websites to earn a little extra money alongside her regular photography work. Naturally, she also started taking professional pictures of Maya, whose cheerful personality and expressive face made her an excellent model.

maya_dog_jrt

maya_dog_jrt
Over the next decade, the pair created an extensive collection of photographs, experimenting with different expressions, poses, and styles. Maya’s pictures consistently became some of the most popular images in Iryna’s stock portfolio. Her photographs appeared in advertisements, magazines, and pet-related products, and some were even purchased by the pet food company Pedigree. However, their biggest success was still to come.

maya_dog_jrt

maya_dog_jrt
In the summer of 2021, Iryna began receiving unexpected messages on Instagram from complete strangers. People were telling her how much they loved her dog and how Maya’s pictures made them happy. At first, she wasn’t entirely sure what was happening. Then, one message finally explained everything: “I don’t know if you’re aware, but your dog is a superstar in Brazil!”

maya_dog_jrt

maya_dog_jrt
It turned out that someone had used Maya’s stock photographs to create a humorous video featuring the song “I Follow Rivers” by Lykke Li. The video became enormously popular among Brazilian internet users, who began calling the dog Aifolou, a playful interpretation of the song’s lyrics. Before many people even knew Maya’s real name or who had photographed her, she had already become a recognizable internet character.

maya_dog_jrt

maya_dog_jrt
Her popularity grew further when her pictures began appearing in another meme, Wenomechainsama, based on misheard lyrics from Calvin Harris’s song “Summer.” Other names and versions followed, introducing Maya’s smiling face to even more people around the world. Iryna was amazed by the creativity of Maya’s growing fan community. People began making drawings, stickers, homemade merchandise, and all kinds of other tributes inspired by her photographs. Some fans even had Maya’s face tattooed on their bodies.

maya_dog_jrt

maya_dog_jrt
Although Iryna distinguishes between these affectionate fan creations and the unauthorized commercial use of her photographs, she has always appreciated seeing how much joy Maya brings to people. She also began receiving messages from followers who told her that pictures of Maya helped them through difficult periods or simply made their days a little better. For Iryna, knowing that photographs she had taken with so much love could have such an effect on strangers was incredibly meaningful. Meanwhile, life brought some major changes for the pair. Following Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022, Iryna and Maya moved to Barcelona, Spain, where they continued their lives together.

maya_dog_jrt

maya_dog_jrt
Sadly, Maya passed away in 2026 at the age of 16, leaving behind her devastated owner and an enormous community of fans who had grown attached to her through their screens. Iryna received thousands of messages from people around the world expressing their condolences. Many had never met Maya, yet they grieved alongside her owner, sharing how much the little dog had meant to them. “I miss her very much. But at the same time, I’m incredibly grateful that so many people love her and continue sharing her story,” Iryna told us.

obsidian.gremory

preguishi
Today, Maya’s photographs, memes, and fan creations continue to circulate online. Iryna has also created a dedicated website to preserve her memory and celebrate the little dog who became famous simply by being herself. One recent message from a follower was particularly touching for Iryna. “I will tell my children about Maya,” the person wrote.

royal.panda.02

_little_gray_cat
For someone who originally just wanted to photograph her best friend, knowing that Maya will be remembered by people around the world means a great deal. And while many internet users will always recognize her as Aifolou or Wenomechainsama, behind those funny pictures was Maya, a much-loved dog who spent nearly 16 years by her owner’s side.

jujulzink

93,2026-11-17,"Bitcoin price downside sees local lows of $63,269 as Nvidia stock targets $150 as traders brace for volatility over Q3 earnings.
Bitcoin saw selling pressure after Tuesday’s Wall Street open as stock markets stayed on edge ahead of Nvidia’s third-quarter earnings and forward guidance.
Key points:
Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD dipping to $63,269 on Bitstamp, down up to 2.5% on the day.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView
US equities saw minor losses as the session began, with traders eyeing key data from Nvidia, along with cues as to how the company planned to handle the ongoing controversy surrounding its former CEO Jensen Huang.
Amid mounting pressure to reinstate Huang despite resistance from US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, commentators saw little chance of Nvidia’s board reversing its decision to dismiss him.
On Tuesday, the board announced the creation of a National Security and Export Control Oversight Committee, dedicated to “oversight of the company’s national security policies, export control compliance and global trade risk management.”
“We are committed to working constructively with government and industry partners to help establish and advance a robust framework that uses every tool at our disposal to safeguard national security while supporting continued technological innovation,” board chairman Harvey Jones commented in a press release.
Huang will also appear to testify before Congress on Dec. 1. In its latest analysis, trading resource The Kobeissi Letter put the odds of Huang returning at less than 20% in light of the latest events.
“Any indication that the probability is higher would be strongly bullish for the stock,” it wrote on X.
Kobeissi queried whether Huang’s departure could justify the $750 billion loss in market cap Nvidia had suffered, with NVDA dipping to $150.35, down over 3% on the day. On Nov. 12, it hit $148.80, its lowest since late June.

Nvidia stock one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView
Market nerves extended beyond the US ahead of a key moment for the AI sector. South Korea’s tech-heavy KOSPI Composite Index finished Tuesday’s trading session 2.6% lower at 4,968 points — its lowest daily close since Feb. 2.

KOSPI one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView
“The tape feels increasingly fragile as Nvidia’s AI premium continues to unwind and investors reassess the durability of the ecosystem built around its dominance,” CNBC quoted FedWatch Advisors founder Ben Emons as stating in a note.
CNBC and others referenced data from Bank of America’s latest monthly fund manager survey, which showed that investors slashed tech-stock exposure last month from 37% to 12% overweight tech — the largest single monthly reduction since June 2006.
BTC price action sought to break down from its narrow range between $64,000 and $66,000, in place since Nov. 15, as traders awaited Nvidia signals.
Related: Bitcoin realized loss metric beats June high as open interest eyes 2-year low
In an X update on the day, Keith Alan, cofounder of crypto trading platform Material Indicators, stipulated that the market needed to fulfil several key conditions as part of a durable rebound.
BTC/USD, he argued, should reclaim its 200-week simple moving average (SMA) at $67,977, but that this alone was not “enough.” Lower time frames must also produce a series of higher swing lows and higher swing highs, with breakouts through local resistance accompanied by high buyer volume.
“Until then, a bounce has a higher probability of being a trap than a legit recovery effort,” Alan summarized.
Previously, Cointelegraph reported on the status of the 200-week SMA as a key level to defend as support as part of a bear-market recovery.
BTC/USD one-day chart with the 200-week SMA. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView
Cointelegraph is committed to providing independent, high-quality journalism across the crypto, blockchain, AI, and fintech industries.
All news, reviews, and analyses are produced with full journalistic independence and integrity. For more details on our standards and processes, please read our Editorial Policy."
94,2026-11-17,"
There are things that we use for almost every day life that we don’t really think about their design. One of them are bottle openers. As long as they’re able to open the bottle that I want to drink or use for cooking, I’m not really particular about how they’re designed or what material the designer uses. But this new one begs users to actually think about where it came from and what the minimalist design means. 
Clink starts with a curve that its designer didn’t have to create. Hsinhung Chou’s stainless steel bottle opener is cut from standard tube stock, borrowing its three-dimensional shape from material that already exists. It’s a practical response to a less photogenic design problem: custom tooling can demand a serious investment before an independent designer has sold a single object. Chou’s solution is to work around that expense.
Designer: Hsinhung Chou






For the person opening a soda or beer, the payoff from this bottle opener is more immediate. Chou says the tube’s curvature gives Clink greater structural rigidity than a conventional flat opener while creating a contour for the thumb. That’s a sensible ambition for an object you press against your hand. A manufacturing decision becomes part of the grip, rather than remaining an invisible detail in a production spreadsheet.
I like that the starting point is so ordinary. A tube already has a radius, a wall thickness, and a shape that places limits on what can be cut from it. Those limits become useful design information. Instead of asking a factory to reproduce any imaginable shape, Chou has developed an object around the possibilities of an existing material. That feels like a more interesting exercise than styling another flat bar.
The process behind it is laser tube cutting. It is a way to cut different contours into tubes and profiles without changing cutting tools for each geometry. For Clink, the important distinction is that the starting material is already curved. The laser defines the opener within that surface, rather than creating a flat piece that needs its curve added afterward.

That relationship between process and shape is the strongest reason to pay attention here. Plenty of objects come with a manufacturing story, but I’m more interested when knowing the process helps explain the object itself. Clink’s curve has a reason to exist beyond looking distinctive. According to Chou’s description, the same geometry addresses both stiffness and thumb placement. It gives a small detail more than one job.
Still, an ergonomic intention isn’t the same as a comfortable opener. They say it has a satisfying tactile response but since I haven’t experienced it yet, I’ll just take their word for it. The useful questions would be straightforward: how the edges meet your fingers, how securely it engages a cap, and how much pressure the grip puts on your thumb. Those details would decide whether the production logic translates into a tool worth reaching for.
The stainless steel finish is another deliberate part of Chou’s approach, with the material’s surface texture included in his description of the design. My preference with a small metal tool is for that finish to support the shape rather than distract from it. An opener doesn’t need much visual explanation. Its proportions and the places where your hand makes contact should carry most of the conversation.






Clink has also moved beyond a one-off design study. Chou has already batch-produced the openers and sells them for NT$1,280, approximately $38 in its coverage. Of course there’s still shipping to consider to wherever you are so that puts this firmly in considered-purchase territory for a bottle opener. I’d want it to earn its place through use, even if the fabrication story helped get my attention.
The appeal is in recognizing that a standard piece of tubing already contains part of the answer. Chou has used its curve as a design resource, with a practical purpose attached. The remaining test is refreshingly ordinary: when a bottle needs opening, does your hand choose Clink over the other opener already in the drawer?

The company made the incident public on Monday, November 16, five days after filing a complaint with authorities.
By

French market research company Ipsos is investigating a breach that hit one of its internal information systems.
The company made the incident public on Monday, November 16, five days after filing a complaint with authorities.



According to Ipsos, the breach has not disrupted its business. “This incident had no impact on our operations or our ability to deliver for our clients,” the company said.



After detecting the incident, Ipsos launched its response process and worked to contain it, bringing in outside security experts to assist with the probe.



“The investigation is ongoing to determine the nature and scope of the incident,” Ipsos said.



The company has not said whether any data was accessed or stolen.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


“We take protection of the information entrusted to us very seriously. Should the investigation establish that information relating to clients, projects or business partners has been affected, we will contact those concerned directly,” Ipsos said.



The market research giant has not revealed when it detected the breach, which system was affected, or how the intrusion occurred. Ipsos said it could not provide further comment while the probe is underway.



No known cybercrime group appears to have taken credit for an attack on Ipsos. 



Established in France in 1975, Ipsos ranks among the biggest market research firms globally. It has nearly 20,000 employees across 90 markets and works with 5,000 clients. Its services rely on data the company collects itself, including from surveys and social media monitoring.
